A correspondence between surjective local homeomorphisms and a family of separated graphs Thanks: Both authors were partially supported by the project PID2020-113047GB-I00/AEI/10.13039/501100011033. The first-named author was also partially supported by the Spanish State Research Agency, through the Severo Ochoa and María de Maeztu Program for Centers and Units of Excellence in R & \& D (CEX2020-001084-M). The second named author was also partially supported by the ERC Starting Grant “Limits of Structures in Algebra and Combinatorics” No. 805495.
Pere Ara, Joan Claramunt

Abstract
. We present a graph-theoretic model for dynamical systems ( X , σ ) (X,\sigma) given by a surjective local homeomorphism σ \sigma on a totally disconnected compact metrizable space X X . In order to make the dynamics appear explicitly in the graph, we use two-colored Bratteli separated graphs as the graphs used to encode the information. In fact, our construction gives a bijective correspondence between such dynamical systems and a subclass of separated graphs which we call l-diagrams . This construction generalizes the well-known shifts of finite type, and leads naturally to the definition of a generalized finite shift . It turns out that any dynamical system ( X , σ ) (X,\sigma) of our interest is the inverse limit of a sequence of generalized finite shifts. We also present a detailed study of the corresponding Steinberg and C ∗ C^{*} algebras associated with the dynamical system ( X , σ ) (X,\sigma) , and we use the above approximation of ( X , σ ) (X,\sigma) to write these algebras as colimits of the associated algebras of the corresponding generalized finite shifts, which we call generalized finite shift algebras .



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
37B10, 46L55, 16S88

1.
Introduction

Dynamical systems consisting of a pair
(X,f)
, where
X
is a topological space and
f\colon X\to X
is a continuous map, have attracted a great deal of attention along the years. In particular, an important line of investigation consists of approximating a dynamical system by means of other dynamical systems defined by combinatorial data. In many occasions, invariants associated to the original dynamical system can be computed using these combinatorial approximations. In this respect, symbolic dynamics has shown to provide a very useful source of approximating systems, see
[
22
,
31
,
48
]
.
An early example occurs with the existence of a Markov partition on several classes of dynamical systems, which is proved to imply that the system under study is a factor of a corresponding shift associated to the Markov partition, see
[
48
, Proposition 6.5.8]
.

It has recently been shown in
[
39
]
that any dynamical system
(X,f)
, where
X
is a compact Hausdorff space, can be expressed as an inverse limit of one-sided shifts of finite type, and that the important property of shadowing holds for
(X,f)
if and only if the inverse system satisfies the Mittag-Leffler property. See also
[
53
]
and
[
54
]
for results concerning the approximation of zero-dimensional systems in terms of various combinatorial structures.

In a different setting, Bratteli diagrams were used in
[
18
]
to classify approximately finite-dimensional
C^{*}
-algebras. Herman, Putnam and Skau
[
40
]
showed that there is a bijective correspondence (modulo suitable equivalence relations) between essentially minimal homeomorphisms
f
on the Cantor set
X
and a class of
ordered
Bratteli diagrams. These results were later used to obtain classification theorems for the corresponding crossed product
C^{*}
-algebras
C^{*}(X)\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}
associated to the action of
{\mathbb{Z}}
on
X
induced by the homeomorphism
f
, see for instance
[
35
]
. This kind of combinatorial structure has been studied in more generality, for some dynamical systems not necessarily essentially minimal, see for example
[
17
]
. In addition, the above correspondences have been recently upgraded to suitable category equivalences by Amini, Elliott and Golestani, see
[
2
]
and
[
3
]
.

In this paper we develop a new approximation method for any dynamical system
(X,\sigma)
, where
\sigma
is a surjective local homeomorphism and
X
is a totally disconnected compact metrizable space. This is a very large class of dynamical systems, including all homeomorphisms on totally disconnected compact metrizable spaces, in particular all minimal Cantor systems
[
51
]
, all one-sided shifts of finite type, all two-sided shifts, covering maps of compact totally disconnected metrizable spaces (see
[
29
]
,
[
33
]
and Remark
3.17
), the one-sided shifts associated to topological graphs
E=(E^{0},E^{1},r,s)
, where
E^{0}
and
E^{1}
are compact totally disconnected metrizable spaces and
s
is surjective (see
[
42
]
,
[
43
]
,
[
44
]
,
[
45
]
), one-sided shifts on infinite path spaces of finite ultragraphs with no sinks
[
37
]
, and the covers of one-sided subshifts
[
20
]
.

We show that our approximation is well-behaved with respect to the associated
C^{*}
-algebras. Specifically, we prove that the groupoid
C^{*}
-algebra
C^{*}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma))
of the Deaconu-Renault groupoid
{\pazocal G}(X,\sigma)
associated to
(X,\sigma)
is naturally isomorphic to the colimit of the groupoid
C^{*}
-algebras of the approximating systems, see Theorem
1.4
below. This groupoid
C^{*}
-algebra generalizes the crossed product construction mentioned above. A new basic building block emerges from our approach, generalizing both one-sided and two-sided shifts of finite type, which we have termed a
generalized shift of finite type
. The main combinatorial tool employed is the theory of separated graphs. A
separated graph

(E,C)
consists of a graph
E=(E^{0},E^{1},r,s)
together with a prescribed partition
C_{v}
of the set of edges arriving at
v
, for each vertex
v\in E^{0}
. In our work, we use
separated Bratteli diagrams
, which are Bratteli diagrams which are also separated graphs, so that the edges on every layer of the diagram are given a specific separation.

It is worth to mention that for a large class of dynamical systems, including the ones considered in the present paper, it has been shown in
[
16
]
that one can characterize conjugacy of the dynamical systems in terms of the associated
C^{*}
-algebras and also in terms of the associated Deaconu-Renault groupoids. See also
[
19
]
,
[
21
]
,
[
24
]
and
[
38
]
for other studies on the interconnections between local homeomorphisms, groupoids and
C^{*}
-algebras.

Let
LHomeo
be the family of dynamical systems
(X,\sigma)
consisting of a totally disconnected compact metrizable space
X
, together with a surjective local homeomorphism
\sigma:X\rightarrow X
.
In the spirit of the work done in
[
40
]
, we provide a bijective correspondence (modulo suitable equivalence relations) between
LHomeo
and a class of separated Bratteli diagrams, which we call
l
-diagrams
, see Definition
3.2
.

Let
E
be a finite graph having neither sinks nor sources. It is well-known that out of it one can construct a dynamical system
(X_{E},\sigma_{E})\in\textsf{LHomeo}
as follows. The space
X_{E}
is taken to be the set of one-sided infinite paths in the graph, endowed with the topology generated by the cylinder sets, and then
\sigma_{E}
is defined as the one-sided shift. These are the well-known one-sided shifts of finite type. If we consider the space of bi-infinite paths instead of
X_{E}
, we obtain the definition of a two-sided shift of finite type, which is a homeomorphism. These are basic objects in symbolic dynamics.

From an arbitrary directed graph
E
one can construct two important
{\mathbb{C}}
-algebras in a universal way. The first one is a purely algebraic construction, called the
Leavitt path algebra
of the directed graph
E
, denoted by
L_{{\mathbb{C}}}(E)
. Leavitt path algebras were independently introduced by Abrams and Aranda Pino
[
1
]
and by the first-named author, Moreno and Pardo
[
15
]
. The second construction, which chronologically precedes the first, also contains analytic information and is called the
graph
C^{*}
-algebra
of the directed graph
E
, denoted by
C^{*}(E)
. Graph
C^{*}
-algebras were first studied in detail in the papers
[
47
]
and
[
46
]
. It turns out that
C^{*}(E)
is the enveloping
C^{*}
-algebra of
L_{{\mathbb{C}}}(E)
for each directed graph
E
, in the sense of Definition
2.4
.

The connection with the theory of topological groupoids was already explored in
[
47
]
, where it is shown that the graph
C^{*}
-algebra of a locally finite graph is isomorphic to the
C^{*}
-algebra of a suitably defined
graph groupoid

{\pazocal G}_{E}
associated to
E
. This was later generalized to arbitrary graphs. When the graph is finite and has neither sinks nor sources, the graph groupoid
{\pazocal G}_{E}
turns out to be the Deaconu-Renault groupoid of the one-sided shift
(X_{E},\sigma_{E})
, and thus we obtain the isomorphism
C^{*}(E)\cong C^{*}({\pazocal G}(X_{E},\sigma_{E}))
. This connection was extended to the purely algebraic situation in
[
56
]
and
[
26
]
through the consideration of the
Steinberg algebra

A_{{\mathbb{C}}}({\pazocal G})
of an ample étale groupoid
{\pazocal G}
. Concretely, it is shown in
[
26
, Proposition 4.3]
that there is a natural
*
-algebra isomorphism
L_{{\mathbb{C}}}(E)\cong A_{{\mathbb{C}}}({\pazocal G}_{E})
for each directed graph
E
. There are multiple interesting connections between these groupoid algebras and symbolic dynamics, see for instance the recent papers
[
16
]
and
[
23
]
.

The first-named author and Goodearl introduced in
[
12
]
and
[
13
]
the Leavitt path algebra
L_{{\mathbb{C}}}(E,C)
and the graph
C^{*}
-algebras
C^{*}(E,C)
associated to a separated graph
(E,C)
. These are defined in a similar way to the Leavitt path algebras and graph
C^{*}
-algebras of ordinary directed graphs, but taking into account the partitions
\{C_{v}\mid v\in E^{0}\}
in the defining relations of such algebras (see Definitions
2.1
and
2.2
for the precise definitions). Thenceforth there has been a considerable amount of work towards the theory of separated graph algebras
[
4
,
5
,
9
,
11
,
14
,
36
,
49
,
50
]
, not only because they provide a whole rich class of examples of algebras not being realizable as graph algebras, but because they proved to be useful in solving, either in the positive or in the negative, important conjectures (see e.g.
[
6
,
7
,
8
,
10
]
).

The relation between dynamical systems and separated graphs was first demonstrated in a paper by the first-named author and Exel
[
10
]
. The authors attach to each finite bipartite separated graph
(E,C)
a partial dynamical system
(\Omega(E,C),{\mathbb{F}},\theta)
, where
{\mathbb{F}}={\mathbb{F}}(E^{1})
is the free group on the set of edges, in such a way that the algebraic crossed product
C_{{\mathbb{C}}}(\Omega(E,C))\rtimes_{\theta^{*}}^{\text{alg}}{\mathbb{F}}
is canonically isomorphic to a well-determined quotient
L_{{\mathbb{C}}}(E,C)/J
of
L_{{\mathbb{C}}}(E,C)
. They also found similar results in the
C^{*}
-algebraic setting. As an application of these results, the authors were able to construct non-paradoxical topological actions of discrete groups on Cantor spaces which do not admit an invariant finitely additive measure, thus providing counterexamples to the topological version of Tarski’s alternative, see
[
10
, Section 7]
for details. In connection with this work, it was shown in
[
14
]
that the study of the above mentioned dynamical systems associated to separated graphs is equivalent to the study of
convex subshifts
, a notion coined by the first-named author and Lolk in
[
14
]
, introduced as a far-reaching unification proposal for the well-known one-sided and two-sided subshifts.

The first main result of the paper is the following:

Theorem 1.1

(Theorem
3.14
)
.

Out of a dynamical system
(X,\sigma)\in\emph{\textsf{LHomeo}}
, one can construct an
l
-diagram
(F,D)
. Conversely, out of an
l
-diagram
(F,D)
, one can construct a dynamical system
(X,\sigma)\in\emph{\textsf{LHomeo}}
.

Moreover, these correspondences are inverse of each other, so we obtain, modulo appropriate equivalence relations, a bijective correspondence between
LHomeo
and
LDiag
, the class of
l
-diagrams.

In particular, if we restrict our attention to homeomorphisms, the corresponding diagrams have a simpler form. We call such separated Bratteli diagrams
h
-diagrams
(see Definition
4.1
) and we denote by
Homeo
the subclass of
LHomeo
consisting of those dynamical systems
(X,\sigma)
such that
\sigma
is a homeomorphism.

Theorem 1.2

(Theorem
4.8
)
.

The restriction of the above correspondences gives a bijective correspondence between the subclass of homeomorphisms
Homeo
and the subclass
HDiag
of
h
-diagrams.

It follows from Theorem
1.2
and
[
40
, Theorem 4.7]
that there is a bijective map between equivalence classes of essentially simple ordered Bratteli diagrams and a subset of equivalence classes of
h
-diagrams. It would be interesting to determine explicitly the range of this bijective map. Moreover, one can try to build a
category
of
h
-diagrams, in the spirit of
[
3
]
, and then extend the above bijection to a suitable equivalence of categories. Another line of future research is extending the techniques developed in the present paper to cover the case of partial (local) homeomorphisms, and relate this extension with the work done by Giordano, Gonçalves and Starling in
[
34
]
.

Let
(E,C)
be a finite bipartite separated graph satisfying the conditions needed to be the first layer of an
l
-diagram. These conditions are explicitly stated in Definition
5.6
, where these separated graphs are named
generalized finite shift graphs
. By applying a process called the
canonical resolution
of the bipartite separated graph we obtain an
l
-diagram
(F,D)
(Proposition
5.11
) which, by an application of Theorem
1.1
, gives rise to a dynamical system
(X,\sigma)\in\textsf{LHomeo}
. Those dynamical systems arising by applying this construction are named
generalized finite shifts
, since its class includes both one-sided and two-sided finite shifts. Our next main result states that any dynamical system
(X,\sigma)\in\textsf{LHomeo}
can be approximated, in a sense made precise below, by generalized finite shifts. This is done by working with an explicit realization of the generalized finite shifts, which is based on the work done in
[
14
]
.

Theorem 1.3

(Theorem
5.26
)
.

Given
(X,\sigma)\in\emph{\textsf{LHomeo}}
, there exists an inverse system
\{(X_{n},\sigma_{n}),\psi_{n,m}\}
in
LHomeo
consisting of generalized finite shift graphs such that

(X,\sigma)\cong\varprojlim(X_{n},\sigma_{n}).

Let
(X,\sigma)\in\textsf{LHomeo}
and
(F,D)
be the corresponding
l
-diagram. In the last section of the paper we apply the results obtained in order to study the relationship between the Steinberg algebra and the
C^{*}
-algebra of the Deaconu-Renault groupoid associated with
(X,\sigma)
and a certain corner of an inductive limit of Leavitt path algebras and graph
C^{*}
-algebras corresponding to the finite levels of
(F,D)
. This study culminates in Theorems
6.16
and
6.17
, where it is shown that the aforementioned algebras are isomorphic.

We close the paper by further using Theorem
1.3
and various other tools to obtain a similar approximation result at the level of the Steinberg algebras and the groupoid
C^{*}
-algebras. Here
A_{K}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma))
denotes the Steinberg algebra of
{\pazocal G}(X,\sigma)
, the Deaconu-Renault groupoid associated with
(X,\sigma)
, with coefficients in an arbitrary field with involution
(K,*)
.

Theorem 1.4

(Theorem
6.24
)
.

Let
(X,\sigma)\in\emph{\textsf{LHomeo}}
and write it as an inverse limit of a sequence of generalized finite shifts
\{(X_{n},\sigma_{n})\}_{n}
as in Theorem
1.3
. Then

A_{K}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma))\cong\varinjlim A_{K}({\pazocal G}(X_{n},\sigma_{n})).

At the level of
C^{*}
-algebras, we also get

C^{*}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma))\cong\varinjlim C^{*}({\pazocal G}(X_{n},\sigma_{n})).

The results of this paper, in conjunction with the results in
[
10
]
and
[
11
]
, will be used in forthcoming work by the authors, to study both the stable and the non-stable
K
-theory of the algebras attached to the dynamical systems
(X,\sigma)\in\textsf{LHomeo}
, as well as the type semigroup of
(X,\sigma)
.

The paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
we provide the reader some basic concepts such as the definition of a separated graph, and its associated Leavitt path algebra and
C^{*}
-algebra. We also recall the construction of the Deaconu-Renault groupoid associated with a dynamical system
(X,\sigma)
formed by a surjective local homeomorphism
\sigma
on a totally disconnected compact metrizable space
X
, and the construction of its groupoid
C^{*}
-algebra, and its Steinberg algebra over an arbitrary field with involution. We present in Theorem
3.14
the bijective correspondence between surjective local homeomorphisms on totally disconnected compact metrizable spaces and a certain subfamily of the family of separated graphs, called
l
-diagrams, in Section
3
. Section
4
is then devoted to characterize the class of
l
-diagrams which arises from the class of dynamical systems given by homeomorphisms on totally disconnected compact metrizable spaces, called
h
-diagrams. In Section
5
we show how shifts of finite type fit in our theory, and we present a generalization of them, which we call
generalized finite shifts
. We provide yet another different point of view of such dynamical systems using the work from
[
14
]
, culminating in Theorem
5.26
which shows that any dynamical system of our kind is an inverse limit of a sequence of generalized finite shits. We use this ‘approximation theorem’ in order to give similar characterizations, in terms of colimits, of the Steinberg algebra (respectively,
C^{*}
-algebra) of the Deaconu-Renault groupoid associated with the dynamical system in terms of the sequence of Steinberg algebras (respectively,
C^{*}
-algebras) of the Deaconu-Renault groupoids associated with the generalized finite shifts (Theorem
6.24
). This is done in Section
6
.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section we provide background definitions and concepts needed throughout the paper.

2.1.
Separated graphs

We follow
[
10
]
. A
separated graph
is a pair
(E,C)
where
E=(E^{0},E^{1},r,s)
is a directed graph (
E^{0}
is the set of vertices,
E^{1}
the set of edges, and
r,s\colon E^{1}\to E^{0}
are the range and source maps, respectively), and
C=\bigsqcup_{v\in E^{0}}C_{v}
, where each
C_{v}
is a partition of the set of edges
r^{-1}(v)
into pairwise disjoint, non-empty subsets, for each vertex
v\in E^{0}
. We set
C_{v}=\emptyset
in case
r^{-1}(v)=\emptyset
. We say that
(E,C)
is
finitely separated
if all the sets in
C
are finite, that is, if
|X|<\infty
for all
X\in C
.

A
bipartite separated graph
is a separated graph
(E,C)
where we can write
E^{0}=E^{0,0}\sqcup E^{0,1}
in such a way that
s(E^{1})=E^{0,1}
and
r(E^{1})=E^{0,0}
.

We will follow the conventions from
[
10
]
and
[
14
]
concerning sources and ranges of edges of a graph, and the relations (E), (SCK1) and (SCK2) below. In particular, a non-trivial path in the graph
E
will be a sequence
e_{1}e_{2}\cdots e_{n}
of edges in
E
such that
s(e_{i})=r(e_{i+1})
for
i=1,\dots,n-1
. (A trivial path consists of just one vertex
v
in
E
.) We warn the reader that different conventions are used in various other sources.

We can now define our algebras of interest, namely the Leavitt path algebra and the
C^{*}
-algebra of a separated graph
(E,C)
.

Definition 2.1
.

The
Leavitt path algebra
of the separated graph
(E,C)
with coefficients in a field
K
with involution
*
, denoted by
L_{K}(E,C)
, is the
*
-algebra with generators
E^{0}\sqcup E^{1}\sqcup(E^{1})^{*}
together with the relations

(V)

vw=\delta_{v,w}v
, and
v=v^{*}
for
v,w\in E^{0}
,

(E)

r(e)e=es(e)=e
for
e\in E^{1}
,

(SCK1)

e^{*}f=\delta_{e,f}s(e)
for
e,f\in Y
, for all
Y\in C
,

(SCK2)

v=\sum_{e\in Y}ee^{*}
for every
Y\in C_{v}
finite,
v\in E^{0}
.

Definition 2.2
.

The
C^{*}
-algebra
of the separated graph
(E,C)
, denoted by
C^{*}(E,C)
, is the universal
C^{*}
-algebra with generators
E^{0}\sqcup E^{1}\sqcup(E^{1})^{*}
together with the relations (V),(E),(SCK1) and (SCK2) from Definition
2.1
. In other words,
C^{*}(E,C)
is the enveloping
C^{*}
-algebra of
L_{{\mathbb{C}}}(E,C)
.

2.2.
Groupoids and associated algebras

Given a groupoid
{\pazocal G}
, we will write
{\pazocal G}^{(2)}
for the set of pairs of composable elements, and by
{\pazocal G}^{(0)}
for its set of units. The range and source maps will be denoted by
r:{\pazocal G}\rightarrow{\pazocal G}^{(0)}
and
s:{\pazocal G}\rightarrow{\pazocal G}^{(0)}
, respectively.

In this paper, a
topological groupoid
will be a groupoid
{\pazocal G}
possessing a locally compact Hausdorff topology, in which the range and source maps
r,s
, the inverse map and the multiplication map are all continuous maps. We refer to
[
52
,
55
,
56
,
26
]
for general background on topological groupoids and their associated
C^{*}
-algebras.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
X
and
Y
be topological spaces. A
local homeomorphism
from
X
to
Y
is a continuous map
h\colon X\to Y
such that every
x\in X
has an open neighborhood
U
such that
h(U)
is open in
Y
and
h|_{U}
is a homeomorphism from
U
onto its image
h(U)
. Whenever
X=Y
we say that
h
is a local homeomorphism on
X
.

A topological groupoid
{\pazocal G}
is
étale
if the range map
r\colon{\pazocal G}\to{\pazocal G}^{(0)}
is a local homeomorphism. Then the source map is also a local homeomorphism and
{\pazocal G}^{(0)}
is an open subset of
{\pazocal G}
(see
[
55
, Lemma 8.4.2]
).

An open subset
{\pazocal W}\subseteq{\pazocal G}
is called an
open bisection
if both
r({\pazocal W}),s({\pazocal W})
are open subsets of
{\pazocal G}^{(0)}
, and
r|_{{\pazocal W}},s|_{{\pazocal W}}
are homeomorphisms onto their respective images. Note that a topological groupoid is étale if and only if its topology has a basis of open bisections.

A topological groupoid
{\pazocal G}
is called
ample
if its topology admits a basis consisting of compact open bisections. Clearly all ample groupoids are étale.

From now on
{\pazocal G}
will denote a second countable, Hausdorff and ample groupoid, thus admitting a countable basis for the topology consisting of compact open bisections.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
(K,*)
be a field endowed with an involution
*
. The
Steinberg algebra

A_{K}({\pazocal G})
of
{\pazocal G}
is the
*
-algebra of all the locally constant functions
f\colon{\pazocal G}\to K
with compact support. The multiplication is just the convolution product

(f\ast g)(\gamma)=\sum_{s(\alpha)=s(\gamma)}f(\gamma\alpha^{-1})g(\alpha)

for
f,g\in A_{K}({\pazocal G}),\gamma\in{\pazocal G}
, and the involution is given by the rule

f^{*}(\gamma)=f(\gamma^{-1})^{*}

for
f\in A_{K}({\pazocal G}),\gamma\in{\pazocal G}
.

The
full groupoid
C^{*}
-algebra
of
{\pazocal G}
, denoted by
C^{*}({\pazocal G})
, is the enveloping
C^{*}
-algebra of
A_{{\mathbb{C}}}({\pazocal G})
, in the sense that there is an injective
*
-homomorphism
\iota\colon A_{{\mathbb{C}}}({\pazocal G})\to C^{*}({\pazocal G})
such that any
*
-homomorphism from
A_{{\mathbb{C}}}({\pazocal G})
to a
C^{*}
-algebra
B
uniquely extends to a
*
-homomorphism from
C^{*}({\pazocal G})
to
B
. For second countable, ample Hausdorff groupoids, this definition agrees with the usual definition of the full groupoid
C^{*}
-algebra given by Renault in
[
52
]
, by
[
55
, Lemma 9.2.4]
and
[
27
, Theorem 7.1(1)]
.

Every element of
A_{K}({\pazocal G})
can be written as a finite linear combination of characteristic functions
\mathds{1}_{U_{i}}
, where
\{U_{i}\}
are mutually disjoint compact open bisections. If
U
and
V
are two compact open bisections in
{\pazocal G}
, then we have

\mathds{1}_{U}\cdot\mathds{1}_{V}=\mathds{1}_{UV}

in
A_{K}({\pazocal G})
, where

UV:=\{uv\in{\pazocal G}\mid u\in U,v\in V,s(u)=r(v)\}

is again a compact open bisection.

2.3.
Dynamical systems

We will concentrate on dynamical systems
(X,\sigma)
formed by a totally disconnected compact metrizable space
X
together with a surjective local homeomorphism
\sigma\colon X\to X
(see Definition
2.3
).

Definition 2.5
.

By a
partition
of
X
we mean a finite family
{\pazocal P}
consisting on non-empty, pairwise disjoint clopen subsets of
X
, such that
X=\bigsqcup_{Z\in{\pazocal P}}Z
.

Given two partitions
{\pazocal P}_{1},{\pazocal P}_{2}
of
X
, we say that
{\pazocal P}_{2}
is
finer
than
{\pazocal P}_{1}
, written as
{\pazocal P}_{1}\precsim{\pazocal P}_{2}
, if every element
Z\in{\pazocal P}_{2}
is contained in a (necessarily unique) element
Z^{\prime}\in{\pazocal P}_{1}
, that is
Z\subseteq Z^{\prime}
. Given two partitions
{\pazocal P}_{1},{\pazocal P}_{2}
of
X
, there is a canonical way of constructing a new partition which refines both
{\pazocal P}_{1},{\pazocal P}_{2}
; this new partition is given by the
wedge
of
{\pazocal P}_{1}
and
{\pazocal P}_{2}
, and is defined by

{\pazocal P}_{1}\vee{\pazocal P}_{2}:=\{Z_{1}\cap Z_{2}\mid Z_{1}\in{\pazocal P}_{1},Z_{2}\in{\pazocal P}_{2}\text{ with }Z_{1}\cap Z_{2}\neq\emptyset\}.

The
diameter
of an element
Z\in{\pazocal P}
, being
{\pazocal P}
a partition of
X
, is defined by

\text{diam}(Z):=\sup\{d(x,y)\mid x,y\in Z\},

where
d
is the distance witnessing the metrizability of
X
. The
diameter
of the partition
{\pazocal P}
is thus

\text{diam}({\pazocal P}):=\max\{\text{diam}(Z)\mid Z\in{\pazocal P}\}.

We now recall the definition of the
Deaconu-Renault groupoid
associated with the dynamical system
(X,\sigma)
. Define

{\pazocal G}(X,\sigma):=\bigcup_{m,n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}\{(x,m-n,y)\mid x,y\in X,\sigma^{m}(x)=\sigma^{n}(y)\}\subseteq X\times{\mathbb{Z}}\times X.

This set is given the structure of a groupoid via the following. The set of composable elements will be

{\pazocal G}(X,\sigma)^{(2)}=\{((x,p,y),(z,q,t))\mid y=z\}\subseteq{\pazocal G}(X,\sigma)^{2}.

The multiplication is then given by
(x,p,y)\cdot(y,q,z)=(x,p+q,z)
, and the inverse of an element is simply
(x,p,y)^{-1}=(y,-p,x)
. Thus the unit space of this groupoid is

{\pazocal G}(X,\sigma)^{(0)}=\{(x,0,x)\mid x\in X\}\simeq X.

The range and source maps
r,s:{\pazocal G}(X,\sigma)\rightarrow X
are given, respectively, by
r(x,p,y)=x
and
s(x,p,y)=y
.

This groupoid becomes a topological Hausdorff groupoid in the topology generated by the basis of cylinder sets
{\pazocal B}=\{Z(U,m,n,V)\mid U,V\subseteq X\text{ open, and }m,n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}\}
, where each cylinder
Z(U,m,n,V)
is defined by

Z(U,m,n,V):=\{(x,m-n,y)\mid x\in U,y\in V,\sigma^{m}(x)=\sigma^{n}(y)\}.

In fact,
{\pazocal G}(X,\sigma)
becomes an étale groupoid. More specifically, by fixing
m,n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
and letting
U,V\subseteq X
be open subsets such that both
\sigma^{m}|_{U},\sigma^{n}|_{V}
are homeomorphisms onto their images, then
Z=Z(U,m,n,V)
is such that
r|_{Z},s|_{Z}
are homeomorphisms onto open subsets of
{\pazocal G}(X,\sigma)
. In particular, they are also local homeomorphisms onto open subsets of
{\pazocal G}(X,\sigma)^{(0)}=X
, and so
Z
is in fact an open bisection.

Moreover, since we can take
U,V
to be clopen subsets of
X
(as
X
is totally disconnected), we can take
Z(U,m,n,V)
to be also compact. As a consequence,
{\pazocal G}(X,\sigma)
is an ample, Hausdorff groupoid, with a basis of open compact bisections consisting of the cylinder sets
Z(U,m,n,V)
such that
m,n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
and
U,V\subseteq X
are clopen with the property that
\sigma^{m}|_{U},\sigma^{n}|_{V}
are homeomorphisms onto their images.

3.
The correspondence between local homeomorphisms and
l
-diagrams

In this section we establish a bijective correspondence between a specific family of separated graphs and surjective local homeomorphisms of a second countable totally disconnected compact space
X
.

We first describe the type of separated graphs we consider. It is a subclass of the class of all separated Bratteli diagrams considered in
[
14
]
. We recall the definition of these graphs.

Definition 3.1
.

A
separated (or colored) Bratteli diagram
is an infinite separated graph
(F,D)
with the following properties:

(1)

The vertex set
F^{0}
is the union of finite, non-empty, pairwise disjoint sets
F^{0,j}
,
j\geq 0
.

(2)

The edge set
F^{1}
is the union of finite, non-empty, pairwise disjoint sets
F^{1,j}
,
j\geq 0
.

(3)

The range and source maps satisfy
r(F^{1,j})=F^{0,j}
and
s(F^{1,j})=F^{0,j+1}
for all
j\geq 0
, respectively.

Note that a Bratteli diagram is just a separated Bratteli diagram with the trivial separation.

Now we can define our class of graphs.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
(F,D)
be a separated Bratteli diagram. We say that
(F,D)
is an
l
-diagram
if the following hold:

(1)

(Separations for the even layers) For each
j\geq 0
and
v\in F^{0,2j}
, the separation
D_{v}
is given by

D_{v}=\{B_{v},R_{v}\}

for some (non-empty) subsets
B_{v},R_{v}\subseteq r^{-1}(v)
. The elements of
B_{v}
will be called
blue edges
, and the elements of
R_{v}

red edges
.

(2)

(Separations for the odd layers) For each
j\geq 1
and
v\in F^{0,2j-1}
, the separation
D_{v}
is given by

D_{v}=\{B_{v}\}\cup\{R(f)\mid f\in R_{r(f)},s(f)=v\}

for some (non-empty) subsets
B_{v},R(f)\subseteq r^{-1}(v)
. The elements of
B_{v}
will be called
blue edges
. We write
R_{v}=\bigcup_{f\in s^{-1}(v)\cap R_{r(f)}}R(f)
, and call the elements of
R_{v}
generically
red edges
.

(3)

(Vertices at even layers) For
j\geq 1
, we have

F^{0,2j}=\bigsqcup_{v\in F^{0,2j-1}}s(B_{v})=\bigsqcup_{v\in F^{0,2j-1}}s(R_{v}).

Moreover, for any
v\in F^{0,2j-1}
, we have
s(e)\neq s(e^{\prime})
for all distinct edges
e,e^{\prime}\in B_{v}
, and similarly
s(f)\neq s(f^{\prime})
for all distinct edges
f,f^{\prime}\in R_{v}
.

(4)

(Vertices at the first odd layer) We have

(1)\text{ }F^{0,1}=\bigsqcup_{v\in F^{0,0}}s(B_{v}),\quad\text{ and also }\quad(2)\text{ }F^{0,1}=\bigcup_{v\in F^{0,0}}s(R_{v}).

Moreover,
s(e)\neq s(e^{\prime})
for all distinct edges
e,e^{\prime}
with
e\in B_{v_{1}}
,
e^{\prime}\in B_{v_{2}}
, for any
v_{1},v_{2}\in F^{0,2j}
with
j\geq 0
.

(5)

(Compatibility at even layers) For each
j\geq 0
and
v\in F^{0,2j}
, we have

\bigsqcup_{e\in B_{v}}s(B_{s(e)})=\bigsqcup_{f\in R_{v}}s(R(f)).

(6)

(Rombs at odd layers, red edges) For each
j\geq 1
, each
v\in F^{0,2j-1}
and each pair
(f_{0},f_{1})
of red edges such that
r(f_{0})=v
and
s(f_{0})=r(f_{1})
, there exists a (unique) pair
(e_{0},e_{1})
of blue edges such that
r(e_{0})=r(f_{0})=v
,
s(e_{0})=r(e_{1})
and
s(e_{1})=s(f_{1})
.

(7)

(Rombs at odd layers, blue edges) For each
j\geq 1
, each
v\in F^{0,2j-1}
, each red edge
g\in R_{r(g)}
such that
s(g)=v
, and each pair
(e_{0},e_{1})
of blue edges such that
r(e_{0})=v
and
s(e_{0})=r(e_{1})
, there exists a unique pair
(f_{0},f_{1})
of red edges such that
f_{0}\in R(g)
,
r(f_{0})=r(e_{0})=v
,
s(f_{0})=r(f_{1})
and
s(f_{1})=s(e_{1})
.

The family of
l
-diagrams will be denoted by
LDiag
.

From the very definition of an
l
-diagram we can infer two properties: the first one is that the decompositions given in condition (d) above can be extended to any odd layer; the second one is that one can automatically construct rombs at even layers, using condition (e) above together with the first property already mentioned. These are proven in the next proposition.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
(F,D)
be an
l
-diagram.

(1)

(Vertices at odd layers)
For
j\geq 1
, we have

F^{0,2j-1}=\bigsqcup_{v\in F^{0,2j-2}}s(B_{v})=\bigcup_{v\in F^{0,2j-2}}s(R_{v}).

(2)

(Rombs at even layers)
Let
j\geq 0
and
v\in F^{0,2j}
. Given a pair
(e_{0},e_{1})
of blue edges such that
r(e_{0})=v
and
r(e_{1})=s(e_{0})
, there is a unique pair
(f_{0},f_{1})
of red edges such that
r(f_{0})=v
,
r(f_{1})=s(f_{0})
,
s(f_{1})=s(e_{1})
and
f_{1}\in R(f_{0})
.

Conversely, given a pair
(f_{0},f_{1})
of red edges such that
r(f_{0})=v
and
f_{1}\in R(f_{0})
, there is a unique pair
(e_{0},e_{1})
of blue edges such that
r(e_{0})=v
,
r(e_{1})=s(e_{0})
and
s(e_{1})=s(f_{1})
.

e_{1}
e_{0}
f_{1}
f_{0}
v
F^{0,2j}
F^{0,2j+1}
F^{0,2j+2}

Proof.

(1)

We can prove this by induction. The case
j=1
holds by definition, so assume the statement is true for all indices up to some
j-1\geq 1
and let us prove it for
j
. Given a vertex
v\in F^{0,2j-1}
, being
(F,D)
a separated Bratteli diagram implies that there exists some edge
e\in F^{1,2j-2}
with source
v
, which can be either blue or red.

In case
e=e_{1}\in B_{r(e_{1})}
is a blue edge, then condition (c) above applied to
F^{0,2j-2}
tells us that there is another blue edge
e_{0}\in B_{r(e_{0})}
such that
s(e_{0})=r(e_{1})
. Moreover
r(e_{0})\in F^{0,2j-3}
, thus by the induction hypothesis there is some red edge
g\in R_{r(g)}
such that
s(g)=r(e_{0})
. Now we can apply condition (g) to get a unique pair
(f_{0},f_{1})
of red edges such that
f_{0}\in R(g)
,
r(f_{0})=r(e_{0})
,
s(f_{0})=r(f_{1})
and
s(f_{1})=s(e_{1})=v
. This says precisely that
v
is the source of some red edge from
F^{1,2j-2}
.

In case
e=f_{1}\in R_{r(f_{1})}
is a red edge, then condition (c) again tells us that there is another red edge
f_{0}\in R_{r(f_{0})}
such that
s(f_{0})=r(f_{1})
. We apply condition (f) to get a unique pair
(e_{0},e_{1})
of blue edges such that
r(e_{0})=r(f_{0})
,
s(e_{0})=r(e_{1})
and
s(e_{1})=s(f_{1})=v
. This says precisely that
v
is the source of some blue edge from
F^{1,2j-2}
.

This proves that

F^{0,2j-1}=\bigcup_{v\in F^{0,2j-2}}s(B_{v})=\bigcup_{v\in F^{0,2j-2}}s(R_{v}).

The disjointness of the first union follows from the last part of property (d).

(2)

Let
(e_{0},e_{1})
be a pair of blue edges such that
r(e_{0})=v
and
r(e_{1})=s(e_{0})
. By condition (e) we have

s(e_{1})\in\bigsqcup_{e\in B_{v}}s(B_{s(e)})=\bigsqcup_{f\in R_{v}}s(R(f)),

so there exists a pair of red edges
(f_{0},f_{1})
such that
s(e_{1})=s(f_{1})
, with
f_{1}\in R(f_{0})
and
f_{0}\in R_{v}
(so that
r(f_{1})=s(f_{0})
and
r(f_{0})=v
). The pair
(f_{0},f_{1})
satisfies the required properties. To prove uniqueness, assume that there exists another pair
(f^{\prime}_{0},f^{\prime}_{1})
of red edges such that
r(f^{\prime}_{0})=v
,
s(f^{\prime}_{0})=r(f^{\prime}_{1})
,
s(f^{\prime}_{1})=s(e_{1})=s(f_{1})
and
f_{1}^{\prime}\in R(f_{0}^{\prime})
. Condition (c) ensures that
f^{\prime}_{1}=f_{1}
; in particular
s(f^{\prime}_{0})=r(f^{\prime}_{1})=r(f_{1})=s(f_{0})
. Since
f_{1}^{\prime}=f_{1}\in R(f_{0})\cap R(f_{0}^{\prime})
, we conclude that
f_{0}=f_{0}^{\prime}
. That is, the pair
(f_{0},f_{1})
is unique.

The proof of the converse is analogous, although the last part of condition (d) is needed.

∎

Notation 3.4
.

We will sometimes write
(e_{0},e_{1},f_{0},f_{1})
when referring to the romb associated to either the blue pair of edges
(e_{0},e_{1})
or to the red pair of edges
(f_{0},f_{1})
.

Remarks 3.5
.

(1)

By condition (c) in Definition
3.2
, we deduce that for each
j\geq 1
and
v\in F^{0,2j}
there are exactly two different edges
e,f
such that
s(e)=s(f)=v
, one of them, say
e
, belongs to a blue set
e\in B_{r(e)}
and the other,
f
, belongs to
R(g)
for a unique red edge
g
, that is, there is a unique
g\in R_{r(g)}
such that
s(g)=r(f)
and
f\in R(g)
.

On the other hand, by part (i) of Proposition
3.3
and the last part of condition (d), we deduce that for each
j\geq 1
and
v\in F^{0,2j-1}
there is exactly one blue edge
e\in B_{r(e)}
such that
s(e)=v
. There also exists a red edge
f\in R_{r(f)}
such that
s(f)=v
, but such red edge may
not
be the unique red edge departing from
v
.

(2)

We have exactly 2 “colors” at each vertex
v
from an even layer
F^{0,2j}
, namely the blue edges
B_{v}
and the red edges
R_{v}
.

On the other hand, by remark (1) above, we have
|s^{-1}(v)|
different “colors” at each vertex
v\in F^{2j-1}
,
j\geq 1
, each one corresponding to a different edge departing from
v
.

(3)

The disjointness of both unions in condition (e) of Definition
3.2
is automatic, due to condition (c) of the same definition and remark (1) above.

(4)

The uniqueness of the pair
(e_{0},e_{1})
in condition (f) of Definition
3.2
is also automatic, due to remark (1) above.

(5)

An
l
-diagram cannot have double red edges, except possibly at the first layer. More concretely, for
n\geq 2
, given vertices
v\in F^{0,n-1}
and
w\in F^{0,n}
, there is at most one red edge
f
such that
s(f)=w
and
r(f)=v
. This is clear if
n
is even by remark (1). Assume then that
n=2j+1
for some
j\geq 1
, and suppose that there are two red edges
f_{1},f^{\prime}_{1}
such that
s(f_{1})=s(f^{\prime}_{1})=w
and
r(f_{1})=r(f^{\prime}_{1})=v
. Let
f_{0}
be the unique red edge such that
s(f_{0})=v
, so there is a unique red edge
g\in R_{r(g)}
such that
f_{0}\in R(g)
(see again remark (1)). By condition (f) in Definition
3.2
, there is a unique pair
(e_{0},e_{1})
of blue edges such that
r(e_{0})=r(f_{0}),s(e_{0})=r(e_{1})
and
s(e_{1})=s(f_{1})=w
. But now
(f_{0},f_{1})
and
(f_{0},f^{\prime}_{1})
are two pairs of red edges such that
f_{0}\in R(g)
,
r(f_{0})=r(e_{0}),s(f_{0})=r(f_{1})=r(f^{\prime}_{1})
and
s(f_{1})=s(f^{\prime}_{1})=s(e_{1})
, and this contradicts the uniqueness statement in condition (g) of Definition
3.2
. This shows that there is at most one red edge from
w
to
v
.

Example 3.6
.

Figure
1
presents the first three layers of an
l
-diagram. Here we have, for example, that the separation
D_{u}
associated with the vertex
u\in F^{0,1}
is written as

D_{u}=\{B_{u},R(f_{1}),R(f_{2})\},

where the set
B_{u}
consists of the two blue edges having range
u
,
R(f_{1})
consists of the unique straight red edge having range
u
(which we call
g_{1}
), and
R(f_{2})
consists of the unique dotted red edge having range
u
. Similarly,

D_{v}=\{B_{v},R_{v}\},

where here
B_{v}
consists of the unique blue edge having range
v
, and
R_{v}
consists of the two dotted red edges having range
v
. It is straightforward to check that conditions (a)-(g) from Definition
3.2
are fulfilled, at least for these first three levels.

f_{2}
