Figure 1
.
The first three levels of an
l
-diagram.

In the same Figure
1
we show two examples of rombs. For instance, the first romb, based on the vertex
w\in F^{0,3}
and ending at the vertex
u\in F^{0,1}
, corresponds to condition (f) for the pair of red edges
(g_{1},g_{2})
determining a unique pair of blue edges
(e_{1},e_{2})
satisfying the romb condition, and also corresponds to condition (g) for
g=f_{1}
and the pair of blue edges
(e_{1},e_{2})
determining a unique pair of red edges
(g_{1},g_{2})
satisfying the romb condition and such that
g_{1}\in R(f_{1})
. The second romb, based on the vertex
v
, is a consequence of Proposition
3.3
.

Our correspondence will associate, to every
l
-diagram
(F,D)
, a dynamical system
(X,\sigma)
where
X
is a totally disconnected metrizable compact space, and
\sigma:X\rightarrow X
is a surjective local homeomorphism; and, conversely, an
l
-diagram
(F,D)
will be associated to every such dynamical system
(X,\sigma)
.

Notation 3.7
.

For ease of notation, we will denote by
LHomeo
the family of dynamical systems
(X,\sigma)
where
X
is a totally disconnected metrizable compact space, and
\sigma:X\rightarrow X
is a surjective local homeomorphism.

Of course, the aforementioned constructions will hold ‘up to equivalences’ in appropriate senses.

Definition 3.8
.

Let
\sigma_{1}:X_{1}\rightarrow X_{1}
,
\sigma_{2}:X_{2}\rightarrow X_{2}
be two surjective local homeomorphisms defined on totally disconnected metrizable compact spaces
X_{1},X_{2}
. We say that
(X_{1},\sigma_{1})
and
(X_{2},\sigma_{2})
are
topologically conjugate
if there exists a homeomorphism
h:X_{1}\rightarrow X_{2}
intertwining
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
, that is
h\circ\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}\circ h
.

We write
(X_{1},\sigma_{1})\sim_{t.c.}(X_{2},\sigma_{2})
in case the two dynamical systems are topologically conjugate. It is clear that
\sim_{t.c.}
is an equivalence relation.

Remark 3.9
.

The definition of conjugacy from
[
16
, Definition 2.1]
requires the additional condition that
\sigma_{1}\circ h^{-1}=h^{-1}\circ\sigma_{2}
. However this condition is automatic in our situation, as can be seen by pre- and post-composing the identity
h\circ\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}\circ h
with
h^{-1}
(see also
[
16
, Lemma 2.6]
).

For
l
-diagrams, the notion of equivalence that fits in our context (apart from the natural notion of ‘up to isomorphism’ which we will define later) is the notion of
telescoping
, or
contraction
. For an
l
-diagram
(F,D)
and a given pair
j<l
of non-negative integers, we denote

P_{j,l}=\{(e_{j},e_{j+1},\dots,e_{l-1})\mid e_{i}\in F^{1,i},s(e_{i})=r(e_{i+1})\}\subseteq\prod_{i=j}^{l-1}F^{1,i}.

The source of
\overline{e}=(e_{j},e_{j+1},\dots,e_{l-1})\in P_{j,l}
is
s(\overline{e}):=s(e_{l-1})
, and its range is
r(\overline{e}):=r(e_{j})
.

Definition 3.10
.

Given an
l
-diagram
(F,D)
and a sequence
(m_{n})_{n\geq 0}
,
0\leq m_{0}<m_{1}<m_{2}<\cdots
in
{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{0\}
, with
m_{0}
even and satisfying the property

(CR)

m_{n+1}-m_{n}\equiv 1\text{ }(\text{mod }2),

we define the
telescoping
(or
contraction
) of
(F,D)
with respect to
(m_{n})_{n\geq 0}
as the separated graph
(F^{\prime},D^{\prime})
where:

(1)

(F^{\prime})^{0,n}:=F^{0,m_{n}}
for
n\geq 0
.

(2)

(F^{\prime})^{1,n}:=P_{m_{n},m_{n+1}}^{B}\sqcup P_{m_{n},m_{n+1}}^{R}
for
n\geq 0
, where for a given pair
j<l
of non-negative integers,

P^{B}_{j,l}:=\{(e_{j},\dots,e_{l-1})\in P_{j,l}\mid e_{i}\in B_{r(e_{i})},j\leq i\leq l-1\},

P^{R}_{j,l}:=\{(f_{j},\dots,f_{l-1})\in P_{j,l}\mid f_{i}\in R_{r(f_{i})},\text{ }j\leq i\leq l-1\text{ and }f_{i+1}\in R(f_{i}),\text{ $i$ even}\}.

(3)

For
v\in(F^{\prime})^{0,2j}
with
j\geq 0
,
D^{\prime}_{v}:=\{B^{\prime}_{v},R^{\prime}_{v}\}
, where

B^{\prime}_{v}=\{\overline{e}\in P_{m_{2j},m_{2j+1}}^{B}\mid r(\overline{e})=v\},\quad R^{\prime}_{v}=\{\overline{f}\in P_{m_{2j},m_{2j+1}}^{R}\mid r(\overline{f})=v\}.

(4)

For
v\in(F^{\prime})^{0,2j+1}
with
j\geq 0
,
D^{\prime}_{v}:=\{B^{\prime}_{v}\}\cup\{R^{\prime}(\hat{f})\mid\hat{f}\in(F^{\prime})^{1,2j},s(\hat{f})=v,\hat{f}\in R^{\prime}_{r(\hat{f})}\}
, where

B^{\prime}_{v}=\{\overline{e}\in P_{m_{2j+1},m_{2j+2}}^{B}\mid r(\overline{e})=v\},

R^{\prime}(\hat{f})=\{\overline{f}=(f_{m_{2j+1}},\dots,f_{m_{2j+2}-1})\in P_{m_{2j+1},m_{2j+2}}^{R}\mid f_{m_{2j+1}}\in R(f^{\prime}_{m_{2j+1}-1})\},

where
\hat{f}=(f^{\prime}_{m_{2j}},\dots,f^{\prime}_{m_{2j+1}-1})
.

We note that both
m_{n}
and
n
have the same parity due to condition (CR) and the fact that
m_{0}
is even.

Lemma 3.11
.

(F^{\prime},D^{\prime})
as defined in Definition
3.10
is an
l
-diagram.

Proof.

First observe that
(F^{\prime},D^{\prime})
is a separated Bratteli diagram since
(F,D)
is. To prove that it is indeed an
l
-diagram, we need to check conditions (a)-(g) from Definition
3.2
. Conditions (a) and (b) follow by definition.

(3)

We first show that, for
j\geq 1
,

\bigsqcup_{v\in F^{0,m_{2j-1}}}s(B^{\prime}_{v})\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny(c.1)}}}{=}}F^{0,m_{2j}}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny(c.2)}}}{=}}\bigsqcup_{v\in F^{0,m_{2j-1}}}s(R^{\prime}_{v}).

The inclusion
\subseteq
in
(c.1)
is obvious. For the other one we use condition (c) of Definition
3.2
and part (i) of Proposition
3.3
. Given
v\in F^{0,m_{2j}}
, there exists
v_{1}\in F^{0,m_{2j}-1}
and
e_{m_{2j}-1}\in B_{v_{1}}
such that
v=s(e_{m_{2j}-1})
. Now since
v_{1}\in F^{0,m_{2j}-1}
, by part (i) of Proposition
3.3
there exists
v_{2}\in F^{0,m_{2j}-2}
and
e_{m_{2j}-2}\in B_{v_{2}}
such that
v_{1}=s(e_{m_{2j}-2})
. Proceeding by induction, we construct a sequence
(e_{m_{2j}-i})_{i=1}^{m_{2j}-m_{2j-1}}
of blue edges satisfying
s(e_{m_{2j}-i-1})=r(e_{m_{2j}-i})
and
v=s(e_{m_{2j}-1})
. Then
e:=(e_{m_{2j-1}},\dots,e_{m_{2j}-1})\in P^{B}_{m_{2j-1},m_{2j}}
is such that
s(e)=v
, so the inclusion is now proved. Now in order to prove that the union is indeed disjoint, take
v\in s(B^{\prime}_{v_{1}})\cap s(B^{\prime}_{v_{2}})
with
v_{1}\neq v_{2}\in F^{0,m_{2j-1}}
. There exist then
e_{1}\in B^{\prime}_{v_{1}}
and
e_{2}\in B^{\prime}_{v_{2}}
with
s(e_{1})=v=s(e_{2})
. Let
N
be the integer such that

(e_{1})_{N}\neq(e_{2})_{N}
and
(e_{1})_{j}=(e_{2})_{j}
for
j>N
.
It exists since
v_{1}\neq v_{2}
, so necessarily
e_{1}\neq e_{2}
. But then
s((e_{1})_{N})=s((e_{2})_{N})
and
(e_{1})_{N}\neq(e_{2})_{N}
, and this contradicts either condition (c) of Definition
3.2
or part (i) of Proposition
3.3
at the
N^{\text{th}}
layer, depending on the parity of
N
.

The same arguments hold exactly for
(c.2)
, but special care needs to be taken when proving disjointness of the union. This is because we need to make a distinction in terms of the parity of
N
which is of different nature to the above distinction. Given
v\in s(R^{\prime}_{v_{1}})\cap s(R^{\prime}_{v_{2}})
with
v_{1}\neq v_{2}\in F^{0,m_{2j-1}}
, there exist
f_{1}\in R^{\prime}_{v_{1}}
and
f_{2}\in R^{\prime}_{v_{2}}
with
s(f_{1})=v=s(f_{2})
. We can again consider the integer
N
such that

(f_{1})_{N}\neq(f_{2})_{N}
and
(f_{1})_{j}=(f_{2})_{j}
for
j>N
. Thus
s((f_{1})_{N})=s((f_{2})_{N})
and
(f_{1})_{N}\neq(f_{2})_{N}
. If
N
is odd, the contradiction arises due to condition (c) of Definition
3.2
. If
N
is even, then
s((f_{1})_{N})=s((f_{2})_{N})
belongs to an odd layer, thus
(f_{1})_{N+1}=(f_{2})_{N+1}\in R((f_{1})_{N})\cap R((f_{2})_{N})
by definition of
P_{m_{2j-1},m_{2j}}^{B}
. This is a contradiction because
R((f_{1})_{N})\cap R((f_{2})_{N})=\emptyset
since
(f_{1})_{N}\neq(f_{2})_{N}
.

Now for the second part. Take any
v\in(F^{\prime})^{0,2j-1}=F^{0,m_{2j-1}}
and
e_{1},e_{2}\in B^{\prime}_{v}
. If it were the case that
s(e_{1})=s(e_{2})
, then by applying properties (c) and (d) of Definition
3.2
at the different layers in between
F^{0,m_{2j-1}}
and
F^{0,m_{2j}}
we would deduce that
e_{1}=e_{2}
. An argument similar to the one given in the above paragraph shows the same property for
f_{1},f_{2}\in R^{\prime}_{v}
, analyzing the parity of the different layers.

(4)

The first two parts (1) and (2) follow
mutatis mutandis
the same arguments as in (c) above, using conditions (c) and (d) of Definition
3.2
and part (i) of Proposition
3.3
at the different layers.

The second property follows from the same results again, applied to the different layers.

(5)

We need to show that, for
v\in F^{0,m_{2j}}
,

\bigsqcup_{e\in B^{\prime}_{v}}s(B^{\prime}_{s(e)})=\bigsqcup_{f\in R^{\prime}_{v}}s(R^{\prime}(f)).

Note that we do not need to prove disjointness of the unions, since these will follow by Remark
3.5
(3). Let
w=s(e^{(1)})
, with
e^{(1)}=(e_{m_{2j+1}}^{(1)},\dots,e_{m_{2j+2}-1}^{(1)})\in B^{\prime}_{s(e^{(0)})}
and
e^{(0)}=(e_{m_{2j}}^{(0)},\dots,e_{m_{2j+1}-1}^{(0)})\in B^{\prime}_{v}
. Note that
(e^{(0)},e^{(1)})\in P^{B}_{m_{2j},m_{2j+2}}
, and

m_{2j+2}-m_{2j}\equiv(2j+2)-2j\equiv 0\text{ (mod }2),

so we have an
even
number of edges from
w
to
v
through the blue path
(e^{(0)},e^{(1)})
. We can then write the path as
(e^{(0)},e^{(1)})=(e_{1,0},e_{1,1},\dots,e_{n_{j},0},e_{n_{j},1})
, being
n_{j}
the natural number satisfying
2n_{j}=m_{2j+2}-m_{2j}
. For each layer
1\leq i\leq n_{j}
, we can make use of condition (e) of Definition
3.2
to conclude that there exist pairs of
red
edges
(f_{i,0},f_{i,1})
such that
f_{i,0}\in R_{r(e_{i,0})},f_{i,1}\in R(f_{i,0})
, and
s(f_{i,1})=s(e_{i,1})
. We then construct two red paths

(f^{(0)},f^{(1)})=(f_{1,0},f_{1,1},\dots,f_{n_{j},0},f_{n_{j},1})

of lengths
m_{2j+2}-m_{2j+1}
and
m_{2j+1}-m_{2j}
respectively, satisfying
f^{(0)}\in R^{\prime}_{v}
and
f^{(1)}\in R^{\prime}(f^{(0)})
. Since
w=s(f^{(1)})
, we see that
w\in s(R^{\prime}(f^{(0)}))
with
f^{(0)}\in R^{\prime}_{v}
, thus completing the proof of the first inclusion. The other inclusion is completely analogous, and so we omit it.

(6)

Let
v\in(F^{\prime})^{0,2j-1}=F^{0,m_{2j-1}}
and
(f^{(0)},f^{(1)})
a pair of red edges such that
r(f^{(0)})=v
and
s(f^{(0)})=r(f^{(1)})\in F^{0,m_{2j}}
. Note that
w:=s(f^{(1)})\in F^{0,m_{2j+1}}
and

m_{2j+1}-m_{2j-1}\equiv(2j+1)-(2j-1)\equiv 0\text{ (mod }2),

so we have an
even
number of edges from
w
to
v
through the red path
(f^{(0)},f^{(1)})
. We can then write the path as
(f^{(0)},f^{(1)})=(f_{1,0},f_{1,1},\dots,f_{n_{j},0},f_{n_{j},1})
, being
n_{j}
the natural number satisfying
2n_{j}=m_{2j+1}-m_{2j-1}
. For each layer
1\leq i\leq n_{j}
, we can make use of condition (f) of Definition
3.2
to conclude that there exist pairs of
blue
edges
(e_{i,0},e_{i,1})
such that
r(e_{i,0})=r(f_{i,0})
,
s(e_{i,0})=r(e_{i,1})
and
s(e_{i,1})=s(f_{i,1})=r(f_{i+1,0})=r(e_{i+1,0})
. We then construct two blue paths

(e^{(0)},e^{(1)})=(e_{1,0},e_{1,1},\dots,e_{n_{j},0},e_{n_{j},1})

of lengths
m_{2j+1}-m_{2j}
and
m_{2j}-m_{2j-1}
respectively, satisfying
r(e^{(0)})=r(e_{1,0})=r(f_{1,0})=v
,
s(e^{(0)})=r(e^{(1)})
and
s(e^{(1)})=s(e_{n_{j},1})=s(f_{n_{j},1})=w
. As noticed in Remark
3.5
(4), it is not necessary to prove uniqueness of this blue pair of edges
(e^{(0)},e^{(1)})
.

(7)

Let
v\in(F^{\prime})^{0,2j-1}=F^{0,m_{2j-1}}
, a red edge
g\in R^{\prime}_{r(g)}
such that
s(g)=v
, and
(e^{(0)},e^{(1)})
a pair of blue edges such that
r(e^{(0)})=v
and
s(e^{(0)})=r(e^{(1)})\in F^{0,m_{2j}}
. Note that
w:=s(e^{(1)})\in F^{0,m_{2j+1}}
, and again

m_{2j+1}-m_{2j-1}\equiv(2j+1)-(2j-1)\equiv 0\text{ (mod }2),

so we have an
even
number of edges from
w
to
v
through the blue path
(e^{(0)},e^{(1)})
. We can then write the path as
(e^{(0)},e^{(1)})=(e_{1,0},e_{1,1},\dots,e_{n_{j},0},e_{n_{j},1})
, being
n_{j}
the natural number satisfying
2n_{j}=m_{2j+1}-m_{2j-1}
. For each layer
1\leq i\leq n_{j}
, we can make use of condition (g) of Definition
3.2
to conclude that there exist unique pairs of
red
edges
(f_{i,0},f_{i,1})
such that
f_{1,0}\in R(g_{0}),f_{i,0}\in R(f_{i-1,1})
for
2\leq i\leq n_{j}
, and
r(f_{i,0})=r(e_{i,0}),s(f_{i,0})=r(f_{i,1})
and
s(f_{i,1})=s(e_{i,1})
. Here
g_{0}
is the first red edge of the path
g
, i.e. the one having source
v
. We then construct two red paths

(f^{(0)},f^{(1)})=(f_{1,0},f_{1,1},\dots,f_{n_{j},0},f_{n_{j},1})

with
f^{(0)}\in P^{R}_{m_{2j-1},m_{2j}}
and
f^{(1)}\in P^{R}_{m_{2j},m_{2j+1}}
, satisfying
r(f^{(0)})=r(f_{1,0})=r(e_{1,0})=v
,
s(f^{(0)})=r(f^{(1)})
and
s(f^{(1)})=s(f_{n_{j},1})=s(e_{n_{j},1})=w
. Moreover,
f^{(0)}\in R^{\prime}(g)
by the definition of
R^{\prime}(g)
(Definition
3.10
(d)). This is the desired red pair.

To prove uniqueness, assume that
(\widetilde{f}^{(0)},\widetilde{f}^{(1)})
is another pair of red edges satisfying the same conditions as the pair
(f^{(0)},f^{(1)})
, namely that
\widetilde{f}^{(0)}\in R^{\prime}(g)
,
r(\widetilde{f}^{(0)})=r(e^{(0)})=v
,
s(\widetilde{f}^{(0)})=r(\widetilde{f}^{(1)})
and
s(\widetilde{f}^{(1)})=s(e^{(1)})
. Write it as

(\widetilde{f}^{(0)},\widetilde{f}^{(1)})=(\widetilde{f}_{1,0},\widetilde{f}_{1,1},\dots,\widetilde{f}_{n_{j},0},\widetilde{f}_{n_{j},1}).

We apply condition (f) of Definition
3.2
to each pair of red edges
(\widetilde{f}_{i,0},\widetilde{f}_{i,1})
to conclude that there exist pairs
(\widetilde{e}_{i,0},\widetilde{e}_{i,1})
of blue edges such that
r(\widetilde{e}_{i,0})=r(\widetilde{f}_{i,0})
,
s(\widetilde{e}_{i,0})=r(\widetilde{e}_{i,1})
and
s(\widetilde{e}_{i,1})=s(\widetilde{f}_{i,1})
. Now,

s(\widetilde{e}_{n_{j},1})=s(\widetilde{f}^{(1)})=s(e^{(1)})=s(e_{n_{j},1})\in F^{0,m_{2j+1}}.

By condition (d) of Definition
3.2
, we necessarily have
\widetilde{e}_{n_{j},1}=e_{n_{j},1}
. Thus in particular
s(\widetilde{e}_{n_{j},0})=r(\widetilde{e}_{n_{j},1})=r(e_{n_{j},1})=s(e_{n_{j},0})\in F^{0,m_{2j+1}-1}
. Now condition (c) of Definition
3.2
ensures that
\widetilde{e}_{n_{j},0}=e_{n_{j},0}
necessarily. Therefore
r(\widetilde{f}_{n_{j},0})=r(\widetilde{e}_{n_{j},0})=r(e_{n_{j},0})=r(f_{n_{j},0})
. We continue in this way to get that
\widetilde{e}_{i,j}=e_{i,j}
for all
i,j
, and thus

r(f_{i,0})=r(e_{i,0})=r(\widetilde{e}_{i,0})=r(\widetilde{f}_{i,0})\quad\text{ for all }1\leq i\leq n_{j}.

Now observe that
\widetilde{f}_{i,0}\in R(g_{0})
, and thus
by the uniqueness part of condition (g) of Definition
3.2
, we get that the pairs
(\widetilde{f}_{1,0},\widetilde{f}_{1,1})
and
(f_{1,0},f_{1,1})
are the same. By applying this argument inductively (using that
f^{(0)},f^{(1)},\widetilde{f}^{(0)},\widetilde{f}^{(1)}
belong to
P^{R}_{j,l}
for suitable
j,l
), we end up concluding that
\widetilde{f}^{(0)}=f^{(0)}
and
\widetilde{f}^{(1)}=f^{(1)}
. This proves the uniqueness property we wanted to show.

The lemma is proved.
∎

Definition 3.12
.

We let
\sim_{l}
denote the equivalence relation on
l
-diagrams generated by ‘up to isomorphism’ equivalence and ‘up to contraction’ equivalence, defined as follows.

(1)

(Isomorphism of
l
-diagrams) The
l
-diagrams
(F_{1},D_{1})
and
(F_{2},D_{2})
are said to be
isomorphic
, written
(F_{1},D_{1})\cong(F_{2},D_{2})
, if there exists
\phi:(F_{1},D_{1})\rightarrow(F_{2},D_{2})
an isomorphism of separated diagrams (see
[
13
]
) such that, for any
j\geq 0
, we have

\phi_{1}((B_{1})_{v})=(B_{2})_{\phi_{0}(v)}\quad\text{ and }\quad\phi_{1}((R_{1})_{v})=(R_{2})_{\phi_{0}(v)}

in case
v\in(F_{1})^{0,2j}
, and

\phi_{1}((B_{1})_{v})=(B_{2})_{\phi_{0}(v)}\quad\text{ and }\quad\phi_{1}(R_{1}(f))=R_{2}(\phi_{1}(f))

in case
v\in(F_{1})^{0,2j+1}
, with
f\in R_{r(f)}
and
s(f)=v
. Here
R_{1}(f)\subseteq(R_{1})_{v}
and
R_{2}(\phi_{1}(f))\subseteq(R_{2})_{\phi_{0}(v)}
.

(2)

(Contraction of
l
-diagrams) The
l
-diagrams
(F_{1},D_{1})
and
(F_{2},D_{2})
are said to be
equivalent by contraction
, written
(F_{1},D_{1})\sim_{\text{ctr}}(F_{2},D_{2})
, if there exists a sequence of
l
-diagrams
\{(E_{i},C_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
with
(E_{1},C_{1})=(F_{1},D_{1})
and
(E_{n},C_{n})=(F_{2},D_{2})
so that
(E_{i+1},C_{i+1})
is obtained from
(E_{i},C_{i})
by contraction (as defined in Definition
3.10
), or vice versa. Symbolically, we will write

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(E_{1},C_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(E_{2},C_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(E_{n},C_{n})}

in case
(E_{1},C_{1})\sim_{\text{ctr}}(E_{n},C_{n})
.

It is clear that both relations
\cong
and
\sim_{\text{ctr}}
define equivalence relations, so that
\sim_{l}
is well-defined.

Lemma 3.13
.

(E_{1},C_{1})\sim_{\emph{ctr}}(E_{n},C_{n})
if and only if there exists a sequence of
l
-diagrams
\{(E_{i},C_{i})\}_{i=2}^{n-1}
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(E_{1},C_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(E_{2},C_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(E_{3},C_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(E_{n},C_{n})}

That is, each
(E_{2i},C_{2i})
can be obtained by contracting
(E_{2i-1},C_{2i-1})
and also by contracting
(E_{2i+1},C_{2i+1})
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove that the composition of two contractions is again a contraction. To this aim, suppose that
(F_{3},D_{3})
is a contraction of
(F_{2},D_{2})
and
(F_{2},D_{2})
is a contraction of
(F_{1},D_{1})
. Thus there exist strictly increasing sequences
(m_{n})_{n\geq 0}
and
(m^{\prime}_{n})_{n\geq 0}
in
{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{0\}
, with
m_{0}
and
m^{\prime}_{0}
even and satisfying (CR), and such that
(F_{2},D_{2})=(F^{\prime}_{1},D^{\prime}_{1})
and
(F_{3},D_{3})=(F^{\prime}_{2},D^{\prime}_{2})
. Then the sequence
M_{n}:=m^{\prime}_{m_{n}}
also satisfies (CR):

M_{n+1}-M_{n}=m^{\prime}_{m_{n+1}}-m^{\prime}_{m_{n}}\equiv m_{n+1}-m_{n}\equiv 1\text{ (mod }2).

Moreover,
M_{0}=m^{\prime}_{m_{0}}\equiv m_{0}\equiv 0\text{ (mod }2)
, and thus
M_{0}
is also even. The rest is now clear.
∎

Our first aim is to prove the following theorem.

Theorem 3.14
.

There exists a bijective correspondence between equivalence classes of
l
-diagrams under
\sim_{l}
and topological conjugacy classes of surjective local homeomorphisms on totally disconnected, compact metrizable spaces.

More concretely, there are well-defined maps

\Phi:\emph{\textsf{LHomeo}}/\sim_{t.c.}\longrightarrow\emph{\textsf{LDiag}}/\sim_{l},\quad\Psi:\emph{\textsf{LDiag}}/\sim_{l}\longrightarrow\emph{\textsf{LHomeo}}/\sim_{t.c.}

which are inverse of each other.

The rest of the section is devoted to the proof of Theorem
3.14
.

3.1.
From
LHomeo
to
LDiag

We start by associating an
l
-diagram to a surjective local homeomorphism. Let
(X,\sigma)\in\textsf{LHomeo}
. First, some general remarks about
\sigma
.

Lemma 3.15
.

There always exists a partition
{\pazocal P}
of
X
such that the restriction
\sigma|_{Z}
is a homeomorphism for any
Z\in{\pazocal P}
. Moreover, each
\sigma(Z)
is also clopen.

As a consequence, the set
\sigma^{-1}(\{x\})
is finite for any
x\in X
, and in fact uniformly bounded by
|{\pazocal P}|
above.

Proof.

Since
\sigma
is a local homeomorphism, for any
x\in X
we can find an open set
U_{x}
such that
\sigma(U_{x})
is open in
X
and
\sigma|_{U_{x}}
is a homeomorphism. In fact, since the space
X
is totally disconnected, we can assume that each
U_{x}
is clopen. Then
X=\bigcup_{x\in X}U_{x}
, so by compactness we can find a finite subfamily
\{U_{1},\dots,U_{n}\}
of
\{U_{x}\}_{x\in X}
such that
X=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}
, each
U_{i}
is clopen and
\sigma|_{U_{i}}
is a homeomorphism. By disjontifying
\{U_{1},\dots,U_{n}\}
, we can construct another finite family
\{V_{1},\dots,V_{m}\}
such that its elements are clopen and pairwise disjoint,
X=\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}V_{i}
, and each
\sigma|_{V_{i}}
is a homeomorphism. This family is our desired partition
{\pazocal P}
, so
|{\pazocal P}|=m
.

Note that
\sigma(V_{i})
is open in
X
for
1\leq i\leq m
. Moreover, since each
V_{i}
is closed in
X
, it is in particular compact, thus
\sigma(V_{i})
is also compact. Since
X
is a Hausdorff space, this implies that
\sigma(V_{i})
is closed. That is, each
\sigma(V_{i})
is clopen.

To conclude, assume that there is some
x\in X
with at least
m+1
different pre-images
y_{1},\dots,y_{m+1}\in X
. Since
X=\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}V_{i}
, there are at least two pre-images of
x
that lie inside the same
V_{i}
, say
y_{1},y_{2}\in V_{i}
. But this contradicts the fact that
\sigma
is injective when restricted to
V_{i}
. The lemma now follows.
∎

Let
N(\sigma):=\text{sup}\{|\sigma^{-1}(\{x\})|\mid x\in X\}
, which is a finite quantity by the lemma above. For each
1\leq i\leq N(\sigma)
, define

{\pazocal U}_{i}:=\{x\in X\mid|\sigma^{-1}(\{x\})|=i\}.

Since
\sigma
is surjective, the family
\{{\pazocal U}_{1},\dots,{\pazocal U}_{N(\sigma)}\}
covers
X
. In fact, they form a partition of
X
into clopen sets, as we prove in the following lemma.

Lemma 3.16
.

The family
\{{\pazocal U}_{1},\dots,{\pazocal U}_{N(\sigma)}\}
forms a partition of
X
into clopen sets.

Proof.

It is clear that the sets
{\pazocal U}_{1},\dots,{\pazocal U}_{N(\sigma)}
are disjoint and cover
X
. Thus it is enough to show that each
{\pazocal U}_{i}
is clopen. We will in fact show that each set

{\pazocal U}_{\geq i}:=\{x\in X\mid|\sigma^{-1}(\{x\})|\geq i\},

for
1\leq i\leq N(\sigma)
, is clopen, from which the result will follow immediately since
{\pazocal U}_{i}={\pazocal U}_{\geq i}\backslash{\pazocal U}_{\geq i+1}
for
1\leq i<N(\sigma)
, and
{\pazocal U}_{N(\sigma)}={\pazocal U}_{\geq N(\sigma)}
.

By letting
{\pazocal P}
be any partition satisfying the properties of Lemma
3.15
, we see that

{\pazocal U}_{\geq i}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{i})\in{\pazocal P}^{i}\\
\text{all different}\end{subarray}}\sigma(Z_{1})\cap\cdots\cap\sigma(Z_{i}).

Indeed, given
x\in{\pazocal U}_{\geq i}
, there exist at least
i
different pre-images of
x
, namely
y_{1},\dots,y_{i}
. Since
\sigma
restricts to a homeomorphism on each
Z\in{\pazocal P}
, it is clear that
y_{j}\in Z_{j}
for different
Z_{j}
’s. Thus
x\in\sigma(Z_{1})\cap\cdots\cap\sigma(Z_{i})
for
(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{i})\in{\pazocal P}^{i}
all different. The converse inclusion is trivial, since any element in
\sigma(Z_{1})\cap\cdots\cap\sigma(Z_{i})
for
(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{i})\in{\pazocal P}^{i}
all different has, by definition, at least
i
different pre-images.

The result follows since each
\sigma(Z)
is clopen.
∎

Remark 3.17
.

Any covering map of topological spaces (in the sense of
[
25
]
) is a surjective local homeomorphism, and any surjective local homeomorphism on a compact space is a covering map. Hence the results from
[
29
]
and
[
33
]
apply to the family of dynamical systems studied in the present paper. In particular it follows from
[
33
, Theorem 9.1]
that, if
(X,\sigma)\in\textsf{LHomeo}
, then the groupoid
C^{*}
-algebra
C^{*}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma))
is isomorphic to the crossed product
C(X)\rtimes_{\alpha,\mathscr{L}}\mathbb{N}
, where
\alpha\colon C(X)\to C(X)
is the
*
-homomorphism given by
\alpha(f)=f\circ\alpha
and
\mathscr{L}\colon C(X)\to C(X)
is the transfer operator

\mathscr{L}(f)(x)=\frac{1}{|\sigma^{-1}(x)|}\sum_{y\in\sigma^{-1}(x)}f(y).

Note that in
[
25
, Example 4.5]
it is constructed an example of a surjective local homeomorphism
f\colon X\to Y
which is not a covering map, where
X
and
Y
are totally disconnected, locally compact, second-countable spaces. Take
Z:=\bigsqcup_{n=0}^{\infty}Y_{n}\sqcup\bigsqcup_{m=0}^{\infty}X_{m}
, where
Y_{n}=Y
for all
n\geq 0
and
X_{m}=X
for all
m\geq 0
. Denoting by
y_{n}
the copy of
y\in Y
in
Y_{n}
, and by
x_{m}
the copy of
x\in X
in
X_{m}
, we define
\sigma\colon Z\to Z
by
\sigma(y_{m})=y_{m+1}
for
m\geq 0
,
\sigma(x_{n})=x_{n-1}
for all
n\geq 1
, and
\sigma(x_{0})=f(x)_{0}
. Then
Z
is a totally disconnected, locally compact, second countable space, and
\sigma
is a surjective local homeomorphism on
Z
which is not a covering map. Another nice example of a surjective local homeomorphism which is not a covering map is the map
f\colon{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\{0\}\to{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\{0\}
defined by
f(z)=e^{z}
.

Definition 3.18
.

For a partition
{\pazocal P}
of
X
such that
\sigma
restricts to a homeomorphism on each
Z\in{\pazocal P}
, we define new partitions

(1)

{\pazocal P}^{\sigma}:=\bigcup_{k=1}^{N(\sigma)}\{\sigma(Z_{1})\cap\cdots\cap\sigma(Z_{k})\backslash{\pazocal U}_{\geq k+1}\neq\emptyset\mid Z_{i}\in{\pazocal P}\text{ all different}\}
;

(2)

\sigma^{-1}({\pazocal P}):=\{\sigma^{-1}(Z)\mid Z\in{\pazocal P}\}
.

The next lemma shows that
{\pazocal P}^{\sigma}
and
\sigma^{-1}({\pazocal P})
are indeed well-defined.

Lemma 3.19
.

The sets
{\pazocal P}^{\sigma}
and
\sigma^{-1}({\pazocal P})
constitute partitions of
X
. Also, if
{\pazocal P}_{1}
and
{\pazocal P}_{2}
are partitions of
X
, then

({\pazocal P}_{1}\vee{\pazocal P}_{2})^{\sigma}={\pazocal P}_{1}^{\sigma}\vee{\pazocal P}_{2}^{\sigma}\quad\text{ and }\quad\sigma^{-1}({\pazocal P}_{1}\vee{\pazocal P}_{2})=\sigma^{-1}({\pazocal P}_{1})\vee\sigma^{-1}({\pazocal P}_{2}).

Proof.

The statement for
\sigma^{-1}({\pazocal P})
is clear. Let us prove it for
{\pazocal P}^{\sigma}
.

First, note that each non-empty
\overline{Z}=\sigma(Z_{1})\cap\cdots\cap\sigma(Z_{k})\backslash{\pazocal U}_{\geq k+1}\in{\pazocal P}^{\sigma}
is clopen in
X
, since
{\pazocal U}_{\geq k+1}
is clopen and each
\sigma(Z_{i})
is clopen too.

Second, take
\overline{Z}_{1}=\sigma(Z_{1})\cap\cdots\cap\sigma(Z_{k})\backslash{\pazocal U}_{\geq k+1}
and
\overline{Z}_{2}=\sigma(Z^{\prime}_{1})\cap\cdots\cap\sigma(Z^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}})\backslash{\pazocal U}_{\geq k^{\prime}+1}
elements from
{\pazocal P}^{\sigma}
, so that
(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{k})
are all different, and so are
(Z^{\prime}_{1},\dots,Z^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}})
. If say
k>k^{\prime}
, then
(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{k})
constitute
k>k^{\prime}
different pairwise disjoint clopen sets, thus
\sigma(Z_{1})\cap\cdots\cap\sigma(Z_{k})\subseteq{\pazocal U}_{\geq k^{\prime}+1}
. But then

\overline{Z}_{1}\cap\overline{Z}_{2}=\sigma(Z_{1})\cap\cdots\cap\sigma(Z_{k})\cap\sigma(Z^{\prime}_{1})\cap\cdots\cap\sigma(Z^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}})\backslash({\pazocal U}_{\geq k+1}\cup{\pazocal U}_{\geq k^{\prime}+1})\subseteq{\pazocal U}_{\geq k^{\prime}+1}\backslash{\pazocal U}_{\geq k^{\prime}+1}=\emptyset.

The argument for
k^{\prime}>k
is similar, so we may assume that
k^{\prime}=k
. But then

\overline{Z}_{1}\cap\overline{Z}_{2}=\sigma(Z_{1})\cap\cdots\cap\sigma(Z_{k})\cap\sigma(Z^{\prime}_{1})\cap\cdots\cap\sigma(Z^{\prime}_{k})\backslash{\pazocal U}_{\geq k+1}.

If some
Z_{i}
is different from all
Z^{\prime}_{j}
’s, then
(Z_{i},Z^{\prime}_{1},\dots,Z^{\prime}_{k})
are all different, thus
\sigma(Z_{i})\cap\sigma(Z^{\prime}_{1})\cap\cdots\cap\sigma(Z^{\prime}_{k})\subseteq{\pazocal U}_{\geq k+1}
. This gives, as before,
\overline{Z}_{1}\cap\overline{Z}_{2}=\emptyset
. This proves that the elements of
{\pazocal P}^{\sigma}
are pairwise disjoint.

To conclude, simply note that
\{\sigma(Z_{1})\cap\cdots\cap\sigma(Z_{k})\backslash{\pazocal U}_{\geq k+1}\neq\emptyset\mid Z_{i}\in{\pazocal P}\text{ all different}\}
gives a partition of
{\pazocal U}_{k}
, thus
\bigcup_{\overline{Z}\in{\pazocal P}^{\sigma}}\overline{Z}={\pazocal U}_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup{\pazocal U}_{N(\sigma)}=X
.

The rest of the lemma is straightforward.
∎

We now define the key concept of a
\sigma
-refined sequence of partitions.

Definition 3.20
.

We say that a sequence of partitions
\{{\pazocal P}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
of
X
is a
\sigma
-refined sequence of partitions
of
X
if the following properties hold:

(1)

\sigma
restricts to a homeomorphism
\sigma|_{Z}
for any
Z\in{\pazocal P}_{0}
(the existence of such a partition is guaranteed by Lemma
3.15
).

(2)

{\pazocal P}_{n}\precsim{\pazocal P}_{n+1}
, that is
{\pazocal P}_{n+1}
is finer than
{\pazocal P}_{n}
, for all
n\geq 0
.

(3)

The union
\bigcup_{n\geq 0}{\pazocal P}_{n}
generates the topology of
X
.

(4)

\text{diam}({\pazocal P}_{n})
tends to
0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

(5)

We have

{\pazocal P}_{2j}\vee{\pazocal P}_{2j}^{\sigma}\precsim{\pazocal P}_{2j+1},\quad\text{and}\quad{\pazocal P}_{2j+1}\vee\sigma^{-1}({\pazocal P}_{2j+1})\precsim{\pazocal P}_{2j+2}

for all
j\geq 0
.

Note that conditions (a) and (b) together imply that, for any
n\geq 0
,
\sigma
restricts to a homeomorphism
\sigma|_{Z}
for any
Z\in{\pazocal P}_{n}
.

Remark 3.21
.

Since
X
is compact and all our partitions are finite, it is straightforward to check that any
\sigma
-refined sequence of partitions of
X
in the sense of Definition
3.20
is a complete, tame defining sequence in the sense of
[
28
, Definitions 3.1.1 and 3.1.8]
.

Lemma 3.22
.

Let
(X,\sigma)\in\emph{\textsf{LHomeo}}
. Then
X
admits a
\sigma
-refined sequence of partitions.

Proof.

Take a sequence of partitions
{\pazocal P}^{\prime}_{n}
satisfying conditions (a)-(d) in Definition
3.20
. It always exists by our assumptions on the space
X
. Now define new partitions inductively by

\displaystyle{\pazocal P}_{0}:={\pazocal P}^{\prime}_{0};

\displaystyle{\pazocal P}_{2j+1}:={\pazocal P}^{\prime}_{2j+1}\vee{\pazocal P}_{2j}\vee{\pazocal P}_{2j}^{\sigma}

\displaystyle\text{ for all }j\geq 0;

\displaystyle{\pazocal P}_{2j+2}:={\pazocal P}^{\prime}_{2j+2}\vee{\pazocal P}_{2j+1}\vee\sigma^{-1}({\pazocal P}_{2j+1})

\displaystyle\text{ for all }j\geq 0.

It is clear that the sequence
\{{\pazocal P}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
satisfies the required properties.
∎

Thus from now on we will assume that a
\sigma
-refined sequence of partitions
\{{\pazocal P}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
is given.

Examples 3.23
.

(1)

Take
X=\{0,1\}^{{\mathbb{Z}}}
as a model for the Cantor set, and define the two-sided shift

\varphi\colon X\to X,\quad\varphi(x)_{i}=x_{i+1}.

A basis for the topology of
X
is again given by the cylinder sets: for integers
r,l\geq 0
and
\varepsilon_{-l},...,\varepsilon_{r}\in\{0,1\}
, these are given by

[\varepsilon_{-l}\cdots\underline{\varepsilon_{0}}\cdots\varepsilon_{r}]:=\{x\in X\mid x_{i}=\varepsilon_{i}\text{ for }-l\leq i\leq r\}.

With this topology,
(X,\varphi)\in\textsf{LHomeo}
, and in fact
\varphi
is even a homeomorphism. The sequence of partitions
\{{\pazocal P}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
, given, for
n\geq 0
, by

{\pazocal P}_{2n}=\{[\varepsilon_{-n}\cdots\underline{\varepsilon_{0}}\cdots\varepsilon_{n}]\mid\varepsilon_{i}\in\{0,1\}\text{ for }-n\leq i\leq n\},

{\pazocal P}_{2n+1}=\{[\varepsilon_{-n-1}\varepsilon_{-n}\cdots\underline{\varepsilon_{0}}\cdots\varepsilon_{n}]\mid\varepsilon_{i}\in\{0,1\}\text{ for }-n-1\leq i\leq n\},

is a
\varphi
-refined sequence of partitions of
X
.

(2)

Take
X^{+}=\{0,1\}^{{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}
as another model for the Cantor set, but consider now the one-sided shift

\sigma\colon X^{+}\to X^{+},\quad\sigma(x)_{i}=x_{i+1}.

A basis for the topology of
X^{+}
is again given by the cylinder sets, where now
l=0
. Even though
\sigma
is not a homeomorphism, it is certainly a surjective local homeomorphism, so
(X^{+},\sigma)\in\textsf{LHomeo}
. The sequence of partitions
\{{\pazocal P}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
, given, for
n\geq 0
, by

{\pazocal P}_{n}=\{[\underline{\varepsilon_{0}}\cdots\varepsilon_{n}]\mid\varepsilon_{i}\in\{0,1\}\text{ for }0\leq i\leq n\},

is a
\sigma
-refined sequence of partitions of
X^{+}
.

The main idea to construct the
l
-diagram
(F,D)
from
(X,\sigma)
is the following: the vertices will correspond to the sets
Z\in{\pazocal P}_{n}
, with
{\pazocal P}_{n}
a
\sigma
-refined sequence of partitions; the blue edges will correspond to inclusion maps
Z\subseteq Z^{\prime}
, and the red edges will correspond to the action of
\sigma
or
\sigma^{-1}
, which in the latter case will give rise to various red edges departing from the same vertex.

Construction 3.24
.

Take
\{{\pazocal P}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
to be any
\sigma
-refined sequence of partitions of
X
. We construct
(F,D)
inductively, as follows.

(1)

(Vertices) For
n\geq 0
we set
F^{0,n}={\pazocal P}_{n}
, so that the vertices at the
n^{\text{th}}
layer are the clopen sets in the partition
{\pazocal P}_{n}
.

(2)

(Edges) For
n\geq 0
, we let
F^{1,n}=B^{(n)}\sqcup R^{(n)}
, where the set
B^{(n)}
will consist on the blue edges, and the set
R^{(n)}
will be the set of red edges, which we now define. We also set, for
Z\in F^{0,n}
,

B_{Z}=\{e\in B^{(n)}\mid r(e)=Z\},\quad R_{Z}=\{f\in R^{(n)}\mid r(f)=Z\}.

(b.1)

(Blue edges) The blue edges are defined as follows. For any pair
Z\in{\pazocal P}_{n},Z^{\prime}\in{\pazocal P}_{n+1}
such that
Z^{\prime}\subseteq Z
, we write down a blue edge
e(Z^{\prime},Z)
with source
Z^{\prime}
and range
Z
. Thus

B^{(n)}=\{e(Z^{\prime},Z)\mid Z\in{\pazocal P}_{n},Z^{\prime}\in{\pazocal P}_{n+1}\text{ such that }Z^{\prime}\subseteq Z\}.

(b.2)

(Red edges) For the definition of red edges, we need to distinguish between
even
and
odd
layers.

We start with an even layer
n=2j,j\geq 0
. For each pair
Z\in{\pazocal P}_{2j}
and
Z^{\prime}\in{\pazocal P}_{2j+1}
such that
Z^{\prime}\subseteq\sigma(Z)
, we write down a red edge
f(Z^{\prime},Z)
with source
Z^{\prime}
and range
Z
. Thus

R^{(2j)}=\{f(Z^{\prime},Z)\mid Z\in{\pazocal P}_{2j},Z^{\prime}\in{\pazocal P}_{2j+1}\text{ such that }Z^{\prime}\subseteq\sigma(Z)\}.

Note that it may be the case that there are different red edges departing from the same vertex
Z^{\prime}
, simply because
Z^{\prime}
is in principle contained in an intersection
\sigma(Z_{1})\cap\cdots\cap\sigma(Z_{l})
for some
Z_{i}\in{\pazocal P}_{2j}
.

Now we pay our attention to an odd layer
n=2j+1,j\geq 0
. For
Z^{\prime}\in{\pazocal P}_{2j+1}
and
Z^{\prime\prime}\in{\pazocal P}_{2j+2}
such that
\sigma(Z^{\prime\prime})\subseteq Z^{\prime}
(or equivalently
Z^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\sigma^{-1}(Z^{\prime})
), we write down a red edge
f(Z^{\prime\prime},Z^{\prime})
with source
Z^{\prime\prime}
and range
Z^{\prime}
. Thus

R^{(2j+1)}=\{f(Z^{\prime\prime},Z^{\prime})\mid Z^{\prime}\in{\pazocal P}_{2j+1},Z^{\prime\prime}\in{\pazocal P}_{2j+2}\text{ such that }Z^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\sigma^{-1}(Z^{\prime})\}.

Note that here, in contrast to what happens for even layers, there is only one red edge departing from each vertex
Z^{\prime\prime}\in{\pazocal P}_{2j+2}
.

(3)

(Separations) We again distinguish between even and odd layers. For an even layer
n=2j
,
j\geq 0
, the separation for
Z\in F^{0,2j}
is defined to be

D_{Z}=\{B_{Z},R_{Z}\}.

For an odd layer
n=2j+1
,
j\geq 0
, the separation for
Z^{\prime}\in F^{0,2j+1}
is defined to be

D_{Z^{\prime}}=\{B_{Z^{\prime}}\}\cup\{R(f)\mid s(f)=Z^{\prime},f\in R_{r(f)}\},

where here
R(f)
denotes the set of all those red edges
g\in R_{Z^{\prime}}
such that
s(g)\subseteq r(f)
. Thus
R_{Z^{\prime}}=\bigcup_{f\in s^{-1}(Z^{\prime})\cap R_{r(f)}}R(f)
.

Z\in F^{0,2j}
Z^{\prime}\in F^{0,2j+1}
Z^{\prime\prime}\in F^{0,2j+2}
Z^{\prime\prime}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\rightarrow Z^{\prime}\text{ if }Z^{\prime\prime}\subseteq Z^{\prime}
Z^{\prime\prime}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rightarrow Z^{\prime}\text{ if }\sigma(Z^{\prime\prime})\subseteq Z^{\prime}
Z^{\prime}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\rightarrow Z\text{ if }Z^{\prime}\subseteq Z
Z^{\prime}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rightarrow Z\text{ if }Z^{\prime}\subseteq\sigma(Z)

Figure 2
.
Construction of the edges of
(F,D)
.

In this way we build a separated Bratteli diagram
(F,D)
. We now show that
(F,D)
is an
l
-diagram. Indeed, it is an
l
-diagram of a special type, since by definition it does not have double red edges in any of its layers. This motivates the following definition.

Definition 3.25
.

Let
(F,D)
be an
l
-diagram. We say that
(F,D)
is a
refined
l
-diagram
if for any two vertices
w\in F^{0,n}
and
v\in F^{0,n-1}
,
n\geq 1
, there is at most one red edge
f
such that
s(f)=w
and
r(f)=v
. By Remark
3.5
(5), this is equivalent to asking the property only at the first layer, that is, for
n=1
.

The following lemma will be useful in the sequel.

Lemma 3.26
.

Let
\{{\pazocal P}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
be a
\sigma
-refined sequence of partitions of
X
.

(1)

For
j\geq 0
, let
Z_{1}\in{\pazocal P}_{2j}
, and let
g\in R_{Z_{1}}
, with
s(g)=Z
. Then
Z\subseteq\sigma(Z_{1})
, and we have

(\sigma|_{Z_{1}})^{-1}(Z)=\sigma^{-1}(Z)\cap Z_{1}=\bigsqcup_{f\in R(g)}s(f).

(2)

For
j\geq 1
, let
Z\in{\pazocal P}_{2j-1}
,
Z^{\prime\prime}\in{\pazocal P}_{2j+1}
and
Z_{1}\in{\pazocal P}_{2j-2}
be such that

Z^{\prime\prime}\subseteq Z\subseteq\sigma(Z_{1}).

Then there is a unique
Z^{\prime}\in{\pazocal P}_{2j}
such that

Z^{\prime}\subseteq Z_{1}\qquad\text{and}\qquad Z^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\sigma(Z^{\prime})\subseteq Z\subseteq\sigma(Z_{1}).

Proof.

(1)

First note that
Z\subseteq\sigma(Z_{1})
by definition of the red edges at even layers.

Now note that since
{\pazocal P}_{2j}\vee\sigma^{-1}({\pazocal P}_{2j+1})\precsim{\pazocal P}_{2j+1}\vee\sigma^{-1}({\pazocal P}_{2j+1})\precsim{\pazocal P}_{2j+2}
, we can write the set
\sigma^{-1}(Z)\cap Z_{1}
as a disjoint union of sets
Z^{\prime\prime}\in{\pazocal P}_{2j+2}
. If
Z^{\prime\prime}\in{\pazocal P}_{2j+2}
and
Z^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\sigma^{-1}(Z)\cap Z_{1}
, then there is a unique red edge
f
such that
s(f)=Z^{\prime\prime}
and
r(f)=Z
, and moreover
s(f)=Z^{\prime\prime}\subseteq Z_{1}=r(g)
, so that
f\in R(g)
. Conversely any
f\in R(g)
gives rise to a set
Z^{\prime\prime}:=s(f)\in{\pazocal P}_{2j+2}
with
Z^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\sigma^{-1}(Z)\cap Z_{1}
. This shows the result.

(2)

Take
j\geq 1
,
Z\in{\pazocal P}_{2j-1}
,
Z^{\prime\prime}\in{\pazocal P}_{2j+1}
, and
Z_{1}\in{\pazocal P}_{2j-2}
such that
Z^{\prime\prime}\subseteq Z\subseteq\sigma(Z_{1})
. Since
{\pazocal P}_{2j}^{\sigma}
is coarser than
{\pazocal P}_{2j+1}
, we can find sets
Z^{\prime}_{1},Z^{\prime}_{2},\dots,Z^{\prime}_{k}\in{\pazocal P}_{2j}
such that

Z^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\sigma(Z^{\prime}_{1})\cap\cdots\cap\sigma(Z^{\prime}_{k})\setminus{\pazocal U}_{\geq k+1}.

We have that
Z^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\sigma(Z^{\prime}_{i})
for
1\leq i\leq k
and
Z^{\prime\prime}\cap\sigma(\overline{Z})=\emptyset
for all
\overline{Z}\in{\pazocal P}_{2j}
distinct from
Z^{\prime}_{1},\dots,Z^{\prime}_{k}
. On the other hand note that, since
Z\subseteq\sigma(Z_{1})
, there is a unique red edge
g
such that
s(g)=Z
and
r(g)=Z_{1}
. By i), we have

(\sigma|_{Z_{1}})^{-1}(Z)=\sigma^{-1}(Z)\cap Z_{1}=\bigsqcup_{f\in R(g)}s(f).

Since
\sigma
is injective on all sets of the partitions
{\pazocal P}_{n}
, we obtain that

Z=\bigsqcup_{f\in R(g)}\sigma(s(f)).

Since
Z^{\prime\prime}\subseteq Z
, it follows that
Z^{\prime\prime}\cap\sigma(s(f))\neq\emptyset
for some
f\in R(g)
. But by the property mentioned above, this means that
s(f)
is one of the sets
Z_{1}^{\prime},\dots,Z_{k}^{\prime}
and thus
Z^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\sigma(s(f))
. Since the sets
\sigma(s(f))
are mutually disjoint for
f\in R(g)
, it follows that there is exactly one
f\in R(g)
such that
Z^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\sigma(s(f))
. Let
f_{0}
be this unique edge in
R(g)
, and set
Z^{\prime}:=s(f_{0})
. Since
f_{0}\in R(g)
we have
Z^{\prime}=s(f_{0})\subseteq r(g)=Z_{1}
and moreover

Z^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\sigma(s(f_{0}))=\sigma(Z^{\prime})\subseteq Z\subseteq\sigma(Z_{1}),

as desired.

∎

Lemma 3.27
.

The separated graph
(F,D)
associated to a
\sigma
-refined sequence of partitions
\{{\pazocal P}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
is a refined
l
-diagram.

Proof.

The condition of not having double red edges follows directly from the definition. We need to check conditions (a)-(g) from Definition
3.2
. Conditions (a) and (b) follow by definition.

(3)

We first show that, for
j\geq 1
,

\bigsqcup_{Z\in{\pazocal P}_{2j-1}}s(B_{Z})\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny(c.1)}}}{=}}{\pazocal P}_{2j}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny(c.2)}}}{=}}\bigsqcup_{Z\in{\pazocal P}_{2j-1}}s(R_{Z}).

We prove the non-trivial inclusion in
(c.1)
. Given
Z^{\prime}\in{\pazocal P}_{2j}
, we can find a (unique)
Z\in{\pazocal P}_{2j-1}
such that
Z^{\prime}\subseteq Z
since
{\pazocal P}_{2j-1}
is coarser than
{\pazocal P}_{2j}
. Therefore there exists, by definition, a blue edge
e(Z^{\prime},Z)
with source
Z^{\prime}
and range
Z
. This proves
(c.1)
. Disjointness of the left union follows from the uniqueness of the set
Z
. Indeed, given
Z^{\prime}\in s(B_{Z_{1}})\cap s(B_{Z_{2}})
with
Z_{1}\neq Z_{2}\in{\pazocal P}_{2j-1}
, we have
Z^{\prime}\subseteq Z_{1}\cap Z_{2}=\emptyset
, a contradiction.

Let us now prove the non-trivial inclusion in
(c.2)
. Since
\sigma^{-1}({\pazocal P}_{2j-1})
is a partition coarser than
{\pazocal P}_{2j}
, given
Z^{\prime}\in{\pazocal P}_{2j}
we can find
Z\in{\pazocal P}_{2j-1}
such that
Z^{\prime}\subseteq\sigma^{-1}(Z)
, equivalently
\sigma(Z^{\prime})\subseteq Z
. This says that there is a red edge
f(Z^{\prime},Z)
with source
Z^{\prime}
and range
Z
. This proves the desired inclusion. The disjointness of the right union follows by analogous arguments as in the above paragraph.

The second part of condition (c) follows by the definition of the edges in
(F,D)
.

(4)

The fact that

\bigsqcup_{Z\in{\pazocal P}_{0}}s(B_{Z})={\pazocal P}_{1}=\bigcup_{Z\in{\pazocal P}_{0}}s(R_{Z})

follows from identical arguments as in (c), using now the fact that the partition
{\pazocal P}_{0}
is coarser than the partition
{\pazocal P}_{1}
. Disjointness of the left union is proved in a similar way as in (c).

The second part of condition (d) follows by the definition of the blue edges in
(F,D)
.

(5)

We need to show that, for
j\geq 0
and
Z\in{\pazocal P}_{2j}
,

\bigcup_{e\in B_{Z}}s(B_{s(e)})=\bigcup_{f\in R_{Z}}s(R(f)).

Disjointness of the unions will follow from condition (c) (see Remark
3.5
(3)).

Let
Z^{\prime\prime}=s(e_{1})
for some
e_{1}\in B_{s(e_{0})}
and some
e_{0}\in B_{Z}
, so that
Z^{\prime\prime}\subseteq s(e_{0})\subseteq Z
. Since
\sigma^{-1}({\pazocal P}_{2j+1})
is a coarser partition than
{\pazocal P}_{2j+2}
we can find
Z^{\prime}\in{\pazocal P}_{2j+1}
such that
\sigma(Z^{\prime\prime})\subseteq Z^{\prime}
. Thus there exists a red edge
f_{1}=f(Z^{\prime\prime},Z^{\prime})\in R_{Z^{\prime}}
such that
Z^{\prime\prime}=s(f_{1})
. Note now that
Z^{\prime}\cap\sigma(Z)\neq\emptyset
, so by the construction of the partition
{\pazocal P}_{2j}^{\sigma}
and the fact that
{\pazocal P}_{2j}^{\sigma}
is coarser than
{\pazocal P}_{2j+1}
, we necessarily have
Z^{\prime}\subseteq\sigma(Z)
. Therefore there exists another red edge
f_{0}=f(Z^{\prime},Z)\in R_{Z}
with
Z^{\prime}=s(f_{0})
. Since
s(f_{1})=Z^{\prime\prime}\subseteq Z=r(f_{0})
, we have
f_{1}\in R(f_{0})
. This proves the inclusion
\subseteq
.

For the other inclusion, let
Z^{\prime\prime}=s(f_{1})
for some
f_{1}\in R(f_{0})
and some
f_{0}\in R_{Z}
, so that
Z^{\prime\prime}=s(f_{1})\subseteq r(f_{0})=Z
. Since
{\pazocal P}_{2j+1}
is coarser than
{\pazocal P}_{2j+2}
, we can find
Z^{\prime}\in{\pazocal P}_{2j+1}
with
Z^{\prime\prime}\subseteq Z^{\prime}
. But then
Z^{\prime}\cap Z\neq\emptyset
, so we necessarily have that
Z^{\prime}\subseteq Z
by the fact that
{\pazocal P}_{2j}
is coarser than
{\pazocal P}_{2j+1}
. This means precisely that there exist blue edges
e_{0}\in B_{Z}
with
Z^{\prime}=s(e_{0})
and
e_{1}\in B_{s(e_{0})}
with
Z^{\prime\prime}=s(e_{1})
. This concludes the proof of the desired inclusion.

(6)

Take
j\geq 1
,
Z\in{\pazocal P}_{2j-1}
and a pair
(f_{0},f_{1})
of red edges such that
f_{0}\in R_{Z},f_{1}\in R_{s(f_{0})}
. By letting
Z^{\prime\prime}:=s(f_{1})
, we have by definition
Z^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\sigma(\overline{Z}^{\prime})\subseteq Z
where
\overline{Z}^{\prime}:=s(f_{0})=r(f_{1})
. Since
{\pazocal P}_{2j}
is coarser than
{\pazocal P}_{2j+1}
, we can find
Z^{\prime}\in{\pazocal P}_{2j}
with
Z^{\prime\prime}\subseteq Z^{\prime}
. But then
Z^{\prime}\cap Z\neq\emptyset
, so necessarily
Z^{\prime}\subseteq Z
since
{\pazocal P}_{2j-1}
is coarser than
{\pazocal P}_{2j}
. This means precisely that there exist blue edges
e_{0}\in B_{Z}
with
Z^{\prime}=s(e_{0})
and
e_{1}\in B_{s(e_{0})}
with
Z^{\prime\prime}=s(e_{1})
. The pair of blue edges
(e_{0},e_{1})
is unique by Remark
3.5
(4). This shows condition (f).

(7)

Take
j\geq 1
,
Z\in{\pazocal P}_{2j-1}
, a red edge
g\in R_{r(g)}
with
Z=s(g)
and
Z_{1}:=r(g)
, and a pair
(e_{0},e_{1})
of blue edges such that
e_{0}\in B_{Z}
and
s(e_{0})=r(e_{1})
. By letting
Z^{\prime\prime}:=s(e_{1})
, we have by definition
Z^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\overline{Z}^{\prime}\subseteq Z
where
\overline{Z}^{\prime}:=s(e_{0})=r(e_{1})
. We obtain the chain

Z^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\overline{Z}^{\prime}\subseteq Z\subseteq\sigma(Z_{1}).

By Lemma
3.26
(ii), there is a unique
Z^{\prime}\in{\pazocal P}_{2j}
such that
Z^{\prime}\subseteq Z_{1}
and

Z^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\sigma(Z^{\prime})\subseteq Z\subseteq\sigma(Z_{1}).

Let
f_{0}
be the red edge such that
Z^{\prime}=s(f_{0})
and
Z=r(f_{0})
, and observe that
f_{0}\in R(g)
because
s(f_{0})\subseteq r(g)
. We then have
Z^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\sigma(s(f_{0}))
, and this means that there is a unique red edge
f_{1}
such that
Z^{\prime\prime}=s(f_{1})
and
Z^{\prime}=r(f_{1})=s(f_{0})
. We have that
r(f_{0})=Z=r(e_{0})
and
s(f_{1})=Z^{\prime\prime}=s(e_{1})
. Therefore we have proved that there is a unique pair
(f_{0},f_{1})
of red edges such that
f_{0}\in R(g)
,
r(f_{0})=r(e_{0})
,
s(f_{0})=r(f_{1})
and
s(f_{1})=s(e_{1})
, as required.

This proves the lemma.
∎

Examples 3.28
.

This is a continuation of Examples
3.23
.

(1)

Take
X=\{0,1\}^{{\mathbb{Z}}}
and
\varphi\colon X\to X
to be the two-sided shift. Using the
\varphi
-refined sequence of partitions
\{{\pazocal P}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
given in Examples
3.23
1), we obtain the
l
-diagram shown in Figure
3
.

[\underline{0}]
[\underline{1}]

[1\underline{0}]

[1\underline{0}1]

.

.

(2)

Take
X^{+}=\{0,1\}^{{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}
and
\sigma\colon X^{+}\to X^{+}
to be the one-sided shift. Using the
\sigma
-refined sequence of partitions
\{{\pazocal P}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
given in Examples
3.23
2), we obtain the
l
-diagram shown in Figure
4
.

[\underline{0}]
[\underline{1}]

[\underline{1}1]
[\underline{0}00]

{\pazocal P}_{1}
{\pazocal P}_{2}

.
First two levels of the
l
-diagram associated with
(X^{+},\sigma)
.

We now proceed by showing that the equivalence class, under
\sim_{l}
, of the
l
-diagram constructed from
(X,\sigma)
does not depend on the choice of the
\sigma
-refined sequence of partitions
\{{\pazocal P}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
. First, a proposition.

Proposition 3.29
.

Let
\{{\pazocal P}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
be a
\sigma
-refined sequence of partitions of
X
. Let
(F,D)
be the
l
-diagram obtained by applying the construction
3.24
. Let also
(m_{n})_{n\geq 0}
be a sequence in
{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{0\}
with
0\leq m_{0}<m_{1}<\cdots
,
m_{0}
even and satisfying
(CR)
, and let

(1)

(F^{\prime},D^{\prime})
be the
l
-diagram obtained by telescoping
(F,D)
with respect to
(m_{n})_{n\geq 0}
;

(2)

(F^{\prime\prime},D^{\prime\prime})
be the
l
-diagram obtained from the
\sigma
-refined sequence of partitions
\{{\pazocal P}_{m_{n}}\}_{n\geq 0}
by applying the construction
3.24
.

Then
(F^{\prime},D^{\prime})\cong(F^{\prime\prime},D^{\prime\prime})
as
l
-diagrams.

Proof.

First of all, note that
(F,D)
and
(F^{\prime},D^{\prime})
make sense by Lemmas
3.27
and
3.11
respectively. Also
(F^{\prime\prime},D^{\prime\prime})
makes sense by Lemma
3.27
since the sequence
(m_{n})_{n\geq 0}
is strictly increasing, and thus
\{{\pazocal P}_{m_{n}}\}_{n\geq 0}
is indeed a
\sigma
-refined sequence of partitions.

Clearly the vertex sets for
(F^{\prime},D^{\prime})
and
(F^{\prime\prime},D^{\prime\prime})
are the same, since
(F^{\prime})^{0,n}=F^{0,m_{n}}={\pazocal P}_{m_{n}}=(F^{\prime\prime})^{0,n}
. We now define a bijective correspondence between
(F^{\prime})^{1,n}=P^{B}_{m_{n},m_{n+1}}\sqcup P^{R}_{m_{n},m_{n+1}}
and
(F^{\prime\prime})^{1,n}=B^{\prime\prime(n)}\sqcup R^{\prime\prime(n)}
.

(1)

Take
e=(e_{m_{n}},\dots,e_{m_{n+1}-1})\in P^{B}_{m_{n},m_{n+1}}
. Put
Z=r(e)=r(e_{m_{n}})\in{\pazocal P}_{m_{n}}
and
Z^{\prime}=s(e)=s(e_{m_{n+1}-1})\in{\pazocal P}_{m_{n+1}}
. By construction, there exists a sequence
(Z_{0},Z_{1},\dots,Z_{m_{n+1}-m_{n}})
of clopen sets, with
Z_{i}\in{\pazocal P}_{m_{n+1}-i}
, satisfying

Z^{\prime}=Z_{0}\subseteq Z_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq Z_{m_{n+1}-m_{n}-1}\subseteq Z_{m_{n+1}-m_{n}}=Z,

and also
s(e_{m_{n+1}-i})=Z_{i-1}
,
r(e_{m_{n+1}-i})=Z_{i}
for each
1\leq i\leq m_{n+1}-m_{n}
. Therefore
Z^{\prime}\subseteq Z
, so
e
corresponds to a
unique
blue edge
\overline{e}\in(F^{\prime\prime})^{1,n}
with source
Z^{\prime}
and range
Z
. This defines an injective map

P_{m_{n},m_{n+1}}^{B}\rightarrow B^{\prime\prime(n)},\quad e\mapsto\overline{e}.

To show bijectivity of this map, take
\overline{e}\in B^{\prime\prime(n)}
with source
Z^{\prime}\in{\pazocal P}_{m_{n+1}}
and range
Z\in{\pazocal P}_{m_{n}}
. Since
{\pazocal P}_{m}
is coarser than
{\pazocal P}_{m+1}
for all
m\geq 0
, there exists a
unique
sequence
(Z_{0},\dots,Z_{m_{n+1}-m_{n}})
of clopen sets, with
Z_{i}\in{\pazocal P}_{m_{n+1}-i}
, such that

Z^{\prime}=Z_{0}\subseteq Z_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq Z_{m_{n+1}-m_{n}-1}\subseteq Z_{m_{n+1}-m_{n}}=Z.

This corresponds to a
unique
blue path
e=(e_{m_{n}},\dots,e_{m_{n+1}-1})
with
r(e_{m_{n+1}-i})=Z_{i}=s(e_{m_{n+1}-i-1})
for all
1\leq i\leq m_{n+1}-m_{n}-1
, and
Z=r(e),Z^{\prime}=s(e)
. Therefore
e\in P^{B}_{m_{n},m_{n+1}}
. It is clear that
e
maps to
\overline{e}
under the above map
P^{B}_{m_{n},m_{n+1}}\rightarrow B^{\prime\prime(n)}
. This proves the desired bijectivity.

(2)

We now deal with the red edges for the even layers, so
n=2j
for
j\geq 0
. Note that in this case
m_{n}
is also even. Take
f=(f_{m_{2j}},\dots,f_{m_{2j+1}-1})\in P^{R}_{m_{2j},m_{2j+1}}
. Put
Z=r(f)=r(f_{m_{2j}})\in{\pazocal P}_{m_{2j}}
and
Z^{\prime}=s(f)=s(f_{m_{2j+1}-1})\in{\pazocal P}_{m_{2j+1}}
. By construction, there exists a sequence
(Z_{0},Z_{1},\dots,Z_{m_{2j+1}-m_{2j}})
of clopen sets, with
Z_{i}\in{\pazocal P}_{m_{2j+1}-i}
, satisfying

Z^{\prime}=Z_{0}\subseteq\sigma(Z_{1})\subseteq Z_{2}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq Z_{m_{2j+1}-m_{2j}-1}\subseteq\sigma(Z_{m_{2j+1}-m_{2j}})=\sigma(Z),

s(f_{m_{2j+1}-i})=Z_{i-1}
and
r(f_{m_{2j+1}-i})=Z_{i}
for each
1\leq i\leq m_{2j+1}-m_{2j}
, and also

Z_{1}\subseteq Z_{3}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq Z_{m_{2j+1}-m_{2j}-2}\subseteq Z_{m_{2j+1}-m_{2j}}=Z.

Therefore
Z^{\prime}\subseteq\sigma(Z)
, so
f
corresponds to a
unique
red edge
\overline{f}\in(F^{\prime\prime})^{1,2j}
with source
Z^{\prime}
and range
Z
. This defines an injective map

P_{m_{2j},m_{2j+1}}^{R}\rightarrow R^{\prime\prime(2j)},\quad f\mapsto\overline{f}.

We now proceed to show its bijectivity. Take
\overline{f}\in R^{\prime\prime(2j)}
with source
Z^{\prime}\in{\pazocal P}_{m_{2j+1}}
and range
Z\in{\pazocal P}_{m_{2j}}
.

Claim
.

There exists a unique sequence
(Z_{0},\dots,Z_{m_{2j+1}-m_{2j}})
of clopen sets
Z_{i}\in{\pazocal P}_{m_{2j+1}-i}
such that

Z^{\prime}=Z_{0}\subseteq\sigma(Z_{1})\subseteq Z_{2}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq Z_{m_{2j+1}-m_{2j}-1}\subseteq\sigma(Z_{m_{2j+1}-m_{2j}})=\sigma(Z)

and

Z_{1}\subseteq Z_{3}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq Z_{m_{2j+1}-m_{2j}-2}\subseteq Z_{m_{2j+1}-m_{2j}}=Z.

Proof.

First we set
Z_{0}:=Z^{\prime}
and
Z_{m_{2j+1}-m_{2j}}:=Z
. Now since the partition
{\pazocal P}_{m}
is coarser than the partition
{\pazocal P}_{m+1}
for all
m\geq 0
, we see that there are unique
Z_{2}\in{\pazocal P}_{m_{2j+1}-2}
,
Z_{4}\in{\pazocal P}_{m_{2j+1}-4},\dots,Z_{m_{2j+1}-m_{2j}-1}\in{\pazocal P}_{m_{2j}+1}
with

Z^{\prime}=Z_{0}\subseteq Z_{2}\subseteq Z_{4}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq Z_{m_{2j+1}-m_{2j}-1}.

Since
Z^{\prime}\subseteq\sigma(Z)
and
{\pazocal P}_{m_{2j}}^{\sigma}
is coarser than
{\pazocal P}_{m_{2j}+1}
, it follows that
Z_{m_{2j+1}-m_{2j}-1}\subseteq\sigma(Z)
. Therefore we have the inclusions

Z_{m_{2j+1}-m_{2j}-3}\subseteq Z_{m_{2j+1}-m_{2j}-1}\subseteq\sigma(Z).

By Lemma
3.26
(ii), there is a unique
Z_{m_{2j+1}-m_{2j}-2}\in{\pazocal P}_{m_{2j}+2}
such that
Z_{m_{2j+1}-m_{2j}-2}\subseteq Z
and

Z_{m_{2j+1}-m_{2j}-3}\subseteq\sigma(Z_{m_{2j+1}-m_{2j}-2})\subseteq Z_{m_{2j+1}-m_{2j}-1}\subseteq\sigma(Z_{m_{2j+1}-m_{2j}})=\sigma(Z).

Iteration of this argument produces the desired sequence
(Z_{0},\dots,Z_{m_{2j+1}-m_{2j}})
. This proves the claim.
∎

The sequence just constructed corresponds to a
unique
red path
f=(f_{m_{2j}},\dots,f_{m_{2j+1}-1})
with
r(f_{m_{2j+1}-i})=Z_{i}=s(f_{m_{2j+1}-i-1})
for all
1\leq i\leq m_{2j+1}-m_{2j}-1
, and
Z=r(f),Z^{\prime}=s(f)
. Therefore
f\in P^{R}_{m_{2j},m_{2j+1}}
. It is clear that
f
maps to
\overline{f}
under the above map
P_{m_{2j},m_{2j+1}}^{R}\rightarrow R^{\prime\prime(2j)}
. Bijectivity now follows.

(3)

Now for the red edges at the odd layers, so
n=2j-1
for
j\geq 1
. Note that in this case
m_{n}
is also odd. Take
f=(f_{m_{2j-1}},\dots,f_{m_{2j}-1})\in P^{R}_{m_{2j-1},m_{2j}}
. Put
Z=r(f)=r(f_{m_{2j-1}})\in{\pazocal P}_{m_{2j-1}}
and
Z^{\prime}=s(f)=s(f_{m_{2j}-1})\in{\pazocal P}_{m_{2j}}
. Take also
g=(g_{m_{2j-2}},\dots,g_{m_{2j-1}-1})\in P^{R}_{m_{2j-2},m_{2j-1}}
to be such that
f\in R^{\prime}(g)
. By construction, there exists a sequence
(Z_{0},Z_{1},\dots,Z_{m_{2j}-m_{2j-1}})
of clopen sets, with
Z_{i}\in{\pazocal P}_{m_{2j}-i}
, satisfying

\sigma(Z^{\prime})=\sigma(Z_{0})\subseteq Z_{1}\subseteq\sigma(Z_{2})\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\sigma(Z_{m_{2j}-m_{2j-1}-1})\subseteq Z_{m_{2j}-m_{2j-1}}=Z,

s(f_{m_{2j}-i})=Z_{i-1}
and
r(f_{m_{2j}-i})=Z_{i}
for each
1\leq i\leq m_{2j}-m_{2j-1}
, and also

Z^{\prime}=Z_{0}\subseteq Z_{2}\subseteq Z_{4}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq Z_{m_{2j}-m_{2j-1}-1}.

Therefore
\sigma(Z^{\prime})\subseteq Z
, so
f
corresponds to a
unique
red edge
\overline{f}\in(F^{\prime\prime})^{1,2j-1}
with source
Z^{\prime}
and range
Z
. This defines an injective map

P_{m_{2j-1},m_{2j}}^{R}\rightarrow R^{\prime\prime(2j-1)},\quad f\mapsto\overline{f}.

Moreover, since
g\in P^{R}_{m_{2j-2},m_{2j-1}}
, there exists a sequence
(\widetilde{Z}_{0},\widetilde{Z}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{Z}_{m_{2j-1}-m_{2j-2}})
of clopen sets
\widetilde{Z}_{i}\in{\pazocal P}_{m_{2j-1}-i}
such that

Z=\widetilde{Z}_{0}\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{Z}_{1})\subseteq\widetilde{Z}_{2}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\widetilde{Z}_{m_{2j-1}-m_{2j-2}-1}\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{Z}_{m_{2j-1}-m_{2j-2}})=\sigma(\widetilde{Z})

and

\widetilde{Z}_{1}\subseteq\widetilde{Z}_{3}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\widetilde{Z}_{m_{2j-1}-m_{2j-2}}=\widetilde{Z},

where
Z=s(g)
,
\widetilde{Z}=r(g)
, and
s(g_{m_{2j-1}-i})=\widetilde{Z}_{i-1}
,
r(g_{m_{2j-1}-i})=\widetilde{Z}_{i}
for each
1\leq i\leq m_{2j-1}-m_{2j-2}
. Since
f\in R^{\prime}(g)
, it follows by the definition of
R^{\prime}(g)
(Definition
3.10
(d)) that
f_{m_{2j-1}}\in R(g_{m_{2j-1}-1})
, and so

Z_{m_{2j}-m_{2j-1}-1}=s(f_{m_{2j-1}})\subseteq r(g_{m_{2j-1}-1})=\widetilde{Z}_{1}.

Combining this with the previous inclusions we obtain

s(\overline{f})=Z^{\prime}=Z_{0}\subseteq Z_{2}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq Z_{m_{2j}-m_{2j-1}-1}\subseteq\widetilde{Z}_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\widetilde{Z}=r(\overline{g}),

where
\overline{g}
is the unique red edge in
(F^{\prime\prime})^{1,2j-2}
constructed from
g
(see (ii) above). This shows that
\overline{f}\in R^{\prime\prime}(\overline{g})
in the
l
-diagram
(F^{\prime\prime},D^{\prime\prime})
.

We now show bijectivity of the map
P_{m_{2j-1},m_{2j}}^{R}\rightarrow R^{\prime\prime(2j-1)}
. Take
\overline{f}\in R^{\prime\prime(2j-1)}
with source
Z^{\prime}\in{\pazocal P}_{m_{2j}}
and range
Z\in{\pazocal P}_{m_{2j-1}}
. Take also
\overline{g}\in R^{\prime\prime(2j-2)}
such that
\overline{f}\in R^{\prime\prime}(\overline{g})
. Since
m_{2j-2}
is even, we know from (ii) above that there is a unique sequence
(\widetilde{Z}_{0},\widetilde{Z}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{Z}_{m_{2j-1}-m_{2j-2}})
of clopen sets
\widetilde{Z}_{i}\in{\pazocal P}_{m_{2j-1}-i}
such that

Z=\widetilde{Z}_{0}\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{Z}_{1})\subseteq\widetilde{Z}_{2}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\widetilde{Z}_{m_{2j-1}-m_{2j-2}-1}\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{Z}_{m_{2j-1}-m_{2j-2}})=\sigma(\widetilde{Z})

and

\widetilde{Z}_{1}\subseteq\widetilde{Z}_{3}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\widetilde{Z}_{m_{2j-1}-m_{2j-2}}=\widetilde{Z},

where
Z=s(\overline{g})
,
\widetilde{Z}=r(\overline{g})
. Let
g\in P^{R}_{m_{2j-2},m_{2j-1}}
be the corresponding red edge.

Claim
.

There exists a unique sequence
(Z_{0},\dots,Z_{m_{2j}-m_{2j-1}})
of clopen sets
Z_{i}\in{\pazocal P}_{m_{2j}-i}
such that

\sigma(Z^{\prime})=\sigma(Z_{0})\subseteq Z_{1}\subseteq\sigma(Z_{2})\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\sigma(Z_{m_{2j}-m_{2j-1}-1})\subseteq Z_{m_{2j}-m_{2j-1}}=Z

and

Z^{\prime}=Z_{0}\subseteq Z_{2}\subseteq Z_{4}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq Z_{m_{2j}-m_{2j-1}-1}\subseteq\widetilde{Z}_{1}.

Proof.

Note that the hypothesis that
\overline{f}\in R^{\prime\prime}(\overline{g})
gives us the condition

Z^{\prime}=s(\overline{f})\subseteq r(\overline{g})=\widetilde{Z}.

Since
\sigma^{-1}({\pazocal P}_{m_{2j}-1})
is coarser than
{\pazocal P}_{m_{2j}}
, there is a unique
Z_{1}\in{\pazocal P}_{m_{2j}-1}
such that
\sigma(Z^{\prime})\subseteq Z_{1}
. We can now find
Z_{3},Z_{5},\dots,Z_{m_{2j}-m_{2j-1}}
such that
Z_{2l+1}\in{\pazocal P}_{m_{2j}-(2l+1)}
and

Z_{1}\subseteq Z_{3}\subseteq Z_{5}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq Z_{m_{2j}-m_{2j-1}}.

Observe that
\sigma(Z^{\prime})\subseteq Z_{1}\subseteq Z_{m_{2j}-m_{2j-1}}
and also
\sigma(Z^{\prime})\subseteq Z
by hypothesis. Since
{\pazocal P}_{m_{2j-1}}
is a partition, we must have
Z_{m_{2j}-m_{2j-1}}=Z
.

Now note that we have inclusions

Z_{m_{2j}-m_{2j-1}-2}\subseteq Z\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{Z}_{1}).

By Lemma
3.26
(ii), we get a unique
Z_{m_{2j}-m_{2j-1}-1}\in{\pazocal P}_{m_{2j-1}+1}
such that
Z_{m_{2j}-m_{2j-1}-1}\subseteq\widetilde{Z}_{1}
and

Z_{m_{2j}-m_{2j-1}-2}\subseteq\sigma(Z_{{m_{2}j}-m_{2j-1}-1})\subseteq Z\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{Z}_{1}).

Continuing in this way, we find unique clopen sets
Z_{2},Z_{4},\dots,Z_{m_{2j}-m_{2j-1}-3}
such that
Z_{2l}\in{\pazocal P}_{m_{2j}-2l}
for
1\leq l\leq\frac{m_{2j}-m_{2j-1}-3}{2}
and

Z_{2}\subseteq Z_{4}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq Z_{m_{2j}-m_{2j-1}-3}\subseteq Z_{m_{2j}-m_{2j-1}-1},

Z_{1}\subseteq\sigma(Z_{2})\subseteq Z_{3}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\sigma(Z_{m_{2j}-m_{2j-1}-1})\subseteq Z\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{Z}_{1}).

We show that
Z^{\prime}\subseteq Z_{2}
. Indeed, we have
\sigma(Z^{\prime})\subseteq Z_{1}\subseteq\sigma(Z_{2})
. Moreover we have that
Z^{\prime}\subseteq\widetilde{Z}
by our hypothesis that
\overline{f}\in R^{\prime\prime}(\overline{g})
, and also we have the inclusions

Z_{2}\subseteq Z_{4}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq Z_{m_{2j}-m_{2j-1}-1}\subseteq\widetilde{Z}_{1}\subseteq\widetilde{Z}_{3}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\widetilde{Z}_{m_{2j-1}-m_{2j-2}}=\widetilde{Z}.

Hence both
Z^{\prime}
and
Z_{2}
are subsets of
\widetilde{Z}
. Since the restriction of
\sigma
to
\widetilde{Z}
is injective and
\sigma(Z^{\prime})\subseteq\sigma(Z_{2})
, we get that
Z^{\prime}\subseteq Z_{2}
. Hence taking
Z_{0}:=Z^{\prime}
we complete our proof of the existence and uniqueness of the sequence
(Z_{0},\dots,Z_{m_{2j}-m_{2j-1}})
, thus proving the claim.
∎

The sequence just constructed corresponds to a
unique
red path
f=(f_{m_{2j-1}},\dots,f_{m_{2j}-1})
with
r(f_{m_{2j}-i})=Z_{i}=s(f_{m_{2j}-i-1})
for all
1\leq i\leq m_{2j}-m_{2j-1}-1
, and
Z=r(f),Z^{\prime}=s(f)
. Therefore
f\in P^{R}_{m_{2j-1},m_{2j}}
, and moreover
f\in R^{\prime}(g)
because
Z_{m_{2j}-m_{2j-1}-1}\subseteq\widetilde{Z}_{1}
. It is clear that
f
maps to
\overline{f}
under the map
P_{m_{2j-1},m_{2j}}^{R}\rightarrow R^{\prime\prime(2j-1)}
.

One can easily check that all the defined bijective maps preserve the separations as in Definition
3.12
(a). The result follows.
∎

Theorem 3.30
.

The equivalence class, under
\sim_{l}
, of the
l
-diagram constructed from
(X,\sigma)
by using the construction
3.24
does not depend on the choice of the
\sigma
-refined sequence of partitions
\{{\pazocal P}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
.

Proof.

Suppose we have two
\sigma
-refined sequences of partitions
\{{\pazocal P}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
,
\{\widetilde{{\pazocal P}}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
. Passing to subsequences (satisfying (CR) and with the first element of the sequence being an even number) does not affect the equivalence class of the resulting
l
-diagram by Proposition
3.29
, so we may assume that

{\pazocal P}_{n}\precsim\widetilde{{\pazocal P}}_{n+1}\precsim{\pazocal P}_{n+2}\quad\text{for all }n\geq 0.

We can create a new sequence of partitions
\{\overline{{\pazocal P}}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
by

\overline{{\pazocal P}}_{n}:=\begin{cases}{\pazocal P}_{3n}&\text{for }n\equiv 0\text{ (mod }3);\\
\widetilde{{\pazocal P}}_{3n}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

This sequence of partitions also satisfies the required properties:

(1)

Since
\overline{{\pazocal P}}_{0}={\pazocal P}_{0}
,
\sigma
restricts to a homeomorphism
\sigma|_{Z}
for each
Z\in\overline{{\pazocal P}}_{0}
.

(2)

For
n\geq 0
, we have

\begin{cases}\overline{{\pazocal P}}_{3n}={\pazocal P}_{9n}\precsim\widetilde{{\pazocal P}}_{9n+1}\precsim\widetilde{{\pazocal P}}_{9n+3}=\overline{{\pazocal P}}_{3n+1},\\
\overline{{\pazocal P}}_{3n+1}=\widetilde{{\pazocal P}}_{9n+3}\precsim\widetilde{{\pazocal P}}_{9n+6}=\overline{{\pazocal P}}_{3n+2},\\
\overline{{\pazocal P}}_{3n+2}=\widetilde{{\pazocal P}}_{9n+6}\precsim{\pazocal P}_{9n+7}\precsim{\pazocal P}_{9n+9}=\overline{{\pazocal P}}_{3n+3}.\end{cases}

(3)

Clearly the union
\bigcup_{n\geq 0}\overline{{\pazocal P}}_{n}
generates the topology of
X
, since both
\bigcup_{n\geq 0}{\pazocal P}_{n}
and
\bigcup_{n\geq 0}\widetilde{{\pazocal P}}_{n}
generate the topology of
X
.

(4)

We have
\text{diam}(\overline{{\pazocal P}}_{n})\leq\text{diam}({\pazocal P}_{3n+1})
, which tends to zero as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

(5)

We compute

\overline{{\pazocal P}}_{2n}\vee\overline{{\pazocal P}}_{2n}^{\sigma}=\begin{cases}{\pazocal P}_{6n}\vee{\pazocal P}_{6n}^{\sigma}\precsim{\pazocal P}_{6n+1}\precsim\widetilde{{\pazocal P}}_{6n+3}=\overline{{\pazocal P}}_{2n+1}&\text{if }2n\equiv 0\text{ (mod }3),\\
\widetilde{{\pazocal P}}_{6n}\vee\widetilde{{\pazocal P}}_{6n}^{\sigma}\precsim\widetilde{{\pazocal P}}_{6n+1}\precsim\widetilde{{\pazocal P}}_{6n+3}=\overline{{\pazocal P}}_{2n+1}&\text{if }2n\equiv 1\text{ (mod }3),\\
\widetilde{{\pazocal P}}_{6n}\vee\widetilde{{\pazocal P}}_{6n}^{\sigma}\precsim\widetilde{{\pazocal P}}_{6n+1}\precsim{\pazocal P}_{6n+3}=\overline{{\pazocal P}}_{2n+1}&\text{if }2n\equiv 2\text{ (mod }3).\end{cases}

In either case

\overline{{\pazocal P}}_{2n}\vee\overline{{\pazocal P}}_{2n}^{\sigma}\precsim\overline{{\pazocal P}}_{2n+1}.

(6)

Also,

\overline{{\pazocal P}}_{2n+1}\vee\sigma^{-1}(\overline{{\pazocal P}}_{2n+1})=\begin{cases}\widetilde{{\pazocal P}}_{6n+3}\vee\sigma^{-1}(\widetilde{{\pazocal P}}_{6n+3})\precsim\widetilde{{\pazocal P}}_{6n+4}\precsim\widetilde{{\pazocal P}}_{6n+6}=\overline{{\pazocal P}}_{2n+2}&\text{if }2n\equiv 0\text{ (mod }3),\\
\widetilde{{\pazocal P}}_{6n+3}\vee\sigma^{-1}(\widetilde{{\pazocal P}}_{6n+3})\precsim\widetilde{{\pazocal P}}_{6n+4}\precsim{\pazocal P}_{6n+6}=\overline{{\pazocal P}}_{2n+2}&\text{if }2n\equiv 1\text{ (mod }3),\\
{\pazocal P}_{6n+3}\vee\sigma^{-1}({\pazocal P}_{6n+3})\precsim{\pazocal P}_{6n+4}\precsim\widetilde{{\pazocal P}}_{6n+6}=\overline{{\pazocal P}}_{2n+2}&\text{if }2n\equiv 2\text{ (mod }3).\end{cases}

In either case

\overline{{\pazocal P}}_{2n+1}\vee\sigma^{-1}(\overline{{\pazocal P}}_{2n+1})\precsim\overline{{\pazocal P}}_{2n+2}.

We let
(F_{1},D_{1}),(F_{2},D_{2})
and
(F,D)
be the
l
-diagrams constructed from the
\sigma
-refined sequences of partitions
\{{\pazocal P}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0},\{\widetilde{{\pazocal P}}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
and
\{\overline{{\pazocal P}}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
, respectively, using the construction given in
3.24
. Consider the two sequences

(m_{n})_{n\geq 0}:=(0,3,6,9,\dots,3n,\dots),\qquad(\widetilde{m}_{n})_{n\geq 0}:=(2,5,\dots,3n+2,\dots),

which clearly satisfy (CR), and
m_{0}\equiv\widetilde{m}_{0}\equiv 0\text{ (mod }2)
. The contraction of
(F,D)
with respect to
(m_{n})_{n\geq 0}
gives an
l
-diagram with associated partitions

{\pazocal P}_{0},{\pazocal P}_{9},{\pazocal P}_{18},{\pazocal P}_{27},\dots,{\pazocal P}_{9n},\dots

so it is itself a contraction of
(F_{1},D_{1})
by Proposition
3.29
. Hence
(F_{1},D_{1})\sim_{l}(F,D)
. Also, the contraction of
(F,D)
with respect to
(\widetilde{m}_{n})_{n\geq 0}
gives an
l
-diagram with associated partitions

\widetilde{{\pazocal P}}_{6},\widetilde{{\pazocal P}}_{15},\widetilde{{\pazocal P}}_{24},\dots,\widetilde{{\pazocal P}}_{9n+6},\dots

so it is itself a contraction of
(F_{2},D_{2})
by Proposition
3.29
again. Hence
(F_{2},D_{2})\sim_{l}(F,D)
.

Altogether we deduce that

(F_{1},D_{1})\sim_{l}(F,D)\sim_{l}(F_{2},D_{2}),

as required.
∎

Using the results obtained so far, we obtain a well-defined map

\widetilde{\Phi}:\textsf{LHomeo}\longrightarrow\textsf{LDiag}/\sim_{l},\quad(X,\sigma)\mapsto[(F,D)]

where
(F,D)
is the
l
-diagram obtained from
(X,\sigma)
by applying the construction
3.24
. Indeed,
\widetilde{\Phi}(X,\sigma)
does not depend on the choice of the
\sigma
-refined sequence of partitions used to define it by Theorem
3.30
.

In the next theorem we prove the last thing left in order to properly define
\Phi
, namely that the equivalence class of
(F,D)
does not depend on the representative chosen for the class of
(X,\sigma)
under the equivalence relation
\sim_{t.c.}
.

Theorem 3.31
.

Suppose we have
(X_{1},\sigma_{1}),(X_{2},\sigma_{2})\in\emph{\textsf{LHomeo}}
which are topologically conjugate through the homeomorphism
h:X_{1}\rightarrow X_{2}
. Then
\widetilde{\Phi}(X_{1},\sigma_{1})=\widetilde{\Phi}(X_{2},\sigma_{2})
.

Proof.

First, notice that
N(\sigma_{1})=N(\sigma_{2})=:N
since
h
is a homeomorphism. Also,
h({\pazocal U}^{(1)}_{k})={\pazocal U}^{(2)}_{k}
for each
1\leq k\leq N
, being

{\pazocal U}^{(i)}_{k}=\{x\in X_{i}\mid|\sigma^{-1}_{i}(\{x\})|=k\}.

Take
\{{\pazocal P}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
a
\sigma_{1}
-refined sequence of partitions of
X_{1}
, and
\{{\pazocal P}^{\prime}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
a
\sigma_{2}
-refined sequence of partitions of
X_{2}
. Let
(F_{i},D_{i})
denote the
l
-diagram constructed from
(X_{i},\sigma_{i})
using the construction
3.24
, for
i=1,2
. We define, for
n\geq 0
, new partitions

{\pazocal P}^{(1)}_{n}:={\pazocal P}_{n}\vee h^{-1}({\pazocal P}^{\prime}_{n})\text{ for $X_{1}$, and }\text{ }{\pazocal P}^{(2)}_{n}:=h({\pazocal P}_{n})\vee{\pazocal P}^{\prime}_{n}\text{ for $X_{2}$.}

Then the sequence
\{{\pazocal P}^{(i)}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
is a
\sigma_{i}
-refined sequence of partitions of
X_{i}
, for
i=1,2
:

(1)

Since
\sigma_{1}
(resp.
\sigma_{2}
) restricts to a homeomorphism over each
Z\in{\pazocal P}_{0}
(resp.
Z\in{\pazocal P}^{\prime}_{0}
), it is clear that each
\sigma_{i}
restricts to a homeomorphism
\sigma_{i}|_{Z}
, for
Z\in{\pazocal P}^{(i)}_{0}
.

(2)

{\pazocal P}^{(1)}_{n}\precsim{\pazocal P}^{(1)}_{n+1}
and
{\pazocal P}^{(2)}_{n}\precsim{\pazocal P}^{(2)}_{n+1}
, since
{\pazocal P}_{n}\precsim{\pazocal P}_{n+1}
and
{\pazocal P}^{\prime}_{n}\precsim{\pazocal P}^{\prime}_{n+1}
.

(3)

Their unions generate the topology of
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
, respectively, since this property holds true for the partitions
\{{\pazocal P}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
of
X_{1}
and
\{{\pazocal P}_{n}^{\prime}\}_{n\geq 0}
of
X_{2}
.

(4)

\text{diam}({\pazocal P}_{n}^{(1)})\leq\text{diam}({\pazocal P}_{n})
and
\text{diam}({\pazocal P}_{n}^{(2)})\leq\text{diam}({\pazocal P}_{n}^{\prime})
, so they both tend to zero as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

(5)

First, one may notice that
h({\pazocal P}^{\sigma_{1}})=h({\pazocal P})^{\sigma_{2}}
for any
\sigma_{1}
-refined partition
{\pazocal P}
of
X_{1}
. This follows from the fact that
h({\pazocal U}^{(1)}_{k})={\pazocal U}^{(2)}_{k}
. Now, for
X_{1}
and
n\geq 0
, we compute

\displaystyle{\pazocal P}^{(1)}_{2n}\vee({\pazocal P}^{(1)}_{2n})^{\sigma_{1}}

\displaystyle={\pazocal P}_{2n}\vee h^{-1}({\pazocal P}^{\prime}_{2n})\vee{\pazocal P}_{2n}^{\sigma_{1}}\vee(h^{-1}({\pazocal P}^{\prime}_{2n}))^{\sigma_{1}}

\displaystyle={\pazocal P}_{2n}\vee{\pazocal P}_{2n}^{\sigma_{1}}\vee h^{-1}({\pazocal P}^{\prime}_{2n})\vee h^{-1}\big(({\pazocal P}^{\prime}_{2n})^{\sigma_{2}}\big)

\displaystyle=\big({\pazocal P}_{2n}\vee{\pazocal P}_{2n}^{\sigma_{1}}\big)\vee h^{-1}\big({\pazocal P}^{\prime}_{2n}\vee({\pazocal P}^{\prime}_{2n})^{\sigma_{2}}\big)

\displaystyle\precsim{\pazocal P}_{2n+1}\vee h^{-1}({\pazocal P}^{\prime}_{2n+1})={\pazocal P}^{(1)}_{2n+1}

and

\displaystyle{\pazocal P}^{(1)}_{2n+1}\vee\sigma_{1}^{-1}({\pazocal P}^{(1)}_{2n+1})

\displaystyle={\pazocal P}_{2n+1}\vee h^{-1}({\pazocal P}^{\prime}_{2n+1})\vee\sigma_{1}^{-1}({\pazocal P}_{2n+1})\vee\sigma_{1}^{-1}(h^{-1}({\pazocal P}^{\prime}_{2n+1}))

\displaystyle=\big({\pazocal P}_{2n+1}\vee\sigma_{1}^{-1}({\pazocal P}_{2n+1})\big)\vee h^{-1}\big({\pazocal P}^{\prime}_{2n+1}\vee\sigma_{2}^{-1}({\pazocal P}^{\prime}_{2n+1})\big)

\displaystyle\precsim{\pazocal P}_{2n+2}\vee h^{-1}({\pazocal P}^{\prime}_{2n+2})={\pazocal P}^{(1)}_{2n+2}.

Similar computations hold for
X_{2}
, that is, for
n\geq 0
, we have

{\pazocal P}^{(2)}_{2n}\vee({\pazocal P}^{(2)}_{2n})^{\sigma_{2}}\precsim{\pazocal P}^{(2)}_{2n+1}\quad\text{ and }\quad{\pazocal P}^{(2)}_{2n+1}\vee\sigma_{2}^{-1}({\pazocal P}^{(2)}_{2n+1})={\pazocal P}^{(2)}_{2n+2}.

Note that
h({\pazocal P}^{(1)}_{n})=h({\pazocal P}_{n}\vee h^{-1}({\pazocal P}^{\prime}_{n}))=h({\pazocal P}_{n})\vee{\pazocal P}^{\prime}_{n}={\pazocal P}^{(2)}_{n}
. Therefore the vertices of
(F_{1},D_{1})
are in bijective correspondence with the vertices of
(F_{2},D_{2})
at each layer
n\geq 0
. To conclude the theorem, we need to show that the edges
F_{1}^{1,n}
are in bijective correspondence with
F_{2}^{1,n}
for all
n\geq 0
, also preserving the separations as in Definition
3.12
(a). These follow directly from the following considerations:

(1)

For
Z\in{\pazocal P}^{(1)}_{2n}
and
Z^{\prime}\in{\pazocal P}^{(1)}_{2n+1}
,
Z^{\prime}\subseteq Z
if and only if
h(Z^{\prime})\subseteq h(Z)
. Also
Z^{\prime}\subseteq\sigma_{1}(Z)
if and only if
h(Z^{\prime})\subseteq h(\sigma_{1}(Z))=\sigma_{2}(h(Z))
.

(2)

For
Z^{\prime}\in{\pazocal P}^{(1)}_{2n+1}
and
Z^{\prime\prime}\in{\pazocal P}^{(1)}_{2n+2}
,
Z^{\prime\prime}\subseteq Z^{\prime}
if and only if
h(Z^{\prime\prime})\subseteq h(Z^{\prime})
. Also
\sigma_{1}(Z^{\prime\prime})\subseteq Z^{\prime}
if and only if
\sigma_{2}(h(Z^{\prime\prime}))=h(\sigma_{1}(Z^{\prime\prime}))\subseteq h(Z^{\prime})
.

This concludes the proof of the theorem.
∎

We finally obtain the desired map
\Phi
.

Theorem 3.32
.

There exists a well-defined map

\Phi:\emph{\textsf{LHomeo}}/\sim_{t.c.}\longrightarrow\emph{\textsf{LDiag}}/\sim_{l},\quad[(X,\sigma)]\mapsto[(F,D)],

where
(F,D)
is the
l
-diagram obtained from
(X,\sigma)
by applying the construction
3.24
.

Proof.

This follows directly from the definition of
\widetilde{\Phi}
and Theorem
3.31
.
∎

3.2.
From
LDiag
to
LHomeo

We now describe how to construct a surjective local homeomorphism
(X,\sigma)
from an
l
-diagram
(F,D)
.

Let
(F,D)\in\textsf{LDiag}
. We first construct the space
X
. It will be given by the set of infinite paths

X=\{(e_{0},e_{1},e_{2},\dots)\mid e_{i}\in B_{r(e_{i})},r(e_{i+1})=s(e_{i})\text{ for all }i\geq 0\}.

Note that all edges are blue. The topology on
X
is the one with basis consisting of the cylinder sets

Z(e_{0},e_{1},\dots,e_{n})=\{(x_{i})_{i\geq 0}\in X\mid x_{0}=e_{0},\dots,x_{n}=e_{n}\},

for
(e_{0},\dots,e_{n})\in P_{0,n}^{B}
. This is indeed a basis for a topology because the intersection of two cylinder sets is either empty or again a cylinder set.

Lemma 3.33
.

The space
X
is a totally disconnected, compact metrizable space.

Proof.

The decompositions

X=\bigsqcup_{(e_{0},\dots,e_{n})\in P_{0,n}^{B}}Z(e_{0},\dots,e_{n}),

for any
n\geq 0
, imply that each cylinder set is a clopen set. Therefore
X
is totally disconnected. It is clearly Hausdorff, second countable and regular, hence by Urysohn’s Metrization Theorem,
X
becomes metrizable.

We can regard
X
as a topological subspace of
\prod_{n\geq 0}F^{1,n}
endowed with the product topology, where each set
F^{1,n}
is endowed with the discrete topology. This space is compact by Tychonoff’s Theorem, and
X
is a closed subspace of it. Therefore
X
is compact.
∎

We now aim to define a natural surjective local homeomorphism
\sigma:X\rightarrow X
, as follows.

Construction 3.34
.

Let
(e_{0},e_{1},e_{2},\dots)\in X
. For each
j\geq 0
we may complete the pair of blue edges
(e_{2j},e_{2j+1})
to a romb
(e_{2j},e_{2j+1},f_{2j},f_{2j+1})
using part (ii) of Proposition
3.3
, so that
r(f_{2j})=r(e_{2j})
,
s(f_{2j})=r(f_{2j+1})
,
s(f_{2j+1})=s(e_{2j+1})
and
f_{2j+1}\in R(f_{2j})
. Note that
s(f_{2j-1})=r(f_{2j})
for each
j\geq 1
. So, for
j\geq 1
, we may consider the red pair
(f_{2j-1},f_{2j})
and complete it to another romb
(e_{2j-1}^{\prime},e_{2j}^{\prime},f_{2j-1},f_{2j})
by using condition (f) in Definition
3.2
. We obtain a well-defined blue path

(e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime},\dots)\in X

starting at
v:=r(e_{1}^{\prime})\in F^{0,1}
. Now, part (1) of condition (d) in Definition
3.2
entails that there is
w\in F^{0,0}
and
e_{0}^{\prime}\in B_{w}
such that
s(e_{0}^{\prime})=v=r(e_{1}^{\prime})
. Also, by the last part of this same condition (d) the edge
e_{0}^{\prime}
is unique with this property. Thus

(e_{0}^{\prime},e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime},\dots)\in X.

We define
\sigma\colon X\to X
by

\sigma(e_{0},e_{1},e_{2},\dots)=(e^{\prime}_{0},e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime},\dots).

e_{0}
e_{1}
e_{2}
e_{3}
e_{4}
e_{5}
f_{0}
f_{1}
f_{2}
f_{3}
f_{4}
f_{5}
e_{0}^{\prime}
e_{1}^{\prime}
e_{2}^{\prime}
e_{3}^{\prime}
e_{4}^{\prime}
e_{5}^{\prime}
v
w

Figure 5
.
Construction of the map
\sigma
.

Lemma 3.35
.

The map
\sigma
is a surjective local homeomorphism.

Proof.

We first show that
\sigma
is continuous. Let
x=(e_{0},e_{1},e_{2},\dots)\in X
, with
e_{i}\in B_{r(e_{i})}
for all
i\geq 0
. Let
y=\sigma(x)=(e^{\prime}_{0},e^{\prime}_{1},e^{\prime}_{2},\dots)
and consider an open neighborhood
Z(e_{0}^{\prime},e^{\prime}_{1},e^{\prime}_{2},\dots,e^{\prime}_{2n})
of
y
. Then we have

\sigma(Z(e_{0},e_{1},\dots,e_{2n+1}))\subseteq Z(e^{\prime}_{0},e^{\prime}_{1},e^{\prime}_{2},\dots,e^{\prime}_{2n}),

and
Z(e_{0},e_{1},\dots,e_{2n+1})
is an open neighborhood of
x
. This shows continuity of
\sigma
.

We now show that
\sigma
is surjective. Indeed we will show that there are a finite number of pre-images of each element
y\in X
.

Let
y=(e_{0}^{\prime},e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime},\dots)\in X
, with
e_{i}^{\prime}\in B_{r(e_{i}^{\prime})}
for all
i\geq 0
. By part (2) of condition (d) in Definition
3.2
, we can choose a red edge
f^{\prime}_{0}
such that
s(f^{\prime}_{0})=s(e_{0}^{\prime})
. Note that existence of such
f^{\prime}_{0}
is ensured, but certainly not uniqueness, although we do have a finite number of possibilities for
f^{\prime}_{0}
. As we show now, this is the only choice we need.

By recursively applying property (g) in Definition
3.2
, we construct a sequence of red edges
f^{\prime}_{1},f^{\prime}_{2},\dots
such that
f^{\prime}_{2j+1}\in R(f^{\prime}_{2j})
and
(e^{\prime}_{2j+1},e^{\prime}_{2j+2},f^{\prime}_{2j+1},f^{\prime}_{2j+2})
is a romb for all
j\geq 0
. This can be done since each
s(f^{\prime}_{2j})\in F^{0,2j+1}
. Note that the sequence
f^{\prime}_{1},f^{\prime}_{2},\dots
is uniquely determined by
y
and by
f^{\prime}_{0}
. Finally we use part (ii) of Proposition
3.3
to build pairs of blue edges
(e_{2j},e_{2j+1})
such that
(e_{2j},e_{2j+1},f^{\prime}_{2j},f^{\prime}_{2j+1})
is a romb, for all
j\geq 0
. Then
x=(e_{0},e_{1},e_{2},\dots)\in X
is an element such that
\sigma(x)=y
, as required. This shows in particular that
\sigma
is surjective.

Conversely, if
\sigma(x)=y
, with
x=(e_{0},e_{1},e_{2},\dots)
, and if
(f_{0},f_{1})
is the unique pair of red edges such that
f_{1}\in R(f_{0})
and
(e_{0},e_{1},f_{0},f_{1})
is a romb, then
x
is the unique pre-image of
y
determined by the choice of
f_{0}
as the red edge
f
such that
s(f)=s(e_{0}^{\prime})
. This means that there exist exactly
|s^{-1}(s(e_{0}^{\prime}))|-1
pre-images of
y
. Observe also that this implies that
\sigma
is injective when restricted to
Z(e_{0},e_{1})
, because
f_{0}
is determined by
(e_{0},e_{1})
.

To show that
\sigma
is a local homeomorphism, it suffices to show that
\sigma
is an open map, since we already know that
\sigma
is continuous and locally injective. To show that
\sigma
is an open map, it suffices to prove that for any blue path
(e_{0},e_{1},\dots,e_{2n-1})\in P^{B}_{0,2n}
, with
n\geq 1
, we have that
\sigma(Z(e_{0},e_{1},\dots,e_{2n-1}))
is an open subset of
X
. Now, if

y=(e_{0}^{\prime},e_{1}^{\prime},\dots,e^{\prime}_{2n},e^{\prime}_{2n+1},\dots)=\sigma(x)
for some
x\in Z(e_{0},e_{1},\dots e_{2n-1})
, then following the proof of surjectivity given above we can easily see that

Z(e^{\prime}_{0},\dots,e^{\prime}_{2n})\subseteq\sigma(Z(e_{0},e_{1},\dots,e_{2n-1})).

This shows that
\sigma
is an open map, hence it is a local homeomorphism.
∎

Using the above construction, we obtain a well-defined map

\widetilde{\Psi}:\textsf{LDiag}\longrightarrow\textsf{LHomeo}/\sim_{t.c.},\quad(F,D)\mapsto[(X,\sigma)]

where
(X,\sigma)
is the dynamical system obtained from
(F,D)
by applying the construction
3.34
. In the next theorem we show that the equivalence class of
(X,\sigma)
does not depend on the representative chosen for the class
(F,D)
under the equivalence relation
\sim_{l}
.

Theorem 3.36
.

Let
(F_{1},D_{1}),(F_{2},D_{2})\in\emph{\textsf{LDiag}}
be two equivalent
l
-diagrams under
\sim_{l}
. Write
(X_{i},\sigma_{i})
for the dynamical system obtained from
(F_{i},D_{i})
by applying the construction
3.34
, for
i=1,2
. Then there exists a homeomorphism
h:X_{1}\rightarrow X_{2}
such that
h\circ\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}\circ h
.

Proof.

It suffices to consider the case where
(F_{2},D_{2})
is a contraction of
(F_{1},D_{1})
with respect to a sequence
(m_{n})_{n\geq 0}
in
{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{0\}
with
0\leq m_{0}<m_{1}<\cdots
, satisfying (CR) and with
m_{0}
even. We write
(F,D):=(F_{1},D_{1}),(F^{\prime},D^{\prime}):=(F_{2},D_{2})
, and
(X,\sigma):=(X_{1},\sigma_{1}),(X^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}):=(X_{2},\sigma_{2})
.

We have

\displaystyle X=\{(e_{0},e_{1},\dots)\mid e_{i}\in F^{1,i}\text{ blue edges, }r(e_{i+1})=s(e_{i})\text{ for all }i\geq 0\},

\displaystyle X^{\prime}=\{((e_{m_{0}},\dots,e_{m_{1}-1}),(e_{m_{1}},\dots,e_{m_{2}-1}),\dots)\mid(e_{m_{0}},...,e_{m_{1}},...,e_{m_{2}},...)\in X\}.

There is an obvious identification
X\cong X^{\prime}
, which is in fact a homeomorphism (it sends cylinder sets to cylinder sets). It clearly gives our desired conjugacy
h
.
∎

We summarize our construction in the following theorem.

Theorem 3.37
.

There exists a well-defined map

\Psi:\emph{\textsf{LDiag}}/\sim_{l}\longrightarrow\emph{\textsf{LHomeo}}/\sim_{t.c.},\quad[(F,D)]\mapsto[(X,\sigma)],

where
(X,\sigma)\in\emph{\textsf{LHomeo}}
is the dynamical system obtained from
(F,D)
by applying the construction
3.34
.

Proof.

This follows directly from the definition of
\widetilde{\Psi}
and Theorem
3.36
.
∎

3.3.
Equivalence between both constructions

In this section we prove that both constructions
3.24
and
3.34
are inverses of each other, modulo the stated equivalences in
LHomeo
and
LDiag
. First, a technical lemma. Recall Definition
3.25
for the notion of a refined
l
-diagram.

Lemma 3.38
.

Let
(F,D)
be an
l
-diagram. Then
(F,D)
is a refined
l
-diagram if and only if
\sigma|_{Z(v)}
is injective for all
v\in F^{0,0}
. Here
Z(v)
is defined naturally as the set of those elements
(e_{0},e_{1},e_{2},\dots)\in X
such that
r(e_{0})=v
.

Proof.

By Remark
3.5
(5) it is enough to show that
\sigma|_{Z(v)}
is injective for all
v\in F^{0,0}
if and only if there are no double red edges from
F^{0,1}
to
F^{0,0}
, that is, given
w\in F^{0,1}
and
v\in F^{0,0}
, there is at most one red edge
f
such that
s(f)=w
and
r(f)=v
.

Suppose first that there are no double red edges from a vertex in
F^{0,1}
to a vertex in
F^{0,0}
. Let
y=(e_{0}^{\prime},e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime},\dots)\in X
, where
e_{i}^{\prime}\in B_{r(e_{i}^{\prime})}
for all
i\geq 0
. By the proof of Lemma
3.35
, the set of pre-images of
y
by
\sigma
is in bijective correspondence with the set of red edges
f_{0}
such that
s(f_{0})=s(e_{0}^{\prime})
. Hence there is at most one element
x\in Z(v)
such that
\sigma(x)=y
, for each
v\in F^{0,0}
.

Conversely, if there are two red edges
f_{0},f_{0}^{\prime}
such that
s(f_{0})=s(f_{0}^{\prime})=w\in F^{0,1}
and
r(f_{0})=r(f_{0}^{\prime})=v\in F^{0,0}
, then any point
y=(e_{0}^{\prime},e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime},\dots)\in X
such that
s(e_{0}^{\prime})=w
has at least two pre-images in
