is determined by a sequence
(e_{0},e_{1},e_{2},\dots)
in the Bratteli diagram
(F,D)
. Let
(e_{2j},e_{2j+1},f_{2j},f_{2j+1})
be the rombs associated to the pairs
(e_{2j},e_{2j+1})
of blue edges, for each
j\geq 0
. By definition of
\sigma
(Construction
3.34
), we have that
\sigma(\xi)=\xi^{\prime}
, with
\xi^{\prime}=(a_{1},e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime},\dots)
, where
(e_{2j-1}^{\prime},e_{2j}^{\prime},f_{2j-1},f_{2j})
are the rombs associated to the pairs
(f_{2j-1},f_{2j})
of red edges, for
j\geq 1
, and
a_{1}
is the unique blue edge such that
s(a_{1})=r(e_{1}^{\prime})=r(f_{1})=s(f_{0})
.

Note that, for
j\geq 1
, the edges
e_{2j-1}
and
e_{2j}
correspond to the edges

[\xi^{2j-1}\overset{e_{0}}{\longleftarrow}(e_{0}^{-1}.\,\xi)^{2j-1}]\quad\text{and}\quad[(e_{0}^{-1}.\,\xi)^{2j}\overset{e_{0}^{-1}}{\longleftarrow}\xi^{2j}]

in
(\theta^{(E,C)})^{[2j-1]}
and
(\theta^{(E,C)})^{[2j]}
under the isomorphisms
\Phi_{2j-1}
and
\Phi_{2j}
, respectively. Similarly, the edges
f_{2j-1}
and
f_{2j}
correspond to the edges

[\xi^{2j-1}\overset{f_{0}}{\longleftarrow}(f_{0}^{-1}.\,\xi)^{2j-1}]\quad\text{and}\quad[(f_{0}^{-1}.\,\xi)^{2j}\overset{f_{0}^{-1}}{\longleftarrow}\xi^{2j}]

in
(\theta^{(E,C)})^{[2j-1]}
and
(\theta^{(E,C)})^{[2j]}
under the isomorphisms
\Phi_{2j-1}
and
\Phi_{2j}
, respectively. It follows that the edges
e^{\prime}_{2j-1}
and
e^{\prime}_{2j}
correspond to the edges

[(a_{1}f_{0}^{-1}.\,\xi)^{2j-1}\overset{a_{1}}{\longleftarrow}(f_{0}^{-1}.\,\xi)^{2j-1}]\quad\text{and}\quad[(f_{0}^{-1}.\,\xi)^{2j}\overset{a_{1}^{-1}}{\longleftarrow}(a_{1}f_{0}^{-1}.\,\xi)^{2j}]

in
(\theta^{(E,C)})^{[2j-1]}
and
(\theta^{(E,C)})^{[2j]}
, respectively. Therefore
\xi^{\prime}=(a_{1},e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime},\dots)
corresponds to
(a_{1}f_{0}^{-1}).\xi=\theta_{a_{1}f_{0}^{-1}}(\xi)
, that is,
\sigma(\xi)=\theta_{a_{1}f_{0}^{-1}}(\xi)
.
∎

With the help of the above proposition, we can give a more concrete form of a generalized finite shift.

Construction 5.19
.

Let
(E,C)
be a generalized finite shift graph, and set
R=\bigsqcup_{v\in E^{0,0}}R_{v}
. For each
f\in R
, consider a symbol
a_{f}
, and form the alphabet
A=\{a_{f}\mid f\in R\}
. Moreover, let
s,r\colon A\to E^{0,0}
be the maps defined by
s(a_{f})=r(f)
and
r(a_{f})=r(e)
, where
e
is the unique blue edge in
E^{1}
such that
s(e)=s(f)
. Let
\mathfrak{X}
be the space of configurations
\xi\subseteq\mathbb{F}(A)
on the free group
\mathbb{F}(A)
on
A
such that

(1)

1\in\xi
;

(2)

\xi
is right convex;

(3)

for each
\alpha\in\xi
, the local configuration of
\xi
at
\alpha
is of the following form: there exists a vertex
v\in E^{0,0}
such that

(\alpha.\xi)^{1}=\{1\}\cup\{a_{f}\}\cup\{a_{f_{i}}^{-1}\mid 1\leq i\leq n\},

where
f\in R_{v}
and there is a blue edge
e\in B_{v}
such that
\{f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\}
is the set of all red edges
g\in R
such that
s(g)=s(e)
.

The local homeomorphism
\tau
on
\mathfrak{X}
is defined by

\tau(\xi)=\xi\cdot a_{f}^{-1},

where
f
is the unique red edge such that
a_{f}\in\xi^{1}
.

Observe that any configuration
\xi\in\mathfrak{X}
contains only elements of the form

a_{f_{1}}^{-1}\cdots a_{f_{m}}^{-1}a_{g_{n}}\cdots a_{g_{1}}

for
n,m\geq 0
, where
f_{i},g_{j}\in R
,
f_{m}\neq g_{n}
,
s(f_{m})=s(g_{n})
,
r(g_{j+1})=r(e_{j})
, where
e_{j}
is the unique blue edge such that
s(e_{j})=s(g_{j})
for
1\leq j\leq n-1
, and
r(f_{i+1})=r(e_{i}^{\prime})
, where
e_{i}^{\prime}
is the unique blue edge such that
s(e_{i}^{\prime})=s(f_{i})
for
1\leq i\leq m-1
.

Note that, for
\xi\in\mathfrak{X}
, and with
\{f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\}
the red edges of
E
such that
a_{f_{i}}^{-1}\in\xi^{1}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
, we have
\xi\cdot a_{f_{i}}\in\mathfrak{X}
, and
\tau(\xi\cdot a_{f_{i}})=\xi
. Hence
\tau
is surjective, but not necessarily injective.

Proposition 5.20
.

Let
(E,C)
be a generalized finite shift graph. Then the dynamical systems
(X,\sigma)
and
(\mathfrak{X},\tau)
are topologically conjugate.

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
5.18
. Indeed, if
\xi\in\Omega(E,C)
is a configuration whose local configuration
\xi_{1}
at
1
is of the form (c.2), then replacing the arrows
ef^{-1}
, where
f
is the unique red edge in
E^{1}
such that
f^{-1}\in(\alpha\,.\,\xi)^{1}
, and
e
is the unique blue edge such that
s(e)=s(f)
, by
a_{f}
, for each
\alpha\in\xi
of even length, we obtain a configuration in
\mathfrak{X}
. And clearly, the process can be reversed. This also gives the desired intertwining between
\sigma
and
\tau
.
∎

We now consider a concrete example of a generalized finite shift which is neither a one-sided nor a two-sided shift.

Example 5.21
.

Let
(E,C)
be the generalized finite shift graph with associated red-blue adjacency matrices

A=\begin{pmatrix}2\\
1\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad I=\begin{pmatrix}1\\

Concretely, we set
E^{0,0}=\{v\}
,
E^{0,1}=\{w_{1},w_{2}\}
,
C_{v}=\{B_{v},R_{v}\}
, where
B_{v}=\{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}\}
,
R_{v}=\{\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\}
, with all edges having range
v
, and with
s(\alpha_{0})=s(\beta_{0})=s(\beta_{1})=w_{1}
,
s(\alpha_{1})=s(\beta_{2})=w_{2}
.

.
Description of the shift map

. Here the configuration

, which has local configuration at

a=\alpha_{0}\beta_{0}^{-1}
,

,
c=\alpha_{1}\beta_{2}^{-1}
, we can further describe the generalized finite shift
(X,\sigma)
associated with
(E,C)
as follows.

Let
\mathbb{F}
be the free group on
\{a,b,c\}
. Then
X
is identified as the set
\mathfrak{X}
of all configurations
\xi\subseteq\mathbb{F}
satisfying the conditions (a)–(c) in Construction
5.19
. In particular, the local configuration of
\xi
at each point is either of the form
\{z,a^{-1},b^{-1}\}
or of the form
\{z,c^{-1}\}
, where
z\in\{a,b,c\}
. The local homeomorphism
\sigma
is identified with
\tau
, which consists in shifting one step in the direction pointed by the directed edges.

\tau(\xi)

Figure 12
.
Description of the shift map
\tau
on
\mathfrak{X}
. Here the configuration
\xi
is given by
\xi=\{1,b,a^{-1},b^{-1},ab,a^{-1}b,a^{-2},b^{-1}a^{-1},b^{-2},a^{-1}b^{-1},\dots\}
, which has local configuration at
1
given by
\{b,a^{-1},b^{-1}\}
. The shifted configuration
\sigma(\xi)
has local configuration at
1
given by
\{a,a^{-1},b^{-1}\}
. The picture conveys the same idea of a river basin present for instance in
[
30
, p. 5041]
. From any vertex there is only one way forward, and this fact enables us to define the shift map.

We now establish the universal property of the dynamical system
(X,\sigma)
associated to a generalized finite shift graph
(E,C)
. Given such a graph, we set
B:=\bigsqcup_{v\in E^{0,0}}B_{v}
and
R:=\bigsqcup_{v\in E^{0,0}}R_{v}
.

Definition 5.22
.

Let
(E,C)
be a generalized finite shift graph with red adjacency matrix
A=(a_{e,v})\in M_{B\times E^{0,0}}({\mathbb{Z}}^{+})
, where as usual
a_{e,v}
is the number of red edges from
s(e)
to
v
. Let
(Y,\rho)
be an object from
LHomeo
. An
(E,C)
-structure
on
(Y,\rho)
is given by a
\rho
-cylinder decomposition

{\pazocal Z}=(\{Z_{e}\mid e\in B\},\{W_{e,v}^{(k)}\mid e\in B,v\in E^{0,0},1\leq k\leq a_{e,v}\}).

That is, we have

Y=\bigsqcup_{e\in B}Z_{e},\qquad W_{v}:=\bigsqcup_{e\in B_{v}}Z_{e}=\bigsqcup_{e\in B}\bigsqcup_{k=1}^{a_{e,v}}W_{e,v}^{(k)},

\rho|_{W_{e,v}^{(k)}}
is injective, and
\rho(W_{e,v}^{(k)})=Z_{e}
for all allowable indices
e,v,k
.

We say that an
(E,C)
-structure
{\pazocal Z}=(\{Z_{e}\},\{W_{e,v}^{(k)}\})
on
(Y,\rho)
is
universal
if for any other
(E,C)
-structure
\tilde{{\pazocal Z}}=(\{\tilde{Z}_{e}\},\{\tilde{W}_{e,v}^{(k)}\})
on
(\tilde{Y},\tilde{\rho})\in\textsf{LHomeo}
, there is a unique
(E,C)
-equivariant continuous map
\psi\colon\tilde{Y}\to Y
. Here
(E,C)
-equivariant means that
\psi(\tilde{Z}_{e})\subseteq Z_{e}
and
\psi(\tilde{W}_{e,v}^{(k)})\subseteq W_{e,v}^{(k)}
for all allowable indices
e,v,k
, and that
\psi\circ\tilde{\rho}=\rho\circ\psi
.

By Propositions
5.11
and
5.8
, every generalized finite shift
(X,\sigma)
, associated to a generalized finite shift graph
(E,C)
, has a natural
(E,C)
-structure. It turns out that this structure is universal.

Theorem 5.23
.

Let
(X,\sigma)
be the generalized finite shift associated to the generalized finite shift graph
(E,C)
. Then the natural
(E,C)
-structure on
X
is universal.

Proof.

The result follows from the universality of the
(E,C)
-dynamical system
\Omega(E,C)
(
[
10
, Corollary 6.11]
). We thus recall the definition of this dynamical system. We will identify
X
as a subspace of
\Omega(E,C)
throughout the proof, as in Proposition
5.18
.

The
(E,C)
-dynamical system on
\Omega(E,C)
consists of the collection of open compact subsets
\Omega(E,C)_{v}
, for
v\in E^{0}
, and the open compact subsets
H_{e}
and
H_{f}
, for
e\in B
and
f\in R
, where

H_{e}=\{\xi\in\Omega(E,C)\mid e^{-1}\in\xi_{1}\},\qquad H_{f}=\{\xi\in\Omega(E,C)\mid f^{-1}\in\xi_{1}\},

together with the homeomorphisms
\theta_{e}\colon\Omega(E,C)_{s(e)}\to H_{e}
and
\theta_{f}\colon\Omega(E,C)_{s(f)}\to H_{f}
, which we defined just after Definition
5.16
. Note that for each
v\in E^{0,0}
, we have
\Omega(E,C)_{v}=\bigsqcup_{e\in B_{v}}H_{e}=\bigsqcup_{f\in R_{v}}H_{f}\subseteq X
.

Let us also identify the natural
(E,C)
-structure on
(X,\sigma)
, adopting the picture of
(X,\sigma)
from Proposition
5.18
. We have that the space corresponding to
e\in B
is just
H_{e}=Z(e)
, and the space corresponding to
f\in R
is just
H_{f}
. Of course we have
X=\bigsqcup_{v\in E^{0,0}}\Omega(E,C)_{v}
and
\Omega(E,C)_{v}=\bigsqcup_{e\in B_{v}}H_{e}
for each
v\in E^{0,0}
. We also have

\Omega(E,C)_{v}=\bigsqcup_{f\in R_{v}}H_{f}=\bigsqcup_{e\in B}\bigsqcup_{k=1}^{a_{e,v}}H_{e,v}^{(k)},

where
H_{e,v}^{(k)}:=H_{f_{e,v}^{(k)}}
, since for each
f\in R_{v}
there is a unique
e\in B
such that
s(e)=s(f)
, and then there is a unique
1\leq k\leq a_{e,v}
such that
f=f_{e,v}^{(k)}
. By the proof of Proposition
5.18
, the space
H_{e,v}^{(k)}
agrees with the space
V_{e,v}^{(k)}
defined just before Proposition
5.5
, namely the space of all
x=(e_{0},e_{1},\dots)\in X
such that the romb corresponding to the pair of blue edges
(e_{0},e_{1})
is of the form
(e_{0},e_{1},f_{0},f_{1})
with
f_{0}=f_{e,v}^{(k)}
. Hence, the
(E,C)
-structure
{\pazocal H}:=(\{H_{e}\},\{H_{e,v}^{(k)}\})
on
(X,\sigma)
agrees with the previously defined
\sigma
-cylinder decomposition
{\pazocal Z}_{0}(F,D)
, where
(F,D)
is the canonical resolution of
(E,C)
.

Now let
{\pazocal Z}=(\{Z_{e}\},\{W_{e,v}^{(k)}\})
be an
(E,C)
-structure on
(Y,\rho)\in\textsf{LHomeo}
. We need to show that there is a unique
(E,C)
-equivariant continuous map
\psi\colon Y\to X
. We first build an
(E,C)
-dynamical system

(\{\overline{Y}_{v}\mid v\in E^{0}\},\{\overline{H}_{x}\mid x\in E^{1}\},\{\overline{\theta}_{x}\mid x\in E^{1}\})

on
\overline{Y}=Y^{0}\sqcup Y^{1}
, where
Y^{0}
and
Y^{1}
are disjoint copies of
Y
. For
i=0,1
we denote by
y^{i}
the canonical image of
y\in Y
in
Y^{i}
, and by
N^{i}
the canonical image of a subset
N
of
Y
in
Y^{i}
, with
W^{(k,i)}_{e,v}:=(W^{(k)}_{e,v})^{i}
.

First we set
\overline{Y}_{v}=\bigsqcup_{e\in B_{v}}Z_{e}^{0}
for
v\in E^{0,0}
, and
\overline{Y}_{w}=Z_{e}^{1}
for
w\in E^{0,1}
, where
e
is the unique blue edge such that
s(e)=w
. Now we set
\overline{H}_{e}=Z^{0}_{e}
for
e\in B
, and
\overline{H}_{f}=W_{e,v}^{(k,0)}
for
f\in R
with
r(f)=v
, where
e\in B
is the unique blue edge such that
s(e)=s(f)
, and
k
is the unique index in
\{1,\dots,a_{e,v}\}
such that
f=f_{e,v}^{(k)}
. Finally we define

\overline{\theta}_{e}\colon\overline{Y}_{s(e)}\to\overline{H}_{e},\qquad\overline{\theta}_{e}(z^{1})=z^{0},\text{ for }z\in Z_{e},

and

\overline{\theta}_{f}\colon\overline{Y}_{s(f)}\to\overline{H}_{f},\qquad\overline{\theta}_{f}(z^{1})=\Big(\rho|_{W_{e,v}^{(k)}}\Big)^{-1}(z)^{0},\text{ for }z\in Z_{e},\text{ where }f=f_{e,v}^{(k)}.

By
[
10
, Corollary 6.11]
, there exists a unique
(E,C)
-equivariant continuous map
\overline{\psi}\colon\overline{Y}\to\Omega(E,C)
. This means that
\overline{\psi}(\overline{Y}_{v})\subseteq\Omega(E,C)_{v}
for all
v\in E^{0}
,
\overline{\psi}(\overline{H}_{x})\subseteq H_{x}
for all
x\in E^{1}
, and that
\overline{\psi}(\overline{\theta}_{x}(y))=\theta_{x}(\overline{\psi}(y))
for all
y\in\overline{Y}_{s(x)}
,
x\in E^{1}
. Note that, for
y^{0}\in Y^{0}
, we have
\overline{\psi}(y^{0})\in\Omega(E,C)_{v}
for some
v\in E^{0,0}
, and hence
\overline{\psi}(y^{0})\in X
. Thus the map

\psi\colon Y\to X,\quad\psi(y)=\overline{\psi}(y^{0})

is well-defined. We have

\psi(Z_{e})=\overline{\psi}(Z_{e}^{0})=\overline{\psi}(\overline{H}_{e})\subseteq H_{e},\qquad\psi(W_{e,v}^{(k)})=\overline{\psi}(W_{e,v}^{(k,0)})=\overline{\psi}(\overline{H}_{f_{e,v}^{(k)}})\subseteq H_{e,v}^{(k)}.

We finally check that
\psi\circ\rho=\sigma\circ\psi
. For this it is enough to check that
\psi\circ\rho|_{W_{e,v}^{(k)}}=\sigma|_{H_{e,v}^{(k)}}\circ\psi
for all
e\in B
,
v\in E^{0,0}
and
1\leq k\leq a_{e,v}
. For
z\in Z_{e}
, we compute

\displaystyle\psi((\rho|_{W_{e,v}^{(k)}})^{-1}(z))

\displaystyle=\overline{\psi}((\rho|_{W_{ev}^{(k)}})^{-1}(z)^{0})

\displaystyle=\overline{\psi}(\overline{\theta}_{f_{e,v}^{(k)}}(z^{1}))

\displaystyle=\theta_{f_{e,v}^{(k)}}(\overline{\psi}(z^{1}))

\displaystyle=\theta_{f_{e,v}^{(k)}}(\overline{\psi}(\overline{\theta}_{e}^{-1}(z^{0})))

\displaystyle=\theta_{f_{e,v}^{(k)}}\theta_{e}^{-1}(\overline{\psi}(z^{0}))=(\sigma|_{H^{(k)}_{e,v}})^{-1}(\psi(z)),

where we have used Proposition
5.18
in the last equality. Hence
\psi
is an
(E,C)
-equivariant continuous map from
Y
to
X
. Uniqueness of
\psi
follows from uniqueness of
\overline{\psi}
. Indeed, the universality of the
(E,C)
-dynamical system
\Omega(E,C)
turns out to be equivalent to the universality of the natural
(E,C)
-structure on
X
.
∎

Remark 5.24
.

For a dynamical system
(Y,\rho)\in\textsf{LHomeo}
with an
(E,C)
-structure
(\{\overline{H}_{e}\}_{e\in B},\{\overline{H}_{f}\}_{f\in R})
, the unique
(E,C)
-equivariant map
\psi\colon Y\to X
from Theorem
5.23
can be explicitly described. Indeed, observe that we have decompositions

Y=\bigsqcup_{e\in B}\overline{H}_{e}=\bigsqcup_{f\in R}\overline{H}_{f}

and homeomorphisms

\rho_{f}\colon\overline{H}_{f}\to\overline{H}_{e},

for each
f\in R
, where
e
is the unique blue edge such that
s(e)=s(f)
. Then, for
y\in Y
,
\psi(y)
consists of the set of elements

a_{f_{1}}^{-1}\cdots a_{f_{m}}^{-1}a_{g_{n}}\cdots a_{g_{1}}\in\mathbb{F}(A),

in the notation of Construction
5.19
, such that

y\in\text{Dom}(\rho_{f_{1}}^{-1}\circ\cdots\circ\rho_{f_{m}}^{-1}\circ\rho_{g_{n}}\circ\cdots\circ\rho_{g_{1}}),

where the above is a composition of partial maps (see e.g.
[
32
, Chapter 2]
).

We end this section by proving that any
(X,\sigma)\in\textsf{LHomeo}
can be expressed as an inverse limit of a sequence of generalized finite shifts.

Let
(X,\sigma)\in\textsf{LHomeo}
, and fix an
l
-diagram
(F,D)
corresponding to
(X,\sigma)
under the bijection established in Theorem
3.39
. For each
i\geq 0
, write
E(i):=(F^{0,2i}\cup F^{0,2i+1},F^{1,2i})
and let
C(i)
be the partition of
E(i)^{1}
inherited from
D
. Then, by Proposition
5.8
,
(E(i),C(i))
is a generalized finite shift graph. We let
(X_{i},\sigma_{i})
be the generalized finite shift (Definition
5.12
) associated to the canonical resolution
(F(i),D(i))
of the generalized finite shift graph
(E(i),C(i))
. Moreover, by Proposition
5.8
,
(X,\sigma)
admits an
(E(i),C(i))
-structure for each
i\geq 0
. It follows from Theorem
5.23
that there is a unique continuous
(E(i),C(i))
-equivariant map
\psi_{i}\colon(X,\sigma)\to(X_{i},\sigma_{i})
for each
i\geq 0
, where we consider the natural
(E(i),C(i))
-structure on
(X_{i},\sigma_{i})
.

Proposition 5.25
.

The space
(X_{i+1},\sigma_{i+1})
admits an
(E(i),C(i))
-structure, in such a way that the natural
(E(i+1),C(i+1))
-structure on
(X_{i+1},\sigma_{i+1})
is a refinement (in the sense of Definition
5.2
) of the mentioned
(E(i),C(i))
-structure.

In particular, the map
\psi_{i}
factors through
\psi_{i+1}
.

Proof.

Let
(G,H)
be the Bratteli diagram whose first two layers consist of the layers
E(i)=(F^{0,2i}\cup F^{0,2i+1},F^{1,2i})
and
(F^{0,2i+1}\cup F^{0,2i+2},F^{1,2i+1})
, respectively, with the separations inherited from
D
, and whose other layers coincide with the layers of
(F(i+1),D(i+1))
, moved down in two units, so that for instance
G^{0,k}=F(i+1)^{0,k-2}
for all
k\geq 2
. Using Proposition
5.11
it is easy to show that
(G,H)
is an
l
-diagram. By Theorem
3.36
, we have that the dynamical system associated to the
l
-diagram
(G,H)
is conjugate to
(X_{i+1},\sigma_{i+1})
. In particular, we see from Proposition
5.8
that
(X_{i+1},\sigma_{i+1})
admits an
(E(i),C(i))
-structure, and moreover the natural
(E(i+1),C(i+1))
-structure on
(X_{i+1},\sigma_{i+1})
is a refinement of the
(E(i),C(i))
-structure.

Now Theorem
5.23
provides us with a unique equivariant continuous map

\psi_{i,i+1}\colon X_{i+1}\to X_{i},

with respect to the respective
(E(i),C(i))
-structures. Since the three maps
\psi_{i}
,
\psi_{i+1}
and
\psi_{i,i+1}
preserve the
(E(i),C(i))
-structure, we get by uniqueness that
\psi_{i}=\psi_{i,i+1}\circ\psi_{i+1}
. This concludes the proof of the proposition.
∎

We therefore have an inverse system
\{(X_{i},\sigma_{i}),\psi_{i,j}\}
in
LHomeo
, and hence we get a dynamical system
(\varprojlim X_{i},\tilde{\sigma})
together with an equivariant continuous map

\psi\colon(X,\sigma)\to(\varprojlim X_{i},\tilde{\sigma}),\quad\psi(x)=(\psi_{0}(x),\psi_{1}(x),\dots).

Theorem 5.26
.

With the notation as above, we have that the natural equivariant map

\psi\colon X\to\varprojlim X_{i}

is a homeomorphism.

Proof.

Let
x=(e_{0},e_{1},e_{2},\dots)\in X
, where all
e_{i}\in F^{1,i}
are blue edges. Since
\psi_{i}
is
(E(i),C(i))
-equivariant, we have

\psi_{i}(x)=(e_{2i},\tilde{e}_{i,1},\tilde{e}_{i,2},\dots),

where
\tilde{e}_{i,j}\in F(i)^{1,j}
for
j\geq 1
. It follows that if
x=(e_{i}),x^{\prime}=(e^{\prime}_{i})\in X
and
\psi(x)=\psi(x^{\prime})
, then
e_{2i}=e^{\prime}_{2i}
for all
i\geq 0
. But then
e_{2i+1}=e^{\prime}_{2i+1}
for all
i\geq 0
, since there is a unique blue edge in
F
joining
r(e_{2i+2})
with
s(e_{2i})
. It follows that
\psi
is injective.

We now show that
\psi
is surjective. Let
(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},\dots)\in\varprojlim X_{i}
. Write

x_{i}=(e_{2i},\tilde{e}_{i,1},\tilde{e}_{i,2},\dots),

where
e_{2i}\in F^{1,2i}
and
\tilde{e}_{i,j}\in F(i)^{1,j}
for all
j\geq 1
. Since
\psi_{i,i+1}(x_{i+1})=x_{i}
and all the maps
\psi_{i,i+1}
are
(E(i),C(i))
-equivariant, it follows by a simple induction that there is a unique blue path

x=(e_{0},e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{2i},e_{2i+1},e_{2i+2},\dots)\in X.

We show that
\psi(x)=(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},\dots)
. For this we need the description of the maps
\psi_{j}
given in Remark
5.24
. We will concentrate in showing that
\psi_{0}(x)=x_{0}
. Consider some
i\geq 0
. Then the first
2i
coordinates of
\psi_{0}(x)
are determined by the set of all elements

a_{f_{1}}^{-1}\cdots a_{f_{m}}^{-1}a_{g_{n}}\cdots a_{g_{1}}\in\mathbb{F}(A)

where
f_{1},\dots,f_{m},g_{1},\dots,g_{n}
are red edges in
E(0)
satisfying the requirements given in Construction
5.19
, such that

x\in\text{Dom}(\theta_{f_{1}}^{-1}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{f_{m}}^{-1}\circ\theta_{g_{n}}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{g_{1}})

and with
n+m\leq i
. Here
\{\theta_{f}\}
is the set of partial maps as in Remark
5.24
associated to the
(E(0),C(0))
-structure on
X
. Now it follows from the construction of
\sigma
(given in Construction
3.34
) and from the proof of surjectivity of
\sigma
(given in Lemma
3.35
) that this information is contained in the blue path
(e_{0},e_{1},\dots,e_{2i-1})
, which is the initial segment of
x
of length
2i
. Note that, by the same argument as before and the definition of the maps
\psi_{j,j+1}
, we see that, for
y\in X_{i}
, the first
2i
components of
\psi_{0,i}(y)
are determined by the unique blue path
(e_{0}^{\prime},e_{1}^{\prime},\dots,e^{\prime}_{2i-1})
joining
r(y)\in F^{0,{2i}}
with a vertex in
F^{0,0}
in the graph
F
, because the
(E(0),C(0))
-structure on
X_{i}
is determined by the canonical
(E(0),C(0))
-structure on the
l
-diagram obtained by topping
(F(i),D(i))
with the first
2i
layers of
(F,D)
. Since
\psi_{i}(x)=(e_{2i},\dots)
and
x_{i}=(e_{2i},\dots)
, that unique blue path is precisely
(e_{0},e_{1},\dots,e_{2i-1})
for both
\psi_{i}(x)
and
x_{i}
, and thus the first
2i
components of
\psi_{0,i}(\psi_{i}(x))
agree with the first
2i
components of
\psi_{0,i}(x_{i})
. But
\psi_{0,i}(\psi_{i}(x))=\psi_{0}(x)
and
\psi_{0,i}(x_{i})=x_{0}
, hence the first
2i
coordinates of
\psi_{0}(x)
must agree with the first
2i
coordinates of
x_{0}
. Since this holds for all
i\geq 0
, we get that
\psi_{0}(x)=x_{0}
.

In the same way we may prove that
\psi_{n}(x)=x_{n}
for all
n\geq 1
, completing the proof.
∎

6.
Approximating algebras associated to a dynamical system

Let
(K,\ast)
be a field endowed with an involution
\ast
. In this section we analyze both the Steinberg algebra
A_{K}({\pazocal G})
and the
C^{*}
-algebra
C^{*}({\pazocal G})
of the Deaconu-Renault groupoid
{\pazocal G}={\pazocal G}(X,\sigma)
associated to an object
(X,\sigma)\in\textsf{LHomeo}
. We will describe approximating processes of such algebras by means of the corresponding algebras associated with the sequence of generalized finite shifts
(X_{i},\sigma_{i})
described at the end of Section
5
.

6.1.
The general approximation construction

Fix
(X,\sigma)\in\textsf{LHomeo}
and a
\sigma
-refined sequence of partitions
\{{\pazocal P}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
of
X
. Let
(F,D)
be the
l
-diagram associated to
\{{\pazocal P}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
, see Construction
3.24
.
We adopt the terminology and notation introduced in Section
5
.

For each
n\geq 0
we define bipartite finite separated graphs
(F_{n},D^{n})
as follows:

F_{n}^{0}:=F_{n}^{0,0}\sqcup F_{n}^{0,1}\text{ with }F_{n}^{0,0}:=F^{0,n}\text{ and }F_{n}^{0,1}:=F^{0,n+1},

F_{n}^{1}:=F^{1,n},

and
D_{v}^{n}:=D_{v}
for
v\in F_{n}^{0,0}
. Note that
r(F_{n}^{1})=F_{n}^{0,0}
and
s(F_{n}^{1})=F_{n}^{0,1}
. With this notation, the generalized finite shift graphs
(E(n),C(n))
introduced in Section
5
are precisely
(F_{2n},C^{2n})
.

For notational convenience, we write
L_{n}:=L_{K}(F_{n},D^{n})
, the Leavitt path algebra of the separated graph
(F_{n},D^{n})
. Also
p_{n}
will denote the projection in
L_{n}
on
F_{n}^{0,0}
, so that

p_{n}=\sum_{v\in F_{n}^{0,0}}v.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
j\geq 0
be an integer.

(1)

For any vertex
v\in F_{2j}^{0,0}
,

\textbf{S}(v):=\sum_{e\in B_{v}}\sum_{e^{\prime}\in B_{s(e)}}s(e^{\prime})=\sum_{f\in R_{v}}\sum_{f^{\prime}\in R(f)}s(f^{\prime})\in L_{2j+1}.

(2)

For any vertex
v\in F_{2j+1}^{0,0}
and any
g\in R_{r(g)}
such that
s(g)=v
,

\textbf{S}(v):=\sum_{e\in B_{v}}\sum_{e^{\prime}\in B_{s(e)}}s(e^{\prime})=\sum_{f\in R(g)}\sum_{f^{\prime}\in R_{s(f)}}s(f^{\prime})\in L_{2j+2}.

Proof.

Note that in both
i)
and
ii)
the sum
\sum_{e\in B_{v}}\sum_{e^{\prime}\in B_{s(e)}}s(e^{\prime})
is a sum of distinct vertices in
F^{n+2}
, where
n
is either
2j
or
2j+1
.

(1)

The set of vertices
s(e^{\prime})
in the above expression is in bijective correspondence with the set of pairs
(e,e^{\prime})
, where
e\in B_{v}
and
e^{\prime}\in B_{s(e)}
. By Proposition
3.3
(ii), the set of pairs
(e,e^{\prime})
where
e\in B_{v}
and
e^{\prime}\in B_{s(e)}
is in bijective correspondence with the set of pairs
(f,f^{\prime})
where
f\in R_{v}
,
f^{\prime}\in R(f)
, and
(f,f^{\prime})
corresponds to
(e,e^{\prime})
if and only if
s(f^{\prime})=s(e^{\prime})
. The result follows.

(2)

The argument is similar to the one given for the first part, taking into account conditions (f) and (g) in the definition of
l
-diagram (Definition
3.2
).∎

Remark 6.2
.

Note that each
e\in s^{-1}(v)
determines a unique set
X(e)\in D_{v}
. This notation is compatible with the notation introduced in Definition
5.10
. With this notation, the formulas in Lemma
6.1
take a uniform shape:

\textbf{S}(v)=\sum_{e\in X}\sum_{e^{\prime}\in X(e)}s(e^{\prime})

for any
X\in D_{v}
,
v\in F^{0}
.

Proposition 6.3
.

There exist unital
*
-homomorphisms
\widetilde{\phi}_{n}\colon L_{n}\rightarrow L_{n+1}
such that
\widetilde{\phi}_{n}(p_{n})=p_{n+2}
and
\widetilde{\phi}_{n}(p_{n+1})=p_{n+1}
.

Proof.

We define
\widetilde{\phi}_{n}:L_{n}\rightarrow L_{n+1}
by the rules

\widetilde{\phi}_{n}(\alpha)=\begin{cases}w&\text{ if }\alpha=w\in F_{n}^{0,1};\\
\textbf{S}(v)&\text{ if }\alpha=v\in F_{n}^{0,0};\\
\sum_{\overline{e}\in B_{s(e)}}\overline{e}^{*}&\text{ if }\alpha=e\in F_{n}^{1}\text{ is a blue edge};\\
\sum_{\overline{f}\in R(f)}\overline{f}^{*}&\text{ if }n=2j\text{ and }\alpha=f\in F_{n}^{1}\text{ is a red edge};\\
\sum_{\overline{f}\in R_{s(f)}}\overline{f}^{*}&\text{ if }n=2j+1\text{ and }\alpha=f\in F_{n}^{1}\text{ is a red edge}.\end{cases}

We must check that the defining relations for
L_{n}
in Definition
2.1
are satisfied. The relations (V), (E) are easily verified. For (SCK1) take first
e,f\in B_{v}
, where
v\in F_{n}^{0,0}=F^{0,n}
. We compute

\begin{split}\widetilde{\phi}_{n}(e)^{*}\widetilde{\phi}_{n}(f)=\Big(\sum_{\overline{e}\in B_{s(e)}}\overline{e}\Big)\Big(\sum_{\overline{f}\in B_{s(f)}}\overline{f}^{*}\Big)=\sum_{\overline{e}\in B_{s(e)}}\sum_{\overline{f}\in B_{s(f)}}\overline{e}\overline{f}^{*}.\end{split}

The product
\overline{e}\overline{f}^{*}
is zero unless
s(\overline{e})=s(\overline{f})
. Since the edges are blue, this implies that
\overline{e}=\overline{f}
(see Definition
3.2
). In particular,
s(e)=r(\overline{e})=r(\overline{f})=s(f)
, and again we deduce that
e=f
. Hence

\widetilde{\phi}_{n}(e)^{*}\widetilde{\phi}_{n}(f)=\delta_{e,f}\Big(\sum_{\overline{e}\in B_{s(e)}}\overline{e}\overline{e}^{*}\Big)=\delta_{e,f}s(e)=\widetilde{\phi}_{n}(\delta_{e,f}s(e)),

where we have used the relation (SCK2) inside
L_{n+1}
.

Assume now that
n=2j
for
j\geq 0
and that
e,f\in R_{v}
for
v\in F_{2j}^{0,0}
. Then

\begin{split}\widetilde{\phi}_{n}(e)^{*}\widetilde{\phi}_{n}(f)=\sum_{\overline{e}\in R(e)}\sum_{\overline{f}\in R(f)}\overline{e}\overline{f}^{*}.\end{split}

Again, the product
\overline{e}\overline{f}^{*}
is zero unless
s(\overline{e})=s(\overline{f})
. We claim that in that case we necessarily have
(e,\overline{e})=(f,\overline{f})
. This follows from Proposition
3.3
(ii). Indeed, a first application of this proposition gives that there is a unique pair
(e_{0},e_{1})
of blue edges such that
r(e_{0})=r(e)=v
,
r(e_{1})=s(e_{0})
and
s(e_{1})=s(\overline{e})
. But now, since we are assuming that
s(\overline{e})=s(\overline{f})
, we have that
(e,\overline{e})
and
(f,\overline{f})
are two pairs of red edges with
\overline{e}\in R(e)
and
\overline{f}\in R(f)
which correspond to the same pair
(e_{0},e_{1})
of blue edges, so the uniqueness part of Proposition
3.3
(ii) gives that
(e,\overline{e})=(f,\overline{f})
, as desired. Hence

\widetilde{\phi}_{n}(e)^{*}\widetilde{\phi}_{n}(f)=\delta_{e,f}\Big(\sum_{\overline{e}\in R(e)}\overline{e}\overline{e}^{*}\Big)=\delta_{e,f}s(e)=\widetilde{\phi}_{n}(\delta_{e,f}s(e)),

where again we have used the relation (SCK2) inside
L_{n+1}
.

Assume finally that
n=2j+1
for
j\geq 0
and that
e,f\in R(g)
for
g\in R_{r(g)}
such that
s(g)\in F_{2j+1}^{0,0}
. Then we have

\begin{split}\widetilde{\phi}_{n}(e)^{*}\widetilde{\phi}_{n}(f)=\sum_{\overline{e}\in R_{s(e)}}\sum_{\overline{f}\in R_{s(f)}}\overline{e}\overline{f}^{*}.\end{split}

Again, the product
\overline{e}\overline{f}^{*}
is zero unless
s(\overline{e})=s(\overline{f})
. We claim that, as in the previous case, we necessarily have
(e,\overline{e})=(f,\overline{f})
. The argument is similar to the above, now using conditions (f) and (g) in Definition
3.2
. Namely, by (f) there is a unique blue pair
(e_{0},e_{1})
such that
(e_{0},e_{1},e,\overline{e})
is a romb. Now by the uniqueness part of (g) (and since
e,f\in R(g)
), we deduce that
(e,\overline{e})=(f,\overline{f})
. Hence

\widetilde{\phi}_{n}(e)^{*}\widetilde{\phi}_{n}(f)=\delta_{e,f}\Big(\sum_{\overline{e}\in R_{s(e)}}\overline{e}\overline{e}^{*}\Big)=\delta_{e,f}s(e)=\widetilde{\phi}_{n}(\delta_{e,f}s(e))

again by the relation (SCK2) inside
L_{n+1}
.

For (SCK2) note that, for
e\in B_{v}
,
v\in F_{n}^{0,0}
, we have

\widetilde{\phi}_{n}(e)\widetilde{\phi}_{n}(e)^{*}=\sum_{f,g\in B_{s(e)}}f^{*}g=\sum_{f\in B_{s(e)}}s(f),

so that

\sum_{e\in B_{v}}\widetilde{\phi}_{n}(e)\widetilde{\phi}_{n}(e)^{*}=\sum_{e\in B_{v}}\sum_{f\in B_{s(e)}}s(f)=\textbf{S}(v)=\widetilde{\phi}_{n}(v)

because of Lemma
6.1
. Similarly, we get that (SCK2) is also preserved for red edges.

This analysis gives the well-definiteness of the maps
\widetilde{\phi}_{n}\colon L_{n}\rightarrow L_{n+1}
. Now note that

\widetilde{\phi}(p_{n+1})=\sum_{w\in F_{n}^{0,1}}w=p_{n+1}\quad\text{ and }\quad\widetilde{\phi}_{n}(p_{n})=\sum_{v\in F_{n}^{0,0}}\textbf{S}(v)=\sum_{w\in F_{n+1}^{0,1}}w=p_{n+2}.

The proposition is now proved.
∎

For
m>n
, denote by
\widetilde{\phi}_{n,m}\colon L_{n}\rightarrow L_{m}
the composition
\widetilde{\phi}_{m-1}\circ\cdots\circ\widetilde{\phi}_{n}
. Set

L_{\infty}:=\varinjlim(L_{n},\widetilde{\phi}_{n,m}),

with canonical maps
\widetilde{\phi}_{n,\infty}\colon L_{n}\to L_{\infty}
.

We will need the following observations.

Lemma 6.4
.

With the above notation, and with
k=m-n
, the following hold.

(1)

If
v_{1},\dots,v_{l}
are mutually distinct vertices in
F_{n}
, then each of
\widetilde{\phi}_{n,m}(v_{i})
is a sum of mutually distinct vertices in
F_{m}
, and moreover the vertices appearing in the decompositions of two different vertices
v_{i}
and
v_{j}
are also mutually distinct.

(2)

If
f\in F_{n}^{1}
, then
\widetilde{\phi}_{n,m}(f)
is a sum of edges
g\in F_{m}^{1}
whose sources are mutually distinct if
k
is even, and is a sum of “ghost” edges
g^{*}
for edges
g\in F_{m}^{1}
whose sources are mutually distinct if
k
is odd. Moreover, for each
w\in F_{m}^{0,0}
, all the edges
g\in F_{m}^{1}
appearing in the above sum with range
w
belong to the same set
Z\in D_{w}
.

Proof.

The easy proof of
i)
is left to the reader. For
ii)
, we proceed by induction on
k
. If
k=1
then
\widetilde{\phi}_{n}(f)=\sum_{\overline{f}\in X(f)}\overline{f}^{*}
, where we use the notation introduced in Remark
6.2
. Since
r(\overline{f})=r(\overline{f}^{\prime})
for all
\overline{f},\overline{f}^{\prime}\in X(f)
, the result follows from the fact that, except possibly at the first layer, distinct blue (respectively, distinct red) edges with the same range must have distinct sources (see Definition
3.2
and Remark
3.5
(5)).

Now suppose that the result holds for
k\geq 1
. Assume first that
k
is even. Then we have that
\widetilde{\phi}_{n,n+k}(f)=\sum_{g\in L}g
, where
L
is a set of edges in
F_{n+k}=F_{m}
whose sources are mutually distinct, and such that all members of
L
with the same range belong to the same element of the partition
D
. Using the notation introduced in Remark
6.2
, we have

\widetilde{\phi}_{n,n+k+1}(f)=\sum_{g\in L}\widetilde{\phi}_{n+k}(g)=\sum_{g\in L}\sum_{\overline{g}\in X(g)}\overline{g}^{*},

where
X(g)\in D_{s(g)}
. Let
w\in F_{m}^{0}
and let
X^{w}\in D_{w}
be the unique set in the partition
D_{w}
to which all elements
g
of
L
with range
w
belong. Using Lemma
6.1
(and Remark
6.2
), we have

\sum_{\{g\in L\mid r(g)=w\}}\sum_{\overline{g}\in X(g)}s(\overline{g})\leq\sum_{g\in X^{w}}\sum_{\overline{g}\in X(g)}s(\overline{g})=\textbf{S}(w).

Since in addition
\textbf{S}(w)\cdot\textbf{S}(w^{\prime})=0
for distinct
w,w^{\prime}\in F_{m}^{0,0}
, we conclude that
\{s(\overline{g})\mid\overline{g}\in X(g),g\in L\}
is a set of mutually distinct vertices in
F_{m+1}^{0,1}
.
Moreover, if
r(\overline{g})=r(\overline{h})
for
\overline{g}\in X(g)
and
\overline{h}\in X(h)
, with
g,h\in L
, then we get

s(g)=r(\overline{g})=r(\overline{h})=s(h)

and by induction hypothesis we conclude that
g=h
, so that
\overline{g},\overline{h}\in X(g)=X(h)
, as wanted.

The case where
k
is odd is treated in the same way.
∎

We now recall some basic definitions, see
[
32
, Definition 12.9]
.

Definition 6.5
.

A set
F
of partial isometries of a
*
-algebra
A
is said to be
tame
if for all two elements
u,u^{\prime}\in U
, where
U
is the multiplicative
*
-subsemigroup of
A
generated by
F
, we have that
e(u)
and
e(u^{\prime})
are commuting elements of
A
, where
e(v)=vv^{*}
for
v\in U
. Note that if
F
is a tame set of partial isometries, then all elements of
U
are indeed partial isometries, and therefore the elements
e(u)
, with
u\in U
, are mutually commuting projections in
A
.

A
*
-algebra
A
is said to be
tame
if it is generated as a
*
-algebra by a tame set of partial isometries. If
A
is a
*
-algebra generated by a subset
F
of partial isometries, there is a universal tame
*
-algebra
A^{\text{ab}}
associated to
F
. By definition, there is a surjective
*
-homomorphism

\pi\colon A\to A^{\text{ab}}
so that
\pi(F)
is a tame set of partial isometries generating
A^{\text{ab}}
, and such that for any
*
-homomorphism

\psi\colon A\to B
of
A
to a
*
-algebra
B
such that
\psi(F)
is a tame set of partial isometries in
B
, there is a unique
*
-homomorphism

\overline{\psi}\colon A^{\text{ab}}\to B
such that
\psi=\overline{\psi}\circ\pi
.
Indeed, we have

A^{\text{ab}}=A/J
, where
J
is the ideal of
A
generated by all the commutators
[e(u),e(u^{\prime})]
, where
u,u^{\prime}\in U
.

If
(E,C)
is a separated graph, then the universal tame
*
-algebra associated to the generating set
E^{0}\sqcup E^{1}
of partial isometries of
L_{K}(E,C)
is denoted by
L^{{\rm ab}}_{K}(E,C)
. In addition, we denote by
{\pazocal O}(E,C)
the
C^{*}
-algebra
C^{*}(E,C)/{\pazocal J}
, where
{\pazocal J}
is the closed ideal of
C^{*}(E,C)
generated by all the commutators
[e(u),e(u^{\prime})]
, for
u,u^{\prime}\in U
, where
U
is the multiplicative
*
-subsemigroup of
C^{*}(E,C)
generated by
E^{0}\cup E^{1}
. Clearly
{\pazocal O}(E,C)
is universal with respect to
*
-homomorphisms from
C^{*}(E,C)
to a tame
C^{*}
-algebra.

The following lemma will be crucial for our main result. We continue with the notation introduced at the beginning of this section.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
U_{n}
be the multiplicative
*
-subsemigroup of
L_{n}=L_{K}(F_{n},D^{n})
generated by the set
F_{n}^{1}
. Let
\omega
be a word in
U_{n}
of length
k
. Then
\widetilde{\phi}_{n,n+k}(\omega\omega^{*})
is a sum of distinct vertices of
F_{n+k}
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
k
. If
k=1
, then either
\omega=e
or
\omega=e^{*}
for some
e\in F_{n}^{1}
. In the first case
\omega=e
, we have

\widetilde{\phi}_{n}(\omega\omega^{*})=\widetilde{\phi}_{n}(ee^{*})=\Big(\sum_{\overline{e}\in X(e)}\overline{e}^{*}\Big)\Big(\sum_{\overline{e}^{\prime}\in X(e)}\overline{e}^{\prime}\Big)=\sum_{\overline{e}\in X(e)}s(\overline{e}),

which is a sum of mutually distinct vertices of
F_{n+1}
. In the second case
\omega=e^{*}
, we have

\widetilde{\phi}_{n}(\omega\omega^{*})=\widetilde{\phi}_{n}(e^{*}e)=\widetilde{\phi}_{n}(s(e))=s(e).

The result follows for
k=1
.

Suppose now that the result is true for words of length
k
and let
\omega\in U_{n}
be a word of length
k+1
. Write
\omega=x\omega_{0}
, where
\omega_{0}
is a word of length
k
and
x
is a word of length
1
(so that
x=f
or
x=f^{*}
for
f\in F_{n}^{1}
). By induction hypothesis we can write

\widetilde{\phi}_{n,n+k}(\omega_{0}\omega_{0}^{*})=\sum_{i=1}^{t}v_{i}

for mutually distinct vertices
v_{i}
of
F_{n+k}
. We now use Lemma
6.4
(ii). Suppose first that
\widetilde{\phi}_{n,n+k}(x)=\sum_{g\in L}g^{*}
, where
L
is a family of edges in
F_{n+k}
whose sources are mutually distinct, such that, for each
w\in F_{n+k}^{0,0}
, all the edges
g\in L
with range
w
belong to the same set
Z\in D_{w}
. We then have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\phi}_{n,n+k}(\omega\omega^{*})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\phi}_{n,n+k}(x)\widetilde{\phi}_{n,n+k}(\omega_{0}\omega_{0}^{*})\widetilde{\phi}_{n,n+k}(x)^{*}

\displaystyle=\Big(\sum_{g\in L}g^{*}\Big)\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{t}v_{i}\Big)\Big(\sum_{h\in L}h\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{t}\sum_{\{g,h\in L\mid r(g)=r(h)=v_{i}\}}g^{*}h

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{t}\sum_{\{g\in L\mid r(g)=v_{i}\}}s(g),

where for the last equality we have used the fact that any two edges
g,h\in L
with the same range
w
belong to the same element of
D_{w}
. Now since the sources of edges in
L
are mutually distinct, we see that
\widetilde{\phi}_{n,n+k}(\omega\omega^{*})
is a sum of mutually distinct vertices in
F_{n+k}^{0,1}
. Since
\widetilde{\phi}_{n,n+k+1}(\omega\omega^{*})=\widetilde{\phi}_{n+k}(\widetilde{\phi}_{n,n+k}(\omega\omega^{*}))
, we obtain the result in this case by Lemma
6.4
(i).

Suppose now that
\widetilde{\phi}_{n,n+k}(x)
is a sum of edges in
F_{n+k}
. Then necessarily

\widetilde{\phi}_{n,n+k+1}(x)=\widetilde{\phi}_{n+k}(\widetilde{\phi}_{n,n+k}(x))

will be of the form
\sum_{g\in L}g^{*}
, where
L
is a family of edges in
F_{n+k+1}
whose sources are mutually distinct, such that, for each
w\in F_{n+k+1}^{0,0}
, all the edges
g\in L
with range
w
belong to the same set
Z\in D_{w}
. Since
\widetilde{\phi}_{n,n+k+1}(\omega_{0}\omega_{0}^{*})
is a sum of mutually distinct vertices by induction hypothesis and by Lemma
6.4
(i), the same argument as before gives us the result also in this case.
∎

We are now in a position to show that the algebra
L_{\infty}=\varinjlim L_{n}
is tame, and compute it by means of the universal tame
*
-algebras of
L_{n}
with respect to the sets
F_{n}
.

Theorem 6.7
.

The
*
-algebra
L_{\infty}
is tame, with generating set of partial isometries
F_{\infty}^{1}:=\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\widetilde{\phi}_{n,\infty}(F_{n}^{1})
. Moreover, the natural
*
-homomorphisms
\widetilde{\phi}_{n,\infty}\colon L_{n}\to L_{\infty}
factor through
L^{{\rm ab}}_{n}:=L^{{\rm ab}}_{K}(F_{n},D^{n})
with homomorphisms
\overline{\phi}_{n,\infty}\colon L^{{\rm ab}}_{n}\to L_{\infty}
, and we have

L_{\infty}=\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\widetilde{\phi}_{n,\infty}(L_{n})=\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\overline{\phi}_{n,\infty}(L^{{\rm ab}}_{n}).

Proof.

Let
n,n^{\prime}
be non-negative integers, and let
\omega,\omega^{\prime}
be words in
U_{n}
and
U_{n^{\prime}}
respectively. By Lemmas
6.6
and
6.4
(i), there exists
m\geq n,n^{\prime}
such that both
\widetilde{\phi}_{n,m}(e(\omega))
and
\widetilde{\phi}_{n^{\prime},m}(e(\omega^{\prime}))
are finite sums of mutually distinct vertices of
F_{m}
. Therefore they are commuting elements of
L_{m}
. It follows that
F_{\infty}^{1}=\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\widetilde{\phi}_{n,\infty}(F_{n}^{1})
is a tame set of partial isometries of
L_{\infty}
. By the universal property of
L^{{\rm ab}}_{n}
,
\widetilde{\phi}_{n,\infty}
factors (uniquely) through a
*
-homomorphism
\overline{\phi}_{n,\infty}\colon L^{{\rm ab}}_{n}\to L_{\infty}
, and obviously
\widetilde{\phi}_{n,\infty}(L_{n})=\overline{\phi}_{n,\infty}(L^{{\rm ab}}_{n})
. This completes the proof.
∎

Let
\widetilde{\phi}_{n}\colon L_{n}\to L_{n+1}
be the
*
-homomorphism defined in Proposition
6.3
. Observe that

\widetilde{\phi}_{n+1}\circ\widetilde{\phi}_{n}(p_{n})=\widetilde{\phi}_{n+1}\Big(\sum_{w\in F_{n+1}^{0,1}}w\Big)=p_{n+2}\in L_{n+2}.

From now on we will write
{\pazocal A}_{n}=p_{n}L_{n}p_{n}
, and we will denote by
\phi_{n}\colon{\pazocal A}_{2n}\rightarrow{\pazocal A}_{2n+2}
the restriction to
{\pazocal A}_{2n}
of the composition
\widetilde{\phi}_{2n+1}\circ\widetilde{\phi}_{2n}
. We consider the inductive limit
{\pazocal A}_{\infty}:=\varinjlim({\pazocal A}_{2n},\phi_{n})
, with canonical maps
\phi_{n,\infty}\colon{\pazocal A}_{2n}\rightarrow{\pazocal A}_{\infty}
. For
m>n
, we set
\phi_{n,m}:=\phi_{m-1}\circ\cdots\circ\phi_{n}\colon{\pazocal A}_{2n}\to{\pazocal A}_{2m}
.

Lemma 6.8
.

We have a natural
*
-isomorphism

{\pazocal A}_{\infty}\cong pL_{\infty}p,

where
p=\widetilde{\phi}_{2n,\infty}(p_{2n})
for all
n\geq 0
.

Proof.

We have obvious compatible
*
-homomorphisms
\widetilde{\phi}_{2n,\infty}\colon{\pazocal A}_{2n}\rightarrow pL_{\infty}p
, and hence we get a
*
-homomorphism

\lambda\colon{\pazocal A}_{\infty}\to pL_{\infty}p

defined by
\lambda(\phi_{n,\infty}(a))=\widetilde{\phi}_{2n,\infty}(a)
for
a\in{\pazocal A}_{2n}
. It is straightforward to check that
\lambda
is a
*
-isomorphism.
∎

Our aim for the rest of this subsection is to find a
*
-isomorphism
A_{K}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma))\cong{\pazocal A}_{\infty}
, thus providing a graph-theoretic model for the Steinberg algebra
A_{K}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma))
of the Deaconu-Renault groupoid
{\pazocal G}(X,\sigma)
associated to
(X,\sigma)
. We will also deal with the corresponding
C^{*}
-algebras.

To start with, we define
*
-homomorphisms
\varphi_{n}\colon{\pazocal A}_{2n}\rightarrow A_{K}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma))
. For this, we use another presentation for
{\pazocal A}_{2n}
, given by
[
5
, Proposition 2.8]
. We warn the reader about the different convention used for sources and ranges in
[
5
]
.

Definition 6.9
.

[
5
, Definition 2.7]

Let
(E,C)
be a finite bipartite separated graph with
E^{0}=E^{0,0}\sqcup E^{0,1}
,
s(E^{1})=E^{0,1}
and
r(E^{1})=E^{0,0}
. Let
LV_{K}(E,C)
be the universal
*
-algebra with generators
P_{V}\sqcup T
where
P_{V}=\{p_{v}\mid v\in E^{0,0}\}
and
T=\{\tau_{(e,f)}\mid e,f\in E^{1},s(e)=s(f)\}
, and subject to the relations

(V’)

p_{v}p_{w}=\delta_{v,w}p_{v}
, and
p_{v}=p_{v}^{*}
for
v,w\in E^{0,0}
;

(T)

\tau_{(e,f)}^{*}=\tau_{(f,e)}
for
e,f\in E^{1}
with
s(e)=s(f)
;

(E’)

\tau_{(e,f)}\cdot p_{r(f)}=p_{r(e)}\cdot\tau_{(e,f)}=\tau_{(e,f)}
for
e,f\in E^{1}
with
s(e)=s(f)
;

(SCK1’)

\tau_{(e,f)}\cdot\tau_{(g,h)}=\delta_{f,g}\tau_{(e,h)}
for
e,f,g,h\in E^{1}
with
s(e)=s(f)
,
s(g)=s(h)
and
f,g\in Y
belonging to the same set
Y\in C
;

(SCK2’)

p_{v}=\sum_{e\in Y}\tau_{(e,e)}
for all
v\in E^{0,0}
and all
Y\in C_{v}
.

We denote by
LV^{{\rm ab}}_{K}(E,C)
the abelianized Leavitt path algebra of
LV_{K}(E,C)
with respect to its generating family
T=\{\tau_{(e,f)}\mid e,f\in E^{1},s(e)=s(f)\}
of partial isometries.

Proposition 6.10
.

[
5
, Proposition 2.8]

Let
(E,C)
be a finite bipartite separated graph with
E^{0}=E^{0,0}\sqcup E^{0,1}
,
s(E^{1})=E^{0,1}
and
r(E^{1})=E^{0,0}
, and set
V=\sum_{v\in E^{0,0}}v\in L_{K}(E,C)
. Then there is a unique
*
-isomorphism

\varphi_{V}\colon LV_{K}(E,C)\to VL_{K}(E,C)V

such that
\varphi_{V}(p_{v})=v
for all
v\in E^{0,0}
and
\varphi_{V}(\tau_{(e,f)})=ef^{*}
for all
e,f\in E^{1}
with
s(e)=s(f)
.

Let us now define the
*
-homomorphisms
\varphi_{n}\colon{\pazocal A}_{2n}\rightarrow A_{K}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma))
. We use the presentation of
{\pazocal A}_{2n}=p_{2n}L_{K}(F_{2n},D^{2n})p_{2n}\cong LV_{K}(F_{2n},D^{2n})
provided by Definition
6.9
(see Proposition
6.10
above). Recall that the vertices of
F_{2n}^{0,0}
correspond to the sets in the partition
{\pazocal P}_{2n}
of
X
, and similarly, the vertices in
F_{2n}^{0,1}
correspond to the sets in the partition
{\pazocal P}_{2n+1}
of
X
.

Given
p_{v}
, with
v\in F_{2n}^{0,0}
, there is a unique clopen subset
U\in{\pazocal P}_{2n}
such that
v=U
. Define

\varphi_{n}(p_{v})=\varphi_{n}(p_{U})=\mathds{1}_{U}\in A_{K}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma)),

where we identify
Z(U,0,0,U)
with
U
. Now we want to define the images of elements of the form
\tau_{(e,f)}
, where
e
and
f
are edges in
F_{2n}
such that
s(e)=s(f)
. We write
s(e)=s(f)=W\in{\pazocal P}_{2n+1}
,
r(e)=U\in{\pazocal P}_{2n}
and
r(f)=V\in{\pazocal P}_{2n}
. We need to distinguish various cases.

(1)

e,f
are both blue edges. By definition, we have then that
W\subseteq U
and
W\subseteq V
. In this case, define

\varphi_{n}(\tau_{(e,f)})=\mathds{1}_{W}\in A_{K}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma)),

where we identify
Z(W,0,0,W)
with
W
.

(2)

e
and
f
are both red edges. In this case we have
W\subseteq\sigma(U)
and
W\subseteq\sigma(V)
. Define

\varphi_{n}(\tau_{(e,f)})=\mathds{1}_{Z((\sigma|_{U})^{-1}(W),1,1,(\sigma|_{V})^{-1}(W))}\in A_{K}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma)).

Observe that all sets
Z
in the partitions
{\pazocal P}_{m}
satisfy the property that the restriction of
\sigma
to
Z
is injective. Hence
Z((\sigma|_{U})^{-1}(W),1,1,(\sigma|_{V})^{-1}(W))
is well-defined.

(3)

e
is a red edge and
f
is a blue edge. In this case we have
W\subseteq\sigma(U)
and
W\subseteq V
. Define

\varphi_{n}(\tau_{(e,f)})=\mathds{1}_{Z((\sigma|_{U})^{-1}(W),1,0,W)}\in A_{K}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma)).

(4)

e
is a blue edge and
f
is a red edge. Then
W\subseteq U
and
W\subseteq\sigma(V)
. Define

\varphi_{n}(\tau_{(e,f)})=\mathds{1}_{Z(W,0,1,(\sigma|_{V})^{-1}(W))}\in A_{K}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma)).

It is straightforward to check that the defining relations from Definition
6.9
are preserved, and hence we obtain well-defined
*
-homomorphisms

\varphi_{n}\colon{\pazocal A}_{2n}\to A_{K}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma))

for all
n\geq 0
.

Observe that the algebras
L_{2n}=L_{K}(F_{2n},D^{2n})
are
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded for all
n\geq 0
, with grading determined by setting
d(v)=0
for each vertex
v\in F_{2n}^{0}
,
d(e)=0
if
e\in F_{2n}^{1}
is a blue edge, and
d(f)=1
if
f\in F_{2n}^{1}
is a red edge. For each
n\geq 0
,
{\pazocal A}_{2n}
is a graded
*
-subalgebra of
L_{2n}
, and the maps
\phi_{n}\colon{\pazocal A}_{2n}\to{\pazocal A}_{2n+2}
are graded
*
-homomorphisms, so we get the structure of a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded
*
-algebra in
{\pazocal A}_{\infty}
.

Proposition 6.11
.

The morphisms
\varphi_{n}\colon{\pazocal A}_{2n}\to A_{K}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma))
are graded and compatible with the maps
\phi_{n}\colon{\pazocal A}_{2n}\to{\pazocal A}_{2n+2}
from the inductive system
({\pazocal A}_{2n},\phi_{n})
. Therefore they define a well-defined graded
*
-homomorphism

\varphi\colon{\pazocal A}_{\infty}\to A_{K}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma)).

Proof.

It is clear from the definitions that the maps
\varphi_{n}
are graded
*
-homomorphisms for all
n\geq 0
, so it only remains to show compatibility with the maps
\phi_{n}
.

First, if
v\in F_{2n}^{0,0}
, then
v=U
for a unique
U\in{\pazocal P}_{2n}
, and
\phi_{n}(v)=\textbf{S}(v)
corresponds to the decomposition

U=\bigsqcup_{\{\overline{U}\in{\pazocal P}_{2n+2}\mid\overline{U}\subseteq U\}}\overline{U}

of
U
as a disjoint union of sets from
{\pazocal P}_{2n+2}
. Hence we obtain that
(\varphi_{n+1}\circ\phi_{n})(p_{v})=\varphi_{n}(p_{v})
.

There are four cases to consider concerning the elements
\tau_{(e,f)}
, all of them similar. We will only deal with the case where
e
is a red edge and
f
is a blue edge. So, let
e
be a red edge and
f
a blue edge, with
s(e)=s(f)=W
and
r(e)=U
,
r(f)=V
, where
U,V\in{\pazocal P}_{2n}
and
W\in{\pazocal P}_{2n+1}
. We then have

(6.1)

\phi_{n}(\tau_{(e,f)})=\phi_{n}(ef^{*})=\sum_{s(g)=s(h)}gh^{*}=\sum_{s(g)=s(h)}\tau_{(g,h)},

where
g
ranges on the set of red edges
g\in R_{s(\overline{e})}
, where
\overline{e}\in R(e)
, and
h
ranges on the set of blue edges
h\in B_{s(\overline{f})}
, where
\overline{f}\in B_{s(f)}
. Note that

S:=\{s(g)\mid g\in R_{s(\overline{e})},\overline{e}\in R(e)\}=\{s(h)\mid h\in B_{s(\overline{f})},\overline{f}\in B_{s(f)}\}

is exactly the set of vertices appearing in the expression of
\textbf{S}(W)
(see Lemma
6.1
(ii)), and just as before, this set corresponds to the set of clopen subsets
\overline{W}\in{\pazocal P}_{2n+3}
such that
\overline{W}\subseteq W
. Given
\overline{W}\in{\pazocal P}_{2n+3}
such that
\overline{W}\subseteq W
, we have inclusions
\overline{W}\subseteq W\subseteq\sigma(U)
and so by Lemma
3.26
(ii) there exists a unique
\overline{U}\in{\pazocal P}_{2n+2}
such that

(6.2)

\overline{U}\subseteq U\quad\text{ and }\quad\overline{W}\subseteq\sigma(\overline{U})\subseteq W\subseteq\sigma(U).

In addition, there is a unique
\overline{V}\in{\pazocal P}_{2n+2}
such that
\overline{W}\subseteq\overline{V}
, and since
\overline{W}\subseteq W\subseteq V
, we must have

(6.3)

\overline{W}\subseteq\overline{V}\subseteq W\subseteq V.

It follows that each of the terms
gh^{*}
in the sum appearing in (
6.1
) corresponds exactly to a triple
(\overline{U},\overline{W},\overline{V})
, where
\overline{W}\in{\pazocal P}_{2n+3}
,
\overline{U},\overline{V}\in{\pazocal P}_{2n+2}
, and (
6.2
) and (
6.3
) hold. We infer that the decomposition (
6.1
) corresponds, under
\varphi_{n+1}
, to the decomposition

\bigsqcup_{\{\overline{W}\in{\pazocal P}_{2n+3}\mid\overline{W}\subseteq W\}}Z((\sigma|_{\overline{U}})^{-1}(\overline{W}),1,0,\overline{W})=Z((\sigma|_{U})^{-1}(W),1,0,W).

Hence we get that
(\varphi_{n+1}\circ\phi_{n})(\tau_{(e,f)})=\varphi_{n}(\tau_{(e,f)})
, as desired.
∎

We now start a study of elements in
{\pazocal A}_{\infty}
.

Definition 6.12
.

Let
n\geq 0
and
U,V\in{\pazocal P}_{2n}
,
W\in{\pazocal P}_{2n+1}
. We say that
(U,W,V)
is an
n
-triple
if
W\subseteq V
and
W\subseteq\sigma(U)
. Note that such a triple determines an element
fe^{*}=\tau_{(f,e)}
in
{\pazocal A}_{2n}
, where
f\in R_{U}
and
e\in B_{V}
, such that
\varphi_{n}(fe^{*})=\mathds{1}_{Z((\sigma|_{U})^{-1}(W),1,0,W)}
.

In the following lemma, we describe the images by
\varphi_{n}
of some elements of
{\pazocal A}_{2n}
.

Lemma 6.13
.

Let
n\geq 0
.

(1)

Suppose that
x=f_{1}e_{1}^{*}\cdots f_{k}e_{k}^{*}\in{\pazocal A}_{2n}
, with
f_{i}
red edges and
e_{i}
blue edges, such that
s(f_{i})=s(e_{i})=W_{i}
,
r(f_{1})=U_{1}
and
r(e_{i})=r(f_{i+1})=U_{i+1}
for
1\leq i\leq k-1
. Consider the set

T_{1}:=(\sigma|_{U_{1}})^{-1}(W_{1}\cap(\sigma|_{U_{2}})^{-1}(W_{2}\cap\cdots\cap(\sigma|_{U_{k-1}})^{-1}(W_{k-1}\cap(\sigma|_{U_{k}})^{-1}(W_{k}))\cdots)).

Then

\varphi_{n}(x)=\mathds{1}_{Z(T_{1},k,0,\sigma^{k}(T_{1}))}

and

\varphi_{n}(xx^{*})=\mathds{1}_{T_{1}}.

(2)

Let
x=(f_{1}e_{1}^{*})\cdots(f_{k-1}e_{k-1}^{*})(f_{k}f_{k+1}^{*})(e_{k+2}f_{k+2}^{*})\cdots(e_{k+l}f_{k+l}^{*})\in{\pazocal A}_{2n}
, with
e_{i}
blue edges and
f_{i}
red edges, such that
s(e_{i})=s(f_{i})=W_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq k-1
and
k+2\leq i\leq k+l
,
s(f_{k})=s(f_{k+1})=W_{k}=W_{k+1}
,
f_{k}\neq f_{k+1}
,
r(f_{1})=U_{1}
,
r(e_{i})=r(f_{i+1})=U_{i+1}
for
1\leq i\leq k-1
,
r(f_{k+i})=r(e_{k+i+1})=U_{k+i}
for
1\leq i\leq l-1
and
r(f_{k+l})=U_{k+l}
. Let
T_{1}
be as in i) and set

T_{2}:=(\sigma|_{U_{k+l}})^{-1}(W_{k+l}\cap(\sigma|_{U_{k+l-1}})^{-1}(W_{k+l-1}\cap\cdots\cap(\sigma|_{U_{k+2}})^{-1}(W_{k+2}\cap(\sigma|_{U_{k+1}})^{-1}(W_{k+1}))\cdots)).

Then
T:=\sigma^{k}(T_{1})\cap\sigma^{l}(T_{2})\subseteq W_{k}
,
(\sigma|_{T_{1}})^{k}
and
(\sigma|_{T_{2}})^{l}
are injective, and, with
U:=(\sigma|_{T_{1}})^{-k}(T)
and
V:=(\sigma|_{T_{2}})^{-l}(T)
, we have

\varphi_{n}(x)=\mathds{1}_{Z(U,k,l,V)}

and

\varphi_{n}(xx^{*})=\mathds{1}_{U}.

Proof.

i)
Note that, for
y\in T_{1}
, we have
y\in U_{1}
and
\sigma(y)\in W_{1}\subseteq U_{2}
. Moreover
\sigma^{2}(y)=\sigma(\sigma(y))\in W_{2}\subseteq U_{3}
. In general we have
\sigma^{i}(y)\in W_{i}\subseteq U_{i+1}
for
1\leq i\leq k-1
, and
\sigma^{k}(y)\in W_{k}
. Since the restriction of
\sigma
to each set
U_{i}
is injective, we see that
\sigma^{k}
is injective on
T_{1}
. We have

\varphi_{n}(x)=\mathds{1}_{{\pazocal Z}},

where

{\pazocal Z}=Z((\sigma|_{U_{1}})^{-1}(W_{1}),1,0,W_{1})\cdots Z((\sigma|_{U_{k}})^{-1}(W_{k}),1,0,W_{k}).

The elements of the right-hand side of the above equality have the form
(y_{0},1,y_{1})(y_{1},1,y_{2})\cdots(y_{k-1},1,y_{k})
, where
y_{0}\in U_{1}
,
y_{1}=\sigma(y_{0})\in W_{1}\subseteq U_{2}
, and in general
y_{i}=\sigma^{i}(y_{0})\in W_{i}\subseteq U_{i+1}
for
1\leq i\leq k-1
, and
\sigma^{k}(y_{0})=y_{k}\in W_{k}
. Then we get that
y_{0}\in T_{1}
and that

(y_{0},1,y_{1})\cdots(y_{k-1},1,y_{k})=(y_{0},k,y_{k})\in Z(T_{1},k,0,\sigma^{k}(T_{1})).

Conversely, if
(y,k,z)\in Z(T_{1},k,0,\sigma^{k}(T_{1}))
, by the definition of
T_{1}
we can write
(y,k,z)
as a product of elements in
Z((\sigma|_{U_{i}})^{-1}(W_{i}),1,0,W_{i})
for all
1\leq i\leq k
.

Now,

\displaystyle\varphi_{n}(xx^{*})

\displaystyle=\varphi_{n}(x)\varphi_{n}(x)^{*}

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}_{Z(T_{1},k,0,\sigma^{k}(T_{1}))}\cdot\mathds{1}_{Z(T_{1},k,0,\sigma^{k}(T_{1}))^{-1}}

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}_{Z(T_{1},k,0,\sigma^{k}(T_{1}))\cdot Z(T_{1},k,0,\sigma^{k}(T_{1}))^{-1}}

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}_{Z(T_{1},k,0,\sigma^{k}(T_{1}))\cdot Z(\sigma^{k}(T_{1}),0,k,T_{1})}

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}_{T_{1}}.

The proof of
ii)
is similar to the proof of
i)
, and is left to the reader.
∎

This result is a first step towards finding a canonical expression for an element of
{\pazocal A}_{\infty}
. The next step is to show a partial injectivity result for
\varphi
.

Lemma 6.14
.

The following statements hold.

(1)

Let
x=f_{1}e_{1}^{*}\cdots f_{k}e_{k}^{*}\in{\pazocal A}_{2n}
be as in Lemma
6.13
(i). Then
\phi_{n,\infty}(x)\neq 0
if and only if
\varphi_{n}(x)\neq 0
.

(2)

Let
x=(f_{1}e_{1}^{*})\cdots(f_{k-1}e_{k-1}^{*})(f_{k}f_{k+1}^{*})(e_{k+2}f_{k+2}^{*})\cdots(e_{k+l}f_{k+l}^{*})\in{\pazocal A}_{2n}
be as in Lemma
6.13
(ii). Then
\phi_{n,\infty}(x)\neq 0
if and only if
\varphi_{n}(x)\neq 0
.

Proof.

First, observe that for any term
x
as in
i)
or
ii)
, we have
\phi_{n,\infty}(x)\neq 0
if and only if
\phi_{n,\infty}(xx^{*})\neq 0
. This is due to the fact that
{\pazocal A}_{\infty}
is tame (Theorem
6.7
), so that
\phi_{n,\infty}(x)
is a partial isometry in
{\pazocal A}_{\infty}
. Similarly,
\varphi_{n}(x)\neq 0
if and only if
\varphi_{n}(xx^{*})\neq 0
.

Let us prove
i)
. If
\varphi_{n}(x)\neq 0
then
\phi_{n,\infty}(x)\neq 0
, because
\varphi_{n}(x)=\varphi(\phi_{n,\infty}(x))
.

Suppose now that
\phi_{n,\infty}(xx^{*})\neq 0
, so in particular
\phi_{n,m}(xx^{*})\neq 0
for all
m>n
. We will show that
T_{1}
is non-empty. We proceed by induction on
k
.

For
k=1
, we have
x=f_{1}e_{1}^{*}
and
xx^{*}=f_{1}f_{1}^{*}
. In this case
T_{1}=(\sigma|_{U_{1}})^{-1}(W_{1})\neq\emptyset
, because
W_{1}\neq\emptyset
and
W_{1}\subseteq\sigma(U_{1})
.

Suppose now that the result is true for all lengths up to
k-1
. Take
x=f_{1}e_{1}^{*}\cdots f_{k}e_{k}^{*}
. Observe that

xx^{*}=f_{1}e_{1}^{*}\cdots e_{k-1}^{*}f_{k}f_{k}^{*}e_{k-1}\cdots e_{1}f_{1}^{*}=(f_{1}e_{1}^{*})yy^{*}(e_{1}^{*}f_{1}),

where
y=f_{2}e_{2}^{*}\cdots f_{k}e_{k}^{*}
. Recall from Lemma
6.6
that
\phi_{n,n+k}(xx^{*})=\widetilde{\phi}_{2n,2n+2k}(xx^{*})
is a sum of a finite number of distinct vertices in
F_{2n+2k}^{0,0}
, and likewise
\phi_{n,n+k-1}(yy^{*})=\widetilde{\phi}_{2n,2n+2k-2}(yy^{*})
is a sum of a finite number of distinct vertices in
F_{2n+2k-2}^{0,0}
. We will show, using induction, that any of the open compact sets corresponding to the vertices appearing in the expression of
\phi_{n,n+k}(xx^{*})
is contained in
T_{1}
. Our assumption that
\phi_{n,\infty}(xx^{*})\neq 0
would then imply that
T_{1}\neq\emptyset
.

Suppose that
\phi_{n,n+k-1}(yy^{*})=\sum_{v}v
for distinct vertices
v\in F_{2n+2k-2}^{0,0}
and that each of the open compact subsets of
X
represented by these vertices is contained in

T^{\prime}:=(\sigma|_{U_{2}})^{-1}(W_{2}\cap(\sigma|_{U_{3}})^{-1}(W_{3}\cap\cdots\cap(\sigma|_{U_{k-1}})^{-1}(W_{k-1}\cap(\sigma|_{U_{k}})^{-1}(W_{k}))\cdots)).

By Lemma
6.4
(ii), we can write
\phi_{n,n+k-1}(e_{1})=\sum_{g\in N}g
, where
N
is a set of blue edges in
F_{2n+2k-2}
with mutually distinct sources. So we get

\displaystyle\phi_{n,n+k-1}(xx^{*})

\displaystyle=\phi_{n,n+k-1}(f_{1})\Big(\sum_{g\in N}g^{*}\Big)\Big(\sum_{v}v\Big)\Big(\sum_{g\in N}g\Big)\phi_{n,n+k-1}(f_{1}^{*})

\displaystyle=\phi_{n,n+k-1}(f_{1})\Big(\sum_{g\in N^{\prime}}s(g)\Big)\phi_{n,n+k-1}(f_{1}^{*}),

where
N^{\prime}
is a non-empty subset of
N
. Again by Lemma
6.4
(ii), we can write
\widetilde{\phi}_{2n,2n+2k-1}(f_{1})=\sum_{h\in L}h^{*}
, where
L
is a subset of red edges in
F_{2n+2k-1}
, whose sources are mutually disjoint, and such that all edges in
L
with the same range belong to the same element of the partition
D
. It follows that

\widetilde{\phi}_{2n,2n+2k-1}(xx^{*})=\Big(\sum_{h\in L}h^{*}\Big)\Big(\sum_{g\in N^{\prime}}s(g)\Big)\Big(\sum_{h\in L}h\Big)=\sum_{h\in L^{\prime}}s(h),

where
L^{\prime}
is a subset of
L
. Note that we have
0\neq\phi_{n,n+k}(xx^{*})=\sum_{h\in L^{\prime}}s(h)
, so
L^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
. Now take any
h\in L^{\prime}
and set
Z=s(h)
,
W=r(h)
. There is
g\in N^{\prime}
such that
r(h)=s(g)
. Moreover we have that
r(g)=V
, where
V\subseteq T^{\prime}
by our induction hypothesis. Since
g
is a blue edge we have
W=r(h)=s(g)\subseteq V\subseteq T^{\prime}
, so that
W\subseteq T^{\prime}
. Since
h
is a red edge in
F_{2n+2k-1}
, we must have
Z\subseteq\sigma^{-1}(W)
. On the other hand, since
xx^{*}\leq f_{1}f_{1}^{*}\leq r(f_{1})=U_{1}
, we have that
s(h)\leq\phi_{n,n+k}(xx^{*})\leq\phi_{n,n+k}(r(f_{1}))
, and we conclude that
Z\subseteq U_{1}
. Similarly
e_{1}^{*}yy^{*}e_{1}\leq s(e_{1})=W_{1}
and thus
s(g)\leq\phi_{n,n+k-1}(e_{1}^{*}yy^{*}e_{1})\leq\phi_{n,n+k-1}(s(e_{1}))
implies that
W\subseteq W_{1}
. We thus have that

W\subseteq W_{1}\cap T^{\prime}\qquad\text{and}\qquad Z\subseteq U_{1}\cap\sigma^{-1}(W)\subseteq U_{1}\cap\sigma^{-1}(W_{1}\cap T^{\prime}),

so we conclude that
Z\subseteq(\sigma|_{U_{1}})^{-1}(W_{1}\cap T^{\prime})=T_{1}
, as desired.

The similar proof of
ii)
is left to the reader.
∎

We can now state our result on canonical forms.

Theorem 6.15
.

Each element
a\in{\pazocal A}_{\infty}
can be represented as
a=\phi_{n,\infty}(b)
for some
n\geq 0
and
b\in{\pazocal A}_{2n}
such that
b
is a finite linear combination of the following types of elements:

(1)

Vertices
p_{U}
, with
U\in{\pazocal P}_{2n}
.

(2)

Terms of the form
x=f_{1}e_{1}^{*}\cdots f_{k}e_{k}^{*}
as in Lemma
6.13
(i), with
\varphi_{n}(x)\neq 0
.

(3)

Adjoints of the terms from (B).

(4)

Terms of the form
x=(f_{1}e_{1}^{*})\cdots(f_{k-1}e_{k-1}^{*})(f_{k}f_{k+1}^{*})(e_{k+2}f_{k+2}^{*})\cdots(e_{k+l}f_{k+l}^{*})
as in Lemma
6.13
(ii), where
k,l\geq 1
and with
\varphi_{n}(x)\neq 0
. When
k=l=1
, the corresponding term must be interpreted as
f_{1}f_{2}^{*}
, where
f_{1},f_{2}
are distinct red edges with the same source.

Note that the family (D) is closed under adjoints.

Proof.

Let
a
be a nonzero element of
{\pazocal A}_{\infty}
. There exists
m\geq 0
and
c\in{\pazocal A}_{2m}
such that
\phi_{m,\infty}(c)=a
. The element
c
will be a linear combination of vertices
p_{U}
, for
U\in{\pazocal P}_{2m}
, and terms which are products of elements
ef^{*}
, where
e
and
f
are red or blue edges such that
s(e)=s(f)\in F_{2m}^{0,1}
.

Observe that a term
ee^{*}
, where
e
is either a blue or a red edge, can be simplified by applying
\phi_{m}
, since
\phi_{m}(ee^{*})
is a sum of distinct vertices in
{\pazocal A}_{2m+2}
by Lemma
6.6
, so of the form (A). We can thus assume that we do not have terms of the form
ee^{*}
in our product. Moreover, since
s(e_{1})=s(e_{2})
implies
e_{1}=e_{2}
for
e_{1},e_{2}
blue edges, we may, in view of the above, assume that no terms
e_{1}e_{2}^{*}
, with both
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
blue edges, appear in the product.

Suppose that we have a product
(fe^{*})(gh^{*})
such that
e,g
are both blue edges. By our assumptions, we necessarily have that both
f
and
h
are red edges. Then we get that

(fe^{*})(gh^{*})=\delta_{e,g}(fh^{*}).

So either the product is
0
or it can be simplified to a product containing a term
fh^{*}
, with both
f
and
h
red edges. By the previous paragraph, we can assume that
f\neq h
. We can thus simplify these terms.

Suppose now that we have a product
(ef^{*})(gh^{*})
such that
f,g
are both red edges. Then we get, as before,

(ef^{*})(gh^{*})=\delta_{f,g}(eh^{*}).

If both
e
and
h
are red edges, then we can assume that they satisfy
e\neq h
(otherwise we can reduce the length of the word by applying
\phi_{m}
). If
e
and
h
are both blue edges, then either
eh^{*}=0
or
e=h
. In the latter case, we can simplify by applying
\phi_{m}
. If
e
is blue and
h
is red the product will be either
0
or of the form
eh^{*}
, and similarly when
e
is red and
h
is blue. Hence these terms can be simplified too.

In conclusion, the only products that cannot be simplified are those where all the factors are of the form
e_{i}f_{i}^{*}
, where all
e_{i}
are blue and all
f_{i}
are red (terms of the form (C)), or all
e_{i}
are red and all
f_{i}
are blue (terms of the form (B)), and those of the form (D).
∎

We are now ready to show the main result of this section.

Theorem 6.16
.

With the previous notation, we have that the map

\varphi\colon{\pazocal A}_{\infty}\to A_{K}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma))

is a graded
*
-isomorphism.

Proof.

In view of Proposition
6.11
, it only remains to show that
\varphi
is bijective. We first show it is surjective. For this, it is enough to check that all the characteristic functions of sets of the form
Z(U,k,l,V)
belong to the image of
\varphi
. Suppose therefore that
U
and
V
are open compact subsets of
X
such that
\sigma^{k}|_{U}
and
\sigma^{l}|_{V}
are injective and that
\sigma^{k}(U)=\sigma^{l}(V)
. Observe that

Z(U,k,l,V)=Z(U,k,0,\sigma^{k}(U))\cdot Z(V,l,0,\sigma^{l}(V))^{-1},

so that it suffices to show that
Z(U,k,0,\sigma^{k}(U))
belongs to the image of
\varphi
.

There is some
n\geq 0
such that
U
can be written as a disjoint union of sets in
{\pazocal P}_{2n}
. Hence we can assume that
U\in{\pazocal P}_{2n}
. We claim that

(6.4)

U=\bigsqcup_{(W_{1},\dots,W_{k})\in{\pazocal B}}(\sigma|_{U_{1}})^{-1}(W_{1}\cap(\sigma|_{U_{2}})^{-1}(W_{2}\cap\cdots\cap(\sigma|_{U_{k-1}})^{-1}(W_{k-1}\cap(\sigma|_{U_{k}})^{-1}(W_{k}))\cdots)),

where

{\pazocal B}=\{(W_{1},\dots,W_{k})\in{\pazocal P}_{2n+1}^{k}\mid W_{i}\subseteq\sigma(U_{i})\text{ and }W_{i}\subseteq U_{i+1}\text{ for }1\leq i\leq k,\text{ for unique }U_{i}\text{'s}\text{ with }U_{1}=U\}.

First, we observe that
T\cap T^{\prime}=\emptyset
, where
T
and
T^{\prime}
are sets as in the right-hand side of (
6.4
) corresponding to different choices
(W_{1},\dots,W_{k})
and
(W_{1}^{\prime},\dots,W_{k}^{\prime})
. Indeed, suppose that
T\cap T^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
and take
x\in T\cap T^{\prime}
. Then
x\in U_{1}
and
\sigma(x)\in W_{1}\cap W_{1}^{\prime}
. Since
W_{1},W_{1}^{\prime}
belong to the partition
{\pazocal P}_{2n+1}
of
X
, we obtain that
W_{1}=W_{1}^{\prime}
. This in turn implies that
U_{2}=U_{2}^{\prime}
. Now note that
\sigma^{2}(x)\in W_{2}\cap W_{2}^{\prime}
and thus
W_{2}=W_{2}^{\prime}
and
U_{3}=U_{3}^{\prime}
. Continuing in this way, we obtain that
(W_{1},\dots,W_{k})=(W_{1}^{\prime},\dots,W_{k}^{\prime})
.

Take now
x\in U=U_{1}
. We have

\sigma(U_{1})=\bigsqcup_{\{W\in{\pazocal P}_{2n+1}\mid W\subseteq\sigma(U_{1})\}}W,

so that there is a unique
W_{1}\in{\pazocal P}_{2n+1}
such that
\sigma(x)\in W_{1}
. Then there is a unique
U_{2}\in{\pazocal P}_{2n}
such that
W_{1}\subseteq U_{2}
. Since
\sigma(x)\in U_{2}
, by the same argument we get
W_{2}\in{\pazocal P}_{2n+1}
such that
W_{2}\subseteq\sigma(U_{2})
and
\sigma^{2}(x)=\sigma(\sigma(x))\in W_{2}
. In this way, we build a family of sets
W_{1},\dots,W_{k}
and
U_{2},\dots,U_{k}
satisfying the specified conditions and such that

x\in(\sigma|_{U_{1}})^{-1}(W_{1}\cap(\sigma|_{U_{2}})^{-1}(W_{2}\cap\cdots\cap(\sigma|_{U_{k-1}})^{-1}(W_{k-1}\cap(\sigma|_{U_{k}})^{-1}(W_{k}))\cdots)).

This shows our claim.

Note that each
(U_{i},W_{i},U_{i+1})
is an
n
-triple in the sense of Definition
6.12
, hence it gives rise to an element
f_{i}e_{i}^{*}
in
{\pazocal A}_{2n}
, where
f_{i}\in R_{U_{i}}
,
e_{i}\in B_{U_{i+1}}
and
\varphi_{n}(f_{i}e_{i}^{*})=\mathds{1}_{Z((\sigma|_{U_{i}})^{-1}(W_{i}),1,0,W_{i})}
. Therefore we see that each sequence
(W_{1},W_{2},\dots,W_{k})\in{\pazocal P}_{2n+1}^{k}
satisfying the conditions stated above, and such that the corresponding set
T
is non-empty, determines an element
x=f_{1}e_{1}^{*}\cdots f_{k}e_{k}^{*}
of the form (B) from Theorem
6.15
, with
\varphi_{n}(x)=\mathds{1}_{Z(T,k,0,\sigma^{k}(T))}
by Lemma
6.13
. Using this observation and (
6.4
), the surjectivity of
\varphi
follows.

Finally we show that
\varphi
is injective. Since
\varphi
is a graded homomorphism, it is enough to show that it is injective in each graded component. Suppose that we have a nonzero homogeneous element, say
a
, in
{\pazocal A}_{\infty}
. By Theorem
6.15
, there exists some
n\geq 0
and an element
b\in{\pazocal A}_{2n}
which is a linear combination of terms of the forms (A), (B), (C), (D) such that
a=\phi_{n,\infty}(b)
. Of course we can assume that
\phi_{n,\infty}
does not annihilate any of these terms, which by Lemma
6.14
implies that
\varphi_{n}
also does not annihilate any of them. It is sufficient to show that the compact open bisections corresponding to two distinct terms of the same degree are disjoint. We will treat only the case where the two terms are of the form (D), i.e. satisfy the conditions in Lemma
6.13
(ii). The cases where other types of terms arise are treated in a similar way.

Suppose that we have two terms

\alpha=(f_{1}e_{1}^{*})\cdots(f_{k-1}e_{k-1}^{*})(f_{k}f_{k+1}^{*})(e_{k+2}f_{k+2}^{*})\cdots(e_{k+l}f_{k+l}^{*}),

\beta=f^{\prime}_{1}(e^{\prime}_{1})^{*}\cdots f^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}-1}(e^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}-1})^{*}f^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}(f^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}+1})^{*}e^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}+2}(f^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}+2})^{*}\cdots e^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}+l^{\prime}}(f^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}+l^{\prime}})^{*}

in
{\pazocal A}_{2n}
as in Lemma
6.13
(ii), and such that
k-l=k^{\prime}-l^{\prime}
. We will assume that
k^{\prime}\geq k
, so that
k^{\prime}=k+r
and
l^{\prime}=l+r
for some
r\geq 0
. Let

W_{1},\dots,W_{k}=W_{k+1},W_{k+2},\dots,W_{k+l}\quad\text{and}\quad U_{1},\dots,U_{k},U_{k+1},\dots,U_{k+l}

be the sets associated to
\alpha
, and

W_{1}^{\prime},\dots,W^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}=W^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}+1},W^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}+2},\dots,W^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}+l^{\prime}}\quad\text{and}\quad U^{\prime}_{1},\dots,U^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}},U^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}+1},\dots,U^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}+l^{\prime}}

be the sets associated to
\beta
. Let
T_{1}
,
T_{2}
,
T
,
U=(\sigma|_{T_{1}})^{-k}(T)
and
V=(\sigma|_{T_{2}})^{-l}(T)
be the sets associated to
\alpha
defined in Lemma
6.13
(ii), and let
T_{1}^{\prime}
,
T_{2}^{\prime}
,
T^{\prime}
,
U^{\prime}=(\sigma|_{T^{\prime}_{1}})^{-k^{\prime}}(T^{\prime})
and
V^{\prime}=(\sigma|_{T^{\prime}_{2}})^{-l^{\prime}}(T^{\prime})
be the ones corresponding to
\beta
. Suppose, by way of contradiction, that

Z(U,k,l,V)\cap Z(U^{\prime},k^{\prime},l^{\prime},V^{\prime})\neq\emptyset,

and let
(x,k-l,y)
be an element in this intersection. Then
x\in U\cap U^{\prime}
,
y\in V\cap V^{\prime}
, and
\sigma^{k}(x)=\sigma^{l}(y)
, which automatically implies
\sigma^{k^{\prime}}(x)=\sigma^{l^{\prime}}(y)
because
k^{\prime}=k+r
and
l^{\prime}=l+r
with
r\geq 0
. Since
U\cap U^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
, necessarily
U_{1}=U_{1}^{\prime}
, and also
U_{k+l}=U^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}+l^{\prime}}
because
V\cap V^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
. Now observe that
\sigma(x)\in W_{1}\cap W_{1}^{\prime}
, so that
W_{1}=W_{1}^{\prime}
and
U_{2}=U_{2}^{\prime}
. Then we get
\sigma^{2}(x)\in W_{2}\cap W_{2}^{\prime}
, so
W_{2}=W_{2}^{\prime}
and
U_{3}=U_{3}^{\prime}
, and so on up to
U_{k}=U^{\prime}_{k}
and
W_{k}=W_{k}^{\prime}
. Starting at the other end, we have
\sigma(y)\in W_{k+l}\cap W^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}+l^{\prime}}
, so that
W_{k+l}=W^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}+l^{\prime}}
and
U_{k+l-1}=U^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}+l^{\prime}-1}
. Then since
\sigma^{2}(y)\in W_{k+l-1}\cap W^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}+l^{\prime}-1}
, we get
W_{k+l-1}=W^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}+l^{\prime}-1}
and
U_{k+l-2}=U^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}+l^{\prime}-2}
. Continuing in this way, we get that
W_{k+l-i}=W^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}+l^{\prime}-i}
and
U_{k+l-i}=U^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}+l^{\prime}-i}
for all
0\leq i\leq l-1
. We now distinguish two cases.

(1)

In case
r=0
we conclude that all sets
W_{i}
and all sets
U_{i}
agree with
W_{i}^{\prime}
and
U_{i}^{\prime}
respectively, so that
\alpha=\beta
.

(2)

If
r>0
, then observe that

\sigma^{k}(x)=\sigma^{l}(y)\in W_{k}^{\prime}\cap W^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}+l^{\prime}-l+1},

so that
W_{k}^{\prime}=W^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}+l^{\prime}-l+1}=W^{\prime}_{k+2r+1}
. Therefore
U^{\prime}_{k+1}=U^{\prime}_{k+2r}
. Since
\sigma^{k+1}(x)=\sigma^{l+1}(y)\in W^{\prime}_{k+1}\cap W^{\prime}_{k+2r}
, we get that
W^{\prime}_{k+1}=W^{\prime}_{k+2r}
and thus
U^{\prime}_{k+2}=U^{\prime}_{k+2r-1}
. Continuing in this way, we see that
W^{\prime}_{k+r-1}=W^{\prime}_{k+r+2}
and thus
U^{\prime}_{k+r}=U^{\prime}_{k+r+1}
, that is, we obtain that
U^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}=U^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}+1}
. But since
f^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}\neq f^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}+1}
and
s(f^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}})=s(f^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}+1})
, we necessarily have that
U^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}=r(f^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}})\neq r(f^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}+1})=U^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}+1}
, and we obtain a contradiction. Therefore the case where
r>0
is not possible.

The conclusion is that, whenever
Z(U,k,l,V)\cap Z(U^{\prime},k^{\prime},l^{\prime},V^{\prime})\neq\emptyset
holds, we necessarily have
\alpha=\beta
, as desired. The proof is complete.
∎

We consider now the situation for the corresponding
C^{*}
-algebras. Writing
C_{n}=C^{*}(F_{n},D^{n})
, we have that, by definition,
C_{n}
is the enveloping
C^{*}
-algebra of
L_{n}
. Hence we get a sequence of
*
-homomorphisms
C_{n}\to C_{n+1}
, also denoted by
\tilde{\phi}_{n}
. Set

C_{\infty}=\varinjlim(C_{n},\tilde{\phi}_{n})
, where the above inductive limit is the colimit of
\{(C_{n},\tilde{\phi}_{n})\}
in the cateory of
C^{*}
-algebras.
We now set

{\pazocal B}_{2n}:=p_{2n}C_{2n}p_{2n}\quad\text{ and }\quad{\pazocal B}_{\infty}=\varinjlim({\pazocal B}_{2n},\phi_{n}),

where, as before,
\phi_{n}
is the restriction of
\tilde{\phi}_{2n+1}\circ\tilde{\phi}_{2n}
to
{\pazocal B}_{2n}
.

We can now state our main result for
C^{*}
-algebras.

Theorem 6.17
.

With the above notation, there is a unique
*
-isomorphism
\overline{\varphi}\colon{\pazocal B}_{\infty}\to C^{*}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma))
such that the following diagram

(6.5)

\textstyle{{\pazocal A}_{\infty}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kern-20.0pt{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\kern-20.0pt{\cong}}
\textstyle{A_{{\mathbb{C}}}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\pazocal B}_{\infty}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kern-20.0pt{\overline{\varphi}}}
\scriptstyle{\kern-20.0pt{\cong}}
\textstyle{C^{*}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma))}

is commutative.

Proof.

It is a simple matter to check that
C_{\infty}
is the enveloping
C^{*}
-algebra of
L_{\infty}
.

Just as in Lemma
6.8
, we have a natural
*
-isomorphism
\overline{\lambda}\colon{\pazocal B}_{\infty}\to pC_{\infty}p
, such that the following diagram

(6.6)

\textstyle{{\pazocal A}_{\infty}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kern-7.0pt{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\kern-7.0pt{\cong}}
\textstyle{pL_{\infty}p\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\pazocal B}_{\infty}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kern-7.0pt{\overline{\lambda}}}
\scriptstyle{\kern-7.0pt{\cong}}
\textstyle{pC_{\infty}p}

is commutative. Since there is a partial isometry
s\in L_{\infty}
such that
ss^{*}=p
and
s^{*}s=1-p
(take for instance
s=\widetilde{\phi}_{0,\infty}(\sum_{e\in B}e)
, where
B
is the set of blue edges in
F^{1,0}
), it follows that
L_{\infty}\cong pL_{\infty}p\otimes M_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
, and
C_{\infty}\cong pC_{\infty}p\otimes M_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
in a compatible way. Since
C_{\infty}
is the enveloping
C^{*}
-algebra of
L_{\infty}
, it follows that
pC_{\infty}p
is the enveloping
C^{*}
-algebra of
pL_{\infty}p
. We infer from (
6.6
) that
{\pazocal B}_{\infty}
is the enveloping
C^{*}
-algebra of
{\pazocal A}_{\infty}
. The result follows from this and Theorem
6.16
, because
C^{*}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma))
is the enveloping
C^{*}
-algebra of
A_{{\mathbb{C}}}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma))
(see Definition
2.4
).
∎

6.2.
Functoriality properties

In this section we show that the Deaconu-Renault construction is indeed a functor from
LHomeo
to
AHGrd
, where
AHGrd
is the category whose objects are all the second countable, ample Hausdorff groupoids, and whose morphisms are the continuous groupoid homomorphisms. We further show that, whenever
\psi\colon Y\to X
is the universal continuous
(E,C)
-equivariant map induced by an
(E,C)
-structure on
Y
, there are induced
*
-homomorphisms at the levels of Steinberg algebras and
C^{*}
-algebras of the corresponding groupoids, respectively.

The following lemma, which gives the mentioned functoriality, may be well-known to experts.

Lemma 6.18
.

Let
(X,\sigma)
and
(Y,\rho)
be objects in
LHomeo
, and let
\psi\colon X\to Y
be an equivariant continuous map. Then the induced map
\psi_{*}\colon{\pazocal G}(X,\sigma)\to{\pazocal G}(Y,\rho)
defined by

\psi_{*}(x,m-n,y)=(\psi(x),m-n,\psi(y))

for
(x,m-n,y)\in{\pazocal G}(X,\sigma)
, is a morphism in
AHGrd
. Hence the Deaconu-Renault groupoid construction can be extended to a functor
\emph{\textsf{DR}}\colon\emph{\textsf{LHomeo}}\to\emph{\textsf{AHGrd}}
.

Proof.

It is clear that
\psi_{*}
is well-defined and an algebraic morphism of groupoids. Thus we only need to show that
\psi_{*}
is continuous.

Observe that the restriction of
\psi_{*}
to the space of units
X
agrees with
\psi
, hence it is continuous. Let
\alpha=(x,m-n,y)\in{\pazocal G}(X,\sigma)
, and let
U
be an open compact subset of
{\pazocal G}(Y,\rho)
containing
\psi_{*}(\alpha)
. Since
\rho^{i}
is a local homeomorphism for each
i\geq 0
, we can find open neighborhoods
U_{1}
of
\psi(x)
and
U_{2}
of
\psi(y)
such that
\rho^{m}|_{U_{1}}
and
\rho^{n}|_{U_{2}}
are injective. Replacing
U
with
U\cap Z(U_{1},m,n,U_{2})
, we can assume that
U\subseteq Z(U_{1},m,n,U_{2})
, and in particular that
U
is an open compact bisection. Note that
s(U)\subseteq U_{2}
and
r(U)\subseteq U_{1}
.

By continuity of
\psi
, we can take open neighborhoods
U_{1}^{\prime}
and
U_{2}^{\prime}
of
x
and
y
in
X
, respectively, such that
\psi(U_{1}^{\prime})\subseteq r(U)
and
\psi(U_{2}^{\prime})\subseteq s(U)
. Then
Z(U_{1}^{\prime},m,n,U_{2}^{\prime})
is an open subset of
{\pazocal G}(X,\sigma)
, and clearly
\alpha\in Z(U_{1}^{\prime},m,n,U_{2}^{\prime})
. Now if
\gamma\in Z(U_{1}^{\prime},m,n,U_{2}^{\prime})
, then
r(\psi_{*}(\gamma))\in r(U)
, and since
U
is contained in the bisection
Z(U_{1},m,n,U_{2})
we conclude that
\psi_{*}(\gamma)\in U
. This shows that

\psi_{*}(Z(U_{1}^{\prime},m,n,U_{2}^{\prime}))\subseteq U,

which gives the continuity of
\psi_{*}
. The last statement follows straightforwardly.
∎

Notation 6.19
.

Let
(E,C)
be an
l
-diagram, or a layer in an
l
-diagram, and let
v
be a vertex in
E
. We will denote by
s_{\mathrm{red}}^{-1}(v)
the set of red edges
f
in
E^{1}
such that
s(f)=v
.

If
{\pazocal G}
is a groupoid and
x\in{\pazocal G}^{0}
, we denote by
{\pazocal G}x
the set of elements
\gamma\in{\pazocal G}
such that
s(\gamma)=x
.

Lemma 6.20
.

Let
(Y,\rho)\in\emph{\textsf{LHomeo}}
and let
(E,C)
be a generalized finite shift graph. Suppose that we have an
(E,C)
-structure on
Y
, and let
\psi\colon Y\to X
be the unique
(E,C)
-equivariant continuous map, where
(X,\sigma)
is the generalized finite shift associated to
(E,C)
. Then for
y\in Y
, the map
\psi_{*}
from Lemma
6.18
induces a bijection from
{\pazocal G}(Y,\rho)y
onto
{\pazocal G}(X,\sigma)\psi(y)
.

Proof.

We start by fixing the notation. We will denote the natural
(E,C)
-structure on
X
by
(\{H_{e}\}_{e\in B},\{H_{f}\}_{f\in R})
, where
B
and
R
are the sets of blue and red edges of
E
, respectively. For
f\in R
, we have a homeomorphism
\theta_{f}\colon H_{f}\to H_{e}
, where
e
is the unique blue edge such that
s(e)=s(f)
, and where
\theta_{f}
is just the restriction of
\sigma
to
H_{f}
. We will also denote by
(\{Z_{e}\}_{e\in B},\{Z_{f}\}_{f\in R})
the open compact sets corresponding to the
(E,C)
-structure on
Y
, and
\eta_{f}\colon Z_{f}\to Z_{e}
for the corresponding homeomorphisms, which are the restrictions of
\rho
to
Z_{f}
. We recall from Definition
5.22
that we have decompositions
Y=\bigsqcup_{e\in B}Z_{e}=\bigsqcup_{f\in R}Z_{f}
such that, for every
v\in E^{0,0}
, we have
\bigsqcup_{e\in B_{v}}Z_{e}=\bigsqcup_{f\in R_{v}}Z_{f}
, and, for each
f\in R
,
\rho|_{Z_{f}}
is injective and
\rho(Z_{f})=Z_{e}
, where
e
is the unique blue edge such that
s(e)=s(f)
.

We use the description of the map
\psi\colon Y\to X
given in Remark
5.24
. It is clear that, given
y\in Y
, the map
\psi_{*}
induces a well-defined map
{\pazocal G}(Y,\rho)y\to{\pazocal G}(X,\sigma)\psi(y)
. Observe that

{\pazocal G}(Y,\rho)y=\{(z,m-n,y)\in{\pazocal G}(Y,\rho)\mid\rho^{m}(z)=\rho^{n}(y)\}.

Moreover, when
\rho^{m}(z)=\rho^{n}(y)
and
n,m>0
, we can obviously assume that
\rho^{m-1}(z)\neq\rho^{n-1}(y)
.

We now give a description of the elements
(z,m-n,y)
as above. Suppose that
y\in Y
and that
\rho^{m}(z)=\rho^{n}(y)
, where
n,m>0
and
\rho^{m-1}(z)\neq\rho^{n-1}(y)
(the cases where
n=0
or
m=0
are simpler and left to the reader). Let
g_{1}\in R
be the unique red edge such that
y\in Z_{g_{1}}
. Then we have
\eta_{g_{1}}(y)=\rho(y)\in Z_{g_{2}}
for a unique
g_{2}\in R
, and continuing in this way we find a sequence
g_{1},\dots,g_{n}
of red edges such that
\rho^{i}(y)\in Z_{g_{i+1}}
for
0\leq i\leq n-1
. Now
\rho^{n}(y)=\rho(\rho^{n-1}(y))\in\rho(Z_{g_{n}})=Z_{e}
, where
e
is the unique blue edge such that
s(e)=s(g_{n})
. Set
e_{m}:=e
for notational convenience. We also have
\rho(\rho^{m-1}(z))=\rho^{m}(z)=\rho^{n}(y)
, and hence there exists a unique
f_{m}\in s_{\mathrm{red}}^{-1}(s(e_{m}))
such that
\rho^{m-1}(z)\in Z_{f_{m}}
. Note that
f_{m}\neq g_{n}
because
\rho^{m-1}(z)\neq\rho^{n-1}(y)
. Now
\rho(\rho^{m-2}(z))\in Z_{e_{m-1}}
for a unique blue edge
e_{m-1}
, and so
\rho^{m-2}(z)\in Z_{f_{m-1}}
for a unique red edge
f_{m-1}\in s_{\mathrm{red}}^{-1}(s(e_{m-1}))
. In this way, we get unique sequences of blue edges
e_{1},\dots,e_{m}
and red edges
f_{1},\dots,f_{m}
, such that
f_{i}\in s_{\mathrm{red}}^{-1}(s(e_{i}))
for
1\leq i\leq m
, and
\rho^{i}(z)\in Z_{f_{i+1}}
for
0\leq i\leq m-1
. Note that the sequence of red edges
f_{1},\dots,f_{m},g_{1},\dots,g_{n}
satisfies the conditions given in Construction
5.19
, so that we may consider the element
a_{f_{1}}^{-1}\cdots a_{f_{m}}^{-1}a_{g_{n}}\cdots a_{g_{1}}\in\mathbb{F}(A)
.

Now observe that

(6.7)

\eta_{f_{1}}^{-1}\circ\cdots\circ\eta_{f_{m}}^{-1}\circ\eta_{g_{n}}\circ\cdots\circ\eta_{g_{1}}(y)=z,

so that certainly
y\in\text{Dom}(\eta_{f_{1}}^{-1}\circ\cdots\circ\eta_{f_{m}}^{-1}\circ\eta_{g_{n}}\circ\cdots\circ\eta_{g_{1}})
.
Conversely, given a sequence
f_{1},\dots,f_{m},g_{1},\dots,g_{n}
satisfying the conditions given in Construction
5.19
and such that
y\in\text{Dom}(\eta_{f_{1}}^{-1}\circ\cdots\circ\eta_{f_{m}}^{-1}\circ\eta_{g_{n}}\circ\cdots\circ\eta_{g_{1}})
, the element
z
defined by equation (
6.7
) satisfies that
\rho^{m}(z)=\rho^{n}(y)
and that
\rho^{m-1}(z)\neq\rho^{n-1}(y)
, thus
(z,m-n,y)\in{\pazocal G}(Y,\rho)
.

We now have, for
(z,m-n,y)\in{\pazocal G}(Y,\rho)y
and corresponding red edges
f_{1},\dots,f_{m},g_{1},\dots,g_{n}
as above,

a_{f_{1}}^{-1}\cdots a_{f_{m}}^{-1}a_{g_{n}}\cdots a_{g_{1}}\in\psi(y)

and, by the
(E,C)
-equivariance of
\psi
,

\psi(z)=\theta_{f_{1}}^{-1}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{f_{m}}^{-1}\circ\theta_{g_{n}}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{g_{1}}(\psi(y)).

To show that
\psi_{*}|_{{\pazocal G}(Y,\rho)y}
is surjective onto
{\pazocal G}(X,\sigma)\psi(y)
, observe that the above argument applied to
(X,\sigma)
gives that any element in
{\pazocal G}(X,\sigma)\psi(y)
is of the form

(\theta_{f_{1}}^{-1}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{f_{m}}^{-1}\circ\theta_{g_{n}}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{g_{1}}(\psi(y)),m-n,\psi(y)),

where
a_{f_{1}}^{-1}\cdots a_{f_{m}}^{-1}a_{g_{n}}\cdots a_{g_{1}}\in\psi(y)
. Then by the description of
\psi
in Remark
5.24
we have that

y\in\text{Dom}(\eta_{f_{1}}^{-1}\circ\cdots\circ\eta_{f_{m}}^{-1}\circ\eta_{g_{n}}\circ\cdots\circ\eta_{g_{1}}),

and then
\psi_{*}(z,m-n,y)=(\theta_{f_{1}}^{-1}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{f_{m}}^{-1}\circ\theta_{g_{n}}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{g_{1}}(\psi(y)),m-n,\psi(y))
, where
z
is given by equation (
6.7
). So we get the surjectivity.

Finally, to show that
\psi_{*}|_{{\pazocal G}(Y,\rho)y}
is injective, suppose that

\displaystyle\psi_{*}(z_{1},m-n,y)

\displaystyle=(\theta_{f_{1}}^{-1}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{f_{m}}^{-1}\circ\theta_{g_{n}}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{g_{1}}(\psi(y)),m-n,\psi(y))
