\displaystyle=(\theta_{f_{1}^{\prime}}^{-1}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{f_{m^{\prime}}^{\prime}}^{-1}\circ\theta_{g_{n^{\prime}}^{\prime}}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{g_{1}^{\prime}}(\psi(y)),m^{\prime}-n^{\prime},\psi(y))

\displaystyle=\psi_{*}(z_{2},m^{\prime}-n^{\prime},y),

for sequences of red edges
f_{1},\dots,f_{m},g_{1},\dots,g_{n}
and
f_{1}^{\prime},\dots,f_{m^{\prime}}^{\prime},g_{1}^{\prime},\dots,g_{n^{\prime}}^{\prime}
satisfying the conditions in Construction
5.19
, and which correspond to
(z_{1},m-n,y),(z_{2},m^{\prime}-n^{\prime},y)\in{\pazocal G}(Y,\rho)y
respectively. We have
m-n=m^{\prime}-n^{\prime}
and we may assume that
m^{\prime}\geq m
, so that setting
m^{\prime}=m+k
we have
k\geq 0
and
n^{\prime}=n+k
. We emphasize that the conditions in Construction
5.19
include in particular that
f_{m}\neq g_{n}
and that
f^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}}\neq g^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}
.

Setting
\xi=\psi(y)
, we have

\theta_{f_{1}}^{-1}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{f_{m}}^{-1}\circ\theta_{g_{n}}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{g_{1}}(\xi)=\theta_{f_{1}^{\prime}}^{-1}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{f_{m^{\prime}}^{\prime}}^{-1}\circ\theta_{g_{n^{\prime}}^{\prime}}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{g_{1}^{\prime}}(\xi).

Hence
\xi\in\text{Dom}(\theta_{g_{1}})\cap\text{Dom}(\theta_{g^{\prime}_{1}})=H_{g_{1}}\cap H_{g_{1}^{\prime}}
and so
g_{1}=g_{1}^{\prime}
. Now we get

\theta_{f_{1}}^{-1}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{f_{m}}^{-1}\circ\theta_{g_{n}}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{g_{2}}(\xi_{1})=\theta_{f_{1}^{\prime}}^{-1}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{f_{m^{\prime}}^{\prime}}^{-1}\circ\theta_{g_{n^{\prime}}^{\prime}}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{g_{2}^{\prime}}(\xi_{1}),

where
\xi_{1}=\theta_{g_{1}}(\xi)=\sigma(\xi)
. The same argument gives that
g_{2}=g_{2}^{\prime}
. Proceeding in this way, we obtain that
g_{i}=g_{i}^{\prime}
for
1\leq i\leq n
. Set
\xi_{n}=\theta_{g_{n}}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{g_{1}}(\xi)
. Then we have

\xi^{\prime}:=\theta_{f_{1}}^{-1}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{f_{m}}^{-1}(\xi_{n})=\theta_{f_{1}^{\prime}}^{-1}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{f^{\prime}_{m}}^{-1}\circ\theta_{f^{\prime}_{m+1}}^{-1}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{f_{m+k}^{\prime}}^{-1}\circ\theta_{g_{n+k}^{\prime}}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{g_{n+1}^{\prime}}(\xi_{n}).

Hence
\xi^{\prime}\in H_{f_{1}}\cap H_{f_{1}^{\prime}}
, and thus
f_{1}=f_{1}^{\prime}
. Applying the same argument to
\theta_{f_{1}}(\xi^{\prime})
, we get that
f_{2}=f_{2}^{\prime}
, and continuing in this way, that
f_{i}=f_{i}^{\prime}
for
1\leq i\leq m
. Therefore, we get

\xi_{n}=\theta_{f_{m}}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{f_{1}}(\xi^{\prime})=\theta_{f^{\prime}_{m+1}}^{-1}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{f_{m+k}^{\prime}}^{-1}\circ\theta_{g_{n+k}^{\prime}}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{g_{n+1}^{\prime}}(\xi_{n}).

If
k>0
, then we have

\theta_{f_{m+k}^{\prime}}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{f_{m+1}^{\prime}}(\xi_{n})=\theta_{g_{n+k}^{\prime}}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{g_{n+1}^{\prime}}(\xi_{n}),

and proceeding as above we get
g_{n+i}^{\prime}=f_{n+i}^{\prime}
for
1\leq i\leq k
. In particular we have
f^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}}=f_{m+k}^{\prime}=g_{n+k}^{\prime}=g^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}
, which is a contradiction with the condition
f^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}}\neq g^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}
. Therefore
k=0
, and thus we have
m^{\prime}=m
,
n^{\prime}=n
,
f_{i}=f_{i}^{\prime}
for
1\leq i\leq m
, and
g_{j}=g_{j}^{\prime}
for
1\leq j\leq n
. Hence we obtain that
(z_{1},m-n,y)=(z_{2},m^{\prime}-n^{\prime},y)
, as desired.
∎

Theorem 6.21
.

Let
(Y,\rho)\in\emph{\textsf{LHomeo}}
and let
(E,C)
be a generalized finite shift graph. Suppose that we have an
(E,C)
-structure on
Y
, and let
\psi\colon Y\to X
be the unique
(E,C)
-equivariant continuous map, where
(X,\sigma)
is the generalized finite shift associated to
(E,C)
. Then the associated groupoid homomorphism

\psi_{*}\colon{\pazocal G}(Y,\rho)\to{\pazocal G}(X,\sigma)

given in Lemma
6.18
induces a
K
-algebra
*
-homomorphism

\psi^{*}\colon A_{K}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma))\to A_{K}({\pazocal G}(Y,\rho))

by the rule
\psi^{*}(f)=f\circ\psi_{*}
for
f\in A_{K}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma))
.

Proof.

We first show that
\psi_{*}
is proper. For this it is enough to shown that
\psi_{*}^{-1}(Z(U,m,n,V))
is compact, where
U
and
V
are open compact subsets of
X
such that
\sigma^{m}|_{U}
and
\sigma^{n}|_{V}
are injective, and
\sigma^{m}(U)=\sigma^{n}(V)
.

For such open compact subset
Z(U,m,n,V)
of
{\pazocal G}(X,\sigma)
, we claim that the set
Z(\psi^{-1}(U),m,n,\psi^{-1}(V))
is a bisection homeomorphic to
\psi^{-1}(V)
, and thus a compact set. For this, we show that the source map
s
induces a bijection

Z(\psi^{-1}(U),m,n,\psi^{-1}(V))\to\psi^{-1}(V).

Let
y\in\psi^{-1}(V)
. Then
\psi(y)\in V
and since
\sigma^{m}(U)=\sigma^{n}(V)
, there is
x\in U
such that
\sigma^{m}(x)=\sigma^{n}(\psi(y))
. Hence
(x,m-n,\psi(y))\in{\pazocal G}(X,\sigma)\psi(y)
. Now by the proof of surjectivity in Lemma
6.20
, there is
z\in Y
such that
\psi_{*}(z,m-n,y)=(x,m-n,\psi(y))
. But then
(z,m-n,y)\in Z(\psi^{-1}(U),m,n,\psi^{-1}(V))
and
s(z,m-n,y)=y
, showing surjectivity.

To show injectivity, suppose that
(z_{1},m-n,y),(z_{2},m-n,y)
are two elements in
Z(\psi^{-1}(U),m,n,\psi^{-1}(V))
. Then,
(\psi(z_{1}),m-n,\psi(y)),(\psi(z_{2}),m-n,\psi(y))\in Z(U,m,n,V)
, and thus
\sigma^{m}(\psi(z_{1}))=\sigma^{n}(\psi(y))=\sigma^{m}(\psi(z_{2}))
. But
\sigma^{m}|_{U}
is injective, so
\psi(z_{1})=\psi(z_{2})
. By Lemma
6.20
, we conclude that
z_{1}=z_{2}
.

We have completed the proof that
\psi_{*}
is proper. Hence, since it is also continuous by Lemma
6.18
, we obtain that
\psi^{*}
is a well-defined linear map. We have to show that
\psi^{*}
preserves the convolution product. For
f,g\in A_{K}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma))
and an element
\gamma=(z,m-n,y)\in{\pazocal G}(Y,\rho)
, we have

\displaystyle(\psi^{*}(f)*\psi^{*}(g))(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\in{\pazocal G}(Y,\rho)y}\psi^{*}(f)(\gamma\alpha^{-1})\psi^{*}(g)(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\in{\pazocal G}(Y,\rho)y}f(\psi_{*}(\gamma)\psi_{*}(\alpha)^{-1})g(\psi_{*}(\alpha))

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta\in{\pazocal G}(X,\sigma)\psi(y)}f(\psi_{*}(\gamma)\beta^{-1})g(\beta)=\psi^{*}(f*g)(\gamma),

where we have used Lemma
6.20
in the third equality. We thus have
\psi^{*}(f)*\psi^{*}(g)=\psi^{*}(f*g)
. It is straightforward to check that
\psi^{*}
is a
*
-map.
∎

We also have a similar result for groupoid
C^{*}
-algebras.

Theorem 6.22
.

Let
(Y,\rho)\in\emph{\textsf{LHomeo}}
and
(X,\sigma)
and
\psi
be as in Theorem
6.21
. Then the groupoid homomorphism
\psi_{*}\colon{\pazocal G}(Y,\rho)\to{\pazocal G}(X,\sigma)
induces a
*
-homomorphism

\psi^{*}\colon C^{*}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma))\to C^{*}({\pazocal G}(Y,\rho))

such that
\psi^{*}(f)=f\circ\psi_{*}
for
f\in C_{c}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma))
.

Proof.

As in the proof of Theorem
6.21
, we see that
\psi^{*}
gives a
*
-homomorphism
C_{c}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma))\to C_{c}({\pazocal G}(Y,\rho))
. Since
C^{*}({\pazocal G})
is the enveloping
C^{*}
-algebra of
C_{c}({\pazocal G})
for any second-countable locally compact Hausdorff étale groupoid
{\pazocal G}
(see
[
55
, Theorem 9.2.3 and Lemma 9.2.4]
), we conclude the result. Alternatively, we may also use that
C^{*}({\pazocal G})
is the enveloping
C^{*}
-algebra of
A_{{\mathbb{C}}}({\pazocal G})
, and use Theorem
6.21
.
∎

6.3.
Algebras as colimits of generalized finite shift algebras

We now consider the situation treated at the end of Section
5
, so that
(X,\sigma)\in\textsf{LHomeo}
,
(F,D)
is a fixed
l
-diagram for
(X,\sigma)
, and we set
(E(n),C(n))=(F_{2n},D^{2n})
for all
n\geq 0
. Recall that
(E(n),C(n))
is a generalized finite shift graph, with associated generalized finite shift
(X_{n},\sigma_{n})
. We denote by
(F(n),D(n))
the canonical resolution of
(E(n),C(n))
, which is an
l
-diagram for
(X_{n},\sigma_{n})
. By Theorem
5.26
, we have
(X,\sigma)=\varprojlim(X_{n},\sigma_{n})
, and the inverse limit maps
\psi_{n}\colon X\to X_{n}
and the connecting maps
\psi_{n,n+1}\colon X_{n+1}\to X_{n}
are universal maps induced by the corresponding
(E(n),C(n))
-structures. It therefore follows from Theorems
6.21
and
6.22
that we have directed systems

(A_{K}({\pazocal G}(X_{n},\sigma_{n})),\psi_{n,n+1}^{*})\quad\text{ and }\quad(C^{*}({\pazocal G}(X_{n},\sigma_{n})),\psi_{n,n+1}^{*})

together with compatible
*
-homomorphisms

A_{K}({\pazocal G}(X_{n},\sigma_{n}))\overset{\psi_{n}^{*}}{\longrightarrow}A_{K}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma))\quad\text{ and }\quad C^{*}({\pazocal G}(X_{n},\sigma_{n}))\overset{\psi_{n}^{*}}{\longrightarrow}C^{*}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma)).

We show in this section that indeed
(A_{K}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma)),\psi_{n}^{*})
and
(C^{*}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma)),\psi_{n}^{*})
are the colimits of the above directed systems in the categories of
*
-algebras and
C^{*}
-algebras, respectively.

We introduce some useful notation. Recall the definitions of the
*
-algebra
{\pazocal A}_{\infty}
and the
C^{*}
-algebra
{\pazocal B}_{\infty}
associated to the
l
-diagram
(F,D)
from Subsection
6.1
.

Given a generalized finite shift graph
(E,C)
, we identify
LV_{K}(E,C)
with the corner
VL_{K}(E,C)V
, where
V=\sum_{v\in E^{0,0}}v\in L_{K}(E,C)
, see Proposition
6.10
. Thinking of
V
as a projection in
C^{*}(E,C)
, we set
C^{*}V(E,C):=VC^{*}(E,C)V
.

Notation 6.23
.

Let
(E,C)
be a generalized finite shift graph. We denote by
LV^{{\rm ab}}_{K}(E,C)
the universal tame
*
-algebra associated to the canonical generating set of partial isometries of
LV_{K}(E,C)
. Similarly
{\pazocal O}V(E,C)
will denote the universal tame
C^{*}
-algebra associated to
C^{*}V(E,C)
. By using
[
5
, Lemma 4.3]
, we will identify
LV^{{\rm ab}}_{K}(E,C)
with the algebra
{\pazocal A}_{\infty}
of the canonical resolution of
(E,C)
. Moreover, the arguments in
[
5
, Lemma 4.3]
apply
verbatim
to the
C^{*}
-algebra counterparts, therefore we will also identify
{\pazocal O}V(E,C)
with the
{\pazocal B}_{\infty}

C^{*}
-algebra associated to the canonical resolution of
(E,C)
.

Applying Theorem
6.16
to
(F(n),D(n))
we obtain a canonical
*
-isomorphism

\varphi^{(n)}\colon LV^{{\rm ab}}_{K}(E(n),C(n))={\pazocal A}_{\infty}^{(n)}\longrightarrow A_{K}({\pazocal G}(X_{n},\sigma_{n})),

where
LV^{{\rm ab}}_{K}(E(n),C(n))={\pazocal A}_{\infty}^{(n)}=\varinjlim_{i}p_{2i}^{(n)}L_{K}(F(n)_{2i},D(n)^{2i})p_{2i}^{(n)}
is the
{\pazocal A}_{\infty}
algebra corresponding to
(F(n),D(n))
(see Notation
6.23
). In particular, since the first layer of
(F(n),D(n))
is
(E(n),C(n))
, we have the
*
-homomorphism

\varphi^{(n)}_{0}\colon LV_{K}(E(n),C(n))\to A_{K}({\pazocal G}(X_{n},\sigma_{n}))

which satisfies

(6.8)

\varphi^{(n)}_{0}=\varphi^{(n)}\circ\pi_{n},

where
\pi_{n}\colon LV_{K}(E(n),C(n))\to LV^{{\rm ab}}_{K}(E(n),C(n))
is the canonical projection map.

In a completely analogous fashion, using Theorem
6.17
, we get
*
-isomorphisms

\overline{\varphi}^{(n)}\colon{\pazocal O}V(E(n),C(n))={\pazocal B}_{\infty}^{(n)}\longrightarrow C^{*}({\pazocal G}(X_{n},\sigma_{n}))

satisfying corresponding properties.

We can now obtain the following result, which links the two developments in Subsections
6.1
and
6.2
.

Theorem 6.24
.

Let
(X,\sigma)\in\emph{\textsf{LHomeo}}
and let
(F,D)
be an
l
-diagram for
(X,\sigma)
. With the above notation, for each
n\geq 0
there exists a unique
*
-homomorphism
\phi_{n}^{\mathrm{ab}}\colon LV^{{\rm ab}}_{K}(E(n),C(n))\to LV^{{\rm ab}}_{K}(E(n+1),C(n+1))
such that the following diagram

(6.9)

\textstyle{LV_{K}(E(n),C(n))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{n}}
\textstyle{LV^{{\rm ab}}_{K}(E(n),C(n))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{n}^{\rm ab}}
\scriptstyle{\kern 5.0pt{\varphi^{(n)}}}
\scriptstyle{\kern 5.0pt{\cong}}
\textstyle{A_{K}({\pazocal G}(X_{n},\sigma_{n}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{n,n+1}^{*}}
\textstyle{LV_{K}(E(n+1),C(n+1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{n+1}}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{n+1,\infty}}
\textstyle{LV^{{\rm ab}}_{K}(E(n+1),C(n+1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kern 10.0pt{\varphi^{(n+1)}}}
\scriptstyle{\kern 10.0pt{\cong}}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{n+1,\infty}^{{\rm ab}}}
\textstyle{A_{K}({\pazocal G}(X_{n+1},\sigma_{n+1}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{n+1}^{*}}
\textstyle{{\pazocal A}_{\infty}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm id}}
\textstyle{{\pazocal A}_{\infty}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kern-15.0pt{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\kern-15.0pt{\cong}}
\textstyle{A_{K}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma))}

is commutative, where
\pi_{n}\colon LV_{K}(E(n),C(n))\to LV^{{\rm ab}}_{K}(E(n),C(n))
are the canonical quotient maps. Hence we get
*
-isomorphisms

(6.10)

A_{K}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma))\cong{\pazocal A}_{\infty}\cong\varinjlim LV^{{\rm ab}}_{K}(E(n),C(n))\quad\text{ and }\quad A_{K}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma))\cong\varinjlim A_{K}({\pazocal G}(X_{n},\sigma_{n})).

Similarly, we have
*
-isomorphisms

(6.11)

C^{*}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma))\cong{\pazocal B}_{\infty}\cong\varinjlim{{\pazocal O}V}(E(n),C(n))\quad\text{ and }\quad C^{*}({\pazocal G}(X,\sigma))\cong\varinjlim C^{*}({\pazocal G}(X_{n},\sigma_{n})).

Proof.

Since
\varphi^{(n)}
and
\varphi^{(n+1)}
are isomorphisms, there is a unique
*
-homomorphism

\phi_{n}^{{\rm ab}}\colon LV^{{\rm ab}}_{K}(E(n),C(n))\to LV^{{\rm ab}}_{K}(E(n+1),C(n+1))

making the upper right square in (
6.9
) commutative.

By (
6.8
), in order to show that the upper left square in (
6.9
) is commutative, it suffices to show the identity
\varphi_{0}^{(n+1)}\circ\phi_{n}=\psi_{n,n+1}^{*}\circ\varphi_{0}^{(n)}
. To do so, we have to fix some notation.

For
u\in E(n)^{0}
, we will denote by
Z^{(n)}(u)
the corresponding cylinder set in
X_{n}
. Let
(G,D)
be the
l
-diagram obtained by placing the two layers
(F_{2n},D^{2n})=(E(n),C(n))
and
(F_{2n+1},D^{2n+1})
on top of
(F(n+1),D(n+1))
, and denote by
{\pazocal P}_{i}^{G}
,
i\geq 0
, the corresponding partitions of
X_{n+1}
. Note that the first three layers of
(G,D)
are identical to
(F_{2n},D^{2n})\cup(F_{2n+1},D^{2n+1})\cup(F_{2n+2},D^{2n+2})
, so in particular we have a natural bijection

\gamma\colon E(n)^{0,0}\cup E(n)^{0,1}\cup E(n+1)^{0,0}\cup E(n+1)^{0,1}\longrightarrow{\pazocal P}^{G}_{0}\cup{\pazocal P}^{G}_{1}\cup{\pazocal P}^{G}_{2}\cup{\pazocal P}^{G}_{3}.

Note that
\gamma(u)=Z^{(n+1)}(u)
for
u\in E(n+1)^{0}
. Using the definition of
\psi_{n,n+1}
, we get, for
u\in E(n)^{0,0}
, that
\psi_{n,n+1}^{-1}(Z^{(n)}(u))=\gamma(u)
, so setting
U:=\gamma(u)
, we have

\psi_{n,n+1}^{-1}(Z^{(n)}(u))=U=\bigsqcup_{\{U^{\prime}\in{\pazocal P}^{G}_{2}\mid U^{\prime}\subseteq U\}}U^{\prime}.

Similarly, for
w\in E(n)^{0,1}
, we have
\psi_{n+1,n}^{-1}(Z^{(n)}(w))=\bigsqcup_{W^{\prime}}W^{\prime}
where the union is extended to all
W^{\prime}\in{\pazocal P}^{G}_{3}
such that
W^{\prime}\subseteq W
, where
W:=\gamma(w)\in{\pazocal P}^{G}_{1}
. It follows that, for
u\in E(n)^{0,0}
, and with
U:=\gamma(u)
, we have

\displaystyle\psi_{n,n+1}^{*}(\varphi_{0}^{(n)}(u))

\displaystyle=\psi_{n,n+1}^{*}(\mathds{1}_{Z^{(n)}(u)})

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}_{Z^{(n)}(u)}\circ\psi_{n,n+1}

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}_{\psi_{n,n+1}^{-1}(Z^{(n)}(u))}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\{U^{\prime}\in{\pazocal P}^{G}_{2}\mid U^{\prime}\subseteq U\}}\mathds{1}_{U^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\varphi_{0}^{(n+1)}(\mathbf{S}(u))=\varphi_{0}^{(n+1)}(\phi_{n}(u)).

Similarly
\psi_{n,n+1}^{*}(\varphi_{0}^{(n)}(w))=\varphi_{0}^{(n+1)}(\phi_{n}(w))
for all
w\in E(n)^{0,1}
.

Let now
f\in R_{u}
,
e\in B_{v}
, where
e,f\in E(n)^{1}
,
u,v\in E(n)^{0,0}
, and
s(e)=s(f)=:w\in E(n)^{0,1}
. Using Lemma
6.1
and the definition of
\phi_{n}
, we have

\phi_{n}(fe^{*})=\sum_{(f^{\prime},e^{\prime})\in A}f^{\prime}(e^{\prime})^{*},

where
A
is the set of all pairs
(f^{\prime},e^{\prime})\in E(n+1)^{1}\times E(n+1)^{1}
such that
f^{\prime}\in R_{u^{\prime}}
,
e^{\prime}\in B_{v^{\prime}}
,
u^{\prime}=r(f^{\prime})\leq\mathbf{S}(u)
,
v^{\prime}=r(e^{\prime})\leq\mathbf{S}(v)
and
w^{\prime}:=s(f^{\prime})=s(e^{\prime})\leq\mathbf{S}(w)
. Each pair
(f^{\prime},e^{\prime})\in A
corresponds exactly to a triple
(U^{\prime},W^{\prime},V^{\prime})
such that
U^{\prime},V^{\prime}\in{\pazocal P}^{G}_{2}
and
W^{\prime}\in{\pazocal P}^{G}_{3}
, with
W^{\prime}\subseteq\sigma_{n+1}(U^{\prime})
and
W^{\prime}\subseteq V^{\prime}
, with
U^{\prime}=\gamma(u^{\prime})\subseteq U=\gamma(u)
,
V^{\prime}=\gamma(v^{\prime})\subseteq V:=\gamma(v)
and
W^{\prime}=\gamma(w^{\prime})\subseteq W=\gamma(w)
. Moreover, observe that

\varphi^{(n+1)}_{0}(f^{\prime}(e^{\prime})^{*})=\mathds{1}_{Z((\sigma|_{U^{\prime}})^{-1}(W^{\prime}),1,0,W^{\prime})}\in A_{K}({\pazocal G}(X_{n+1},\sigma_{n+1})).

We denote by
B
the family of all such triples
(U^{\prime},W^{\prime},V^{\prime})
. Using this, we compute

\displaystyle\psi_{n,n+1}^{*}(\varphi_{0}^{(n)}(fe^{*}))

\displaystyle=\psi_{n,n+1}^{*}(\mathds{1}_{Z((\sigma_{n}|_{Z^{(n)}(u)})^{-1}(Z^{(n)}(w)),1,0,Z^{(n)}(w))})

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}_{Z((\sigma_{n}|_{Z^{(n)}(u)})^{-1}(Z^{(n)}(w)),1,0,Z^{(n)}(w))}\circ(\psi_{n,n+1})_{*}

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}_{({\psi_{n+1,n}}_{*})^{-1}Z((\sigma_{n}|_{Z^{(n)}(u)})^{-1}(Z^{(n)}(w)),1,0,Z^{(n)}(w))}

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}_{(Z((\sigma_{n+1}|_{U})^{-1}(W)),1,0,W)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(U^{\prime},W^{\prime},V^{\prime})\in B}\mathds{1}_{Z((\sigma_{n+1}|_{U^{\prime}})^{-1}(W^{\prime}),1,0,W^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(f^{\prime},e^{\prime})\in A}\varphi_{0}^{(n+1)}(f^{\prime}(e^{\prime})^{*})=\varphi_{0}^{(n+1)}(\phi_{n}(fe^{*})).

Since the family of partial isometries
fe^{*}
, with
f\in R_{u}
,
e\in B_{v}
,
u,v\in E(n)^{0,0}
, generates
LV_{K}(E(n),C(n))
as a
*
-algebra, we get that
\psi_{n,n+1}^{*}\circ\varphi_{0}^{(n)}=\varphi_{0}^{(n+1)}\circ\phi_{n}
, and hence
\phi_{n}^{\text{ab}}\circ\pi_{n}=\pi_{n+1}\circ\phi_{n}
, so that the upper left square of (
6.9
) is commutative.

Using that
\varphi
is an isomorphism, we see that there is a unique
*
-homomorphism

\phi_{n+1,\infty}^{\text{ab}}\colon LV^{{\rm ab}}_{K}(E(n+1),C(n+1))\longrightarrow{\pazocal A}_{\infty}

making the lower right square of (
6.9
) commutative. Now a computation similar to the above gives that

\psi_{n+1}^{*}\circ\varphi_{0}^{(n+1)}=\varphi_{n+1}=\varphi\circ\phi_{n+1,\infty},

and so the lower left square of (
6.9
) is also commutative.

Now recall that, by its definition,
({\pazocal A}_{\infty},\phi_{n,\infty})
is the colimit of the directed system

(LV_{K}(E(n),C(n)),\phi_{n}).

Since all the maps
\pi_{n}
are surjective, the commutativity of (
6.9
) gives first that the maps
\phi_{n,\infty}^{{\rm ab}}
are compatible with the transition maps
\phi_{n}^{{\rm ab}}
, that is,
\phi_{n,\infty}^{{\rm ab}}=\phi_{n+1,\infty}^{{\rm ab}}\circ\phi_{n}^{{\rm ab}}
, and second that
({\pazocal A}_{\infty},\phi_{n,\infty}^{{\rm ab}})
is the colimit of the directed system

(LV^{{\rm ab}}_{K}(E(n),C(n)),\phi_{n}^{{\rm ab}}).

This gives the isomorphism
{\pazocal A}_{\infty}\cong\varinjlim LV_{K}^{\text{ab}}(E(n),C(n))
. Using the commutativity of the right squares of (
6.9
) and the fact that
\varphi
and all
\varphi^{(n)}
are isomorphisms, we obtain (
6.10
).

The proof of (
6.11
) uses a diagram of
C^{*}
-algebras obtained by taking
C^{*}
-completions in diagram (
6.9
). We leave the routine details to the reader. This concludes the proof.
∎
